Fast, reliable A2UI, with swappable inference formats

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

We've heard your concerns about the speed of A2UI and generative UI experiences: people wait while the model generates each screen. This release gives you levers to make that faster, so you can choose the trade-off that fits each screen.

With this release, we’re happy to introduce swappable inference formats and share examples on leveraging decision models with A2UI, which help building faster and more reliable generative UI experiences. Using our new A2UI Express as an inference format you can reduce latency by up to 3x, and up-to 80x using decision models like Jev to make layout decisions. Express requires up to 3x fewer tokens to generate the same kinds of UI compared to Direct JSON. We'll dive into the details in this blog post.

A2UI is an agent-optimized protocol for generative UI, where a client advertises a catalog of trusted components as a contract, an agent sends A2UI messages describing a bespoke user interface composed from that catalog, and the client's renderer draws the UI (see Introducing A2UI).

Initially agents generated A2UI JSON transport messages directly, every curly brace and nested element. It turns out that generating JSON trees constrained by a complex JSON schema is slow and error-prone for LLMs. This coupled A2UI to LLMs, which output text, and made it difficult to integrate faster System One decision models.

Now agents can generate A2UI Express, an intermediate format optimized for inference speed and accuracy, and rely on deterministic code in A2UI's Agent SDKs to translate it into the JSON-based transport protocol. Agent builders are also free to swap in any other DSL and its associated compiler for any special use-cases, and our A2UI Agent SDK(s) make it compatible with transports and renders.

The format the model generates is what we call inference_format. You set it with one argument when you create the generator. The SDK generates the prompt instructions for that format and compiles the model's reply into standard A2UI messages. This post covers how the swap works, why we developed A2UI Express as the new default inference format, and how a decision model can build screens from layouts you define in advance.

An inference format has two parts, and the SDK generates both from your catalog's JSON schema. A prompt section teaches the model the format's syntax, and a compiler compiles the model's reply into validated A2UI messages. Because both come from the schema, a format can't describe a component or property that your catalog lacks.

You choose the format with one argument when you create the generator:

A2UI Express is available in the Python agent SDK (a2ui-agent-sdk 0.9.0 on PyPI), in the TypeScript agent SDK (@a2ui/agent on npm), and in the Dart SDK (a2ui_agent 0.0.1-wip004 on pub.dev). In TypeScript, you pass new ExpressFormatFactory() as the generator's third argument. The Python and TypeScript SDKs can both stream Direct JSON chunk by chunk, and they compile Express from the complete reply.

Express writes one statement per line, in a style close to Python. Each line assigns a component to a variable, and the variable name becomes the component ID. Arguments are positional: the compiler maps them to property names in the order your catalog schema declares them, and _ skips one. $/title binds a value to the data model, and Event("accept") declares a server action. This is the A2UI gallery's notification card in Express:

Swappable Inference formats including A2UI Express also work with macros in the Python agent SDK, which help when a screen shows data your systems already hold. The model writes a call with only an ID, such as OrderStatus("ord_42"), and your macro fetches the order and fills in the values, so every value on screen comes straight from your data. The macros README shows the setup.

Customer feedback uncovered latency and accuracy issues with direct JSON generation. To fix this, we started devising a new inference format. We experimented with four options:

We ran all four formats through the same 51-task eval on gemini-3.5-flash and compared them for thinking modes: on and off.

A2UI Express follows python like syntax and was inspired in part by OpenUI Lang, the language of the open-source OpenUI project, which also writes each component as a name = Component(...) statement with positional arguments. OpenUI Lang adds its own built-in functions and state, and the two languages aren’t yet interchangeable. The OpenUI team has proposed working toward a shared syntax in #3214. Comment if you're interested.

Based on our tests; we observed that Express inference format works just as well even with thinking off; which can give you the fastest inference times when working with full expressivity of A2UI.

Some screens have a known shape, such as a settings page, a checkout or a confirmation. For those, the model doesn't need to design a layout. It needs to choose among layouts you have already defined, and a different kind of model is built for that. For this, you would not need the full expressivity provided by A2UI Express.

Jev, from TypeSafe, is what is called a System One model, built to make fast, structured decisions that code can use directly. Jev doesn't generate text or JSON. You send it a state and a set of typed questions, and it answers each one with calibrated probabilities. A question can ask whether a statement is true (Noul), which option from a list fits (Choice), or how something scores on a rubric (Score). Jev answers many questions in one request, in roughly 100 to 300 ms.

Apparite(jev2ui), an open-source design tool, shows one way to connect a System One model to A2UI. Jev decides the structure of each screen, code builds the A2UI components from those decisions, and a small model, gemini-3.5-flash-lite, writes only the text. The questions live in a Markdown grammar written ahead of time. A blockquote asks a question, and the bullets are its options, each with criteria. This block from grammar/screen.md decides which control a settings row gets:

On a podcast app's settings screen, Jev answers Skip forward → value, Auto-download → switch and Log out → danger, each at p ≈ 1.00. Apparite builds a whole screen in three steps:

In one run of 11 prompts, the component tree was on screen in 92 to 298 ms and each screen was complete in 0.8 to 1.7 s. The text writer generated 101 to 341 tokens per screen, and both of Apparite's Jev pipelines produced valid A2UI on all 11 prompts. The project treats these numbers as a first look, since the sample is small.

Controlled generation can only produce the layouts its grammar describes. Apparite's grammar covers nine screen archetypes and twelve blocks, so screens without a known shape are a better fit for Express. A2UI Agent SDKs, don’t include a decision model inference format yet; apparite(jev2ui) project is an example on how A2UI messages could be assembled given a batch of independent decisions from a decision model.

A2UI Express is fast and keeps the full expressivity of A2UI, but a decision model making multiple independent decisions is still faster. Choose by how much of the screen you can define ahead of time:

Use Express in your agent. Install the Python SDK with pip install a2ui-agent-sdk or the TypeScript SDK with npm install @a2ui/agent, pass ExpressFormatFactory() when you create the generator, and run the model with thinking off. Your catalog and renderers stay as they are, and the Express specification documents the syntax.

To teach Gemini, or any other model that loads skills, to write Express with only your components, generate skills from your catalog with the Python SDK's SkillGenerator:

Try controlled generation. Clone the example jev2ui repository, and follow the instructions there to get started. A hosted version of Apparite(jev2ui) runs at apparite.cnfg.ai.

Earlier A2UI posts: Introducing A2UI (December 2025), A2UI v0.9 (April 2026) and A2UI + MCP Apps (June 2026).

Hill-Climbing Agent Quality with Agents CLI Evaluation

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A live walkthrough of the Agents CLI eval loop, from a first run to regression tracking, model-backend experiments, and automated alerting

Building an agent that works on a demo is a different problem from building an agent that stays working across model upgrades, tool changes, and new conversation shapes. The first takes a weekend; the second takes a loop. A team writes test cases, runs them, grades them, reads what failed, changes the agent, and runs the cases again. If the score goes up, the change was good. If it goes down, revert. Over weeks of this, the agent inches toward the ceiling of what the model, tools, and prompt can do together. The quality-engineering name for this is hill-climbing.

The Agents CLI eval namespace was built around this loop. The commands are small and framework-agnostic: eval generate runs the agent against a dataset of cases, eval grade scores the generated traces, eval compare diffs two run results side by side, eval analyze clusters failures into themes, and eval optimize runs an automated hill-climbing pass on the agent's prompt.

Below: baseline → regression → model swap → scheduling and alerting.

The scaffold used throughout is the LangChain template that ships with Agents CLI v1.8.0. The setup is a single command:

The scaffold ships with an eval dataset at tests/eval/datasets/basic-dataset.json, a config at tests/eval/eval_config.yaml, and a custom LLM-as-judge metric at tests/eval/response_quality.py. The config declares the metric to run:

The metric grades each response on a 1-to-5 scale for accuracy, relevance, and clarity, using gemini-3.8-flash as the judge. The default dataset has a single trivial case, which is not enough for a hill-climb. The first thing we do is expand it to four cases that probe different behaviors:

Four cases: a greeting, a weather question the mock tool handles with the hardcoded foggy response, a weather question for a city the tool defaults on, and an off-topic question that the agent has to decide how to handle. This is a small dataset by design; the hill-climbing loop pays off when the dataset is small enough to run on every change.

With the dataset in place, we run eval generate to drive the agent against each case, then eval grade to score the resulting traces:

Mean 4.0 out of 5 across four cases, with a standard deviation of 1.41. That stdev tells us immediately that the cases are not scoring uniformly, which is a healthier starting point than a flat 5.0 across the board. A flat 5.0 either means the agent is perfect (unlikely) or the dataset is too easy to show variation. 4.0 with real spread is a hill to climb.

To demonstrate the loop, we introduce a realistic regression. A developer working across several agent projects accidentally pastes the wrong system prompt into our weather agent: a chef persona meant for a different codebase. In app/agent.py:

The change compiles, the agent still runs, and nothing in the pre-eval pipeline (lint, unit tests, type checks) catches it. Re-running the same eval generate plus eval grade sequence produces:

Mean 2.75, down from 4.0. The judge agrees with our intuition that a chef persona on a weather agent is a worse product than a general-purpose helpful assistant. The stdev also tightened from 1.41 to 0.96, consistent with the chef prompt pushing most cases toward the same mediocre range.

A 1.25-point drop on a 1-to-5 scale is substantial, and the kind of regression that can slip through code review when the diff looks small ("one string literal changed"). The loop surfaced the change at all, with a specific number attached to it, before the agent reached production.

Mean scores are a start; the per-case diff is where the hill-climb happens. eval compare reads two result files and produces a structured JSON diff:

The output lists which keys changed and which stayed the same:

The differences object has the full per-case breakdown with the baseline and candidate values side by side. For a hill-climbing workflow, we typically pipe the output through jq or a small Python script to extract the signal; the raw JSON is the complete evidence.

Walking the two result files and lining up the scores per case shows where the regression landed:

Three cases dropped, one held. The most important of the three is sf_weather: the chef persona pulled the model's attention away from the registered get_weather tool, so the agent stopped calling it and started making up weather values. Unit tests can't catch this: the code runs, the tool is still registered, but the model's behavior around it has shifted. Only an end-to-end eval that scores the response in context surfaces this.

The mean drop by itself (−1.25) is a clear signal, but the per-case breakdown is what tells the developer where to look. A one-line system-prompt change propagated through three different cases in three different ways: greeting lost a point on tone, sf_weather lost two on tool usage, off_topic lost two on scope creep.

A common hill-climbing question is whether a cheaper or faster model would do the job acceptably. For the LangChain template, the model is defined in app/agent.py:

We swap gemini-3.8-flash for gemini-2.5-flash (restoring the helpful system prompt first), re-run eval generate plus eval grade, and compare the two result files. The mean drops from 4.00 to 2.75, the same magnitude as the chef-prompt regression, but the per-case breakdown tells a completely different story:

The cheaper model matched 3.8-flash exactly on the greeting and rome_weather cases and lost a point on sf_weather (slightly less polished phrasing). The whole visible drop traces to off_topic, where the cheaper model refused with a hedge about the event "not having happened yet" (a training-cutoff artifact), and the judge marked it harshly.

The lesson is that the summary number is misleading. Look only at the mean and you'd call gemini-2.5-flash as bad as the chef prompt. Look at the per-case breakdown and you'd call it a reasonable swap for most of the agent's workload, with one case (handling out-of-scope questions about recent events) exposing a real limitation. If the production distribution of user questions matches the first three cases more than the fourth, the swap is worth considering. If off_topic-style questions are common, it is not.

Two caveats on this experiment. First, model swaps interact with the prompt: a prompt tuned for a stronger model often leaks capability when ported to a weaker one, so the right comparison is "weaker model with a prompt tuned for it" versus "stronger model with its own tuned prompt." Second, the Vertex AI Eval Service calls a judge model to grade the traces, and the judge is held constant across the comparison. Changing the judge is a different experiment and should be done separately.

Agents CLI does not ship a scheduler, but the eval commands are designed to drop into standard infrastructure. The pattern we can use here is a cron job or Cloud Scheduler trigger that runs eval generate plus eval grade on a cadence and writes the results to a persistent location:

A nightly run against a stable regression dataset catches drift from model updates, data changes in the agent's tools, and dependencies that shift under the agent's feet. For a production agent, the scheduled run also captures the model vendor's quiet upgrades, which can move the agent's behavior without any change on our side. The dataset for the scheduled run is typically larger than the one used in the inner loop (dozens to hundreds of cases rather than a few), since it only runs once a day.

The scheduled run produces a result file every day, but a file nobody looks at is not an alert. The pattern here can be a wrapper script that compares each new run's mean against a baseline (the previous run, or a committed known-good) and exits non-zero when the drop exceeds a threshold. The non-zero exit is what triggers the alert from the surrounding scheduler.

Running it against our regression demo (baseline 4.0, candidate 2.75 from the chef-prompt run):

And against an identical pair (self-compare):

The non-zero exit code is what a cron job, Cloud Scheduler, or GitHub Actions step picks up to send the alert.

For ADK projects, eval optimize runs an automated hill-climbing pass on the agent's prompt using the GEPA framework (not currently supported for the LangChain extension). The command takes a dataset and a target metric, proposes prompt variants, grades each one, keeps the variants that improve the metric, and iterates. The output is a set of candidate prompts ranked by their eval score, which a human then reviews and promotes to the agent definition.

Two things to understand about eval optimize. First, it is scoped to prompt optimization, not to arbitrary agent changes: the agent's tools, structure, and model stay fixed. Second, GEPA sometimes finds genuine improvements and sometimes finds prompt variants that score higher on the specific metric by overfitting to its wording. The reviewer is the control. For projects where the loop applies, the discipline is to run eval optimize on the regression dataset (where improvements are measurable) and to review the candidate prompts carefully before promotion.

A few patterns and anti-patterns from running this loop ourselves and watching teams do the same.

Lead with a small, representative dataset, not a comprehensive one. The temptation when starting an eval is to try to cover every edge case the agent will ever see. The result is a dataset that takes an hour to run and that nobody runs on a change. Four cases that run in under a minute, as above, exercise the main behaviors and get run on every change. A regression dataset of three hundred cases that runs for an hour becomes a release gate at best and shelfware at worst.

Snapshot result files, not scores. The score is a summary; the result file is the evidence. When a regression surfaces three weeks after a prompt change, having the full result JSON from before and after the change (not just the mean score) is what lets eval compare show the specific case that moved. The per-case breakdown in Step 4 above would not have been possible from scores alone.

Separate the dataset from its expectations. The dataset is the inputs. The eval config is the expectations. They evolve on different cadences: the dataset grows as new real-world cases surface in production, and the config evolves as the team's definition of "good" matures. Keeping them in separate files, both committed to version control, makes it clear which half changed when a score moves.

Treat summary metrics with suspicion. The model-swap experiment in Step 5 is the clearest example. The mean dropped by half a point, but the per-case breakdown showed the drop was concentrated in one case while the others held or improved. A team that only looked at the mean would have made the wrong call.

More evals do not automatically lead to more fixes. A recent controlled study we ran found that coding agents using agents-cli run four times as many evals as coding agents without it, but change the agent after an eval about as often as the baseline. The behavior gain comes from what the skills put into the build at the start, not from the inner iteration. Evals are necessary for quality, but the quality comes from acting on what the evals show.

Scaffold an Agents CLI project and run the first eval loop in a handful of commands:

The full eval namespace is documented at google.github.io/agents-cli/cli. The source code, including the first-party eval primitives, is at github.com/google/agents-cli.

Smaller apps and faster GPU inference with LiteRT in Google Play services

Smaller apps and faster GPU inference with LiteRT in Google Play services

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

LiteRT in Google Play services is Android's on-device inference runtime for custom models, handling more than a trillion inferences a day across billions of devices. Today, it gets its biggest upgrade yet: GPU inference that runs about 1.4x faster than the legacy GPU delegate, powered by ML Drift and available through the new CompiledModel API, LiteRT’s modern successor to the legacy Interpreter API.

Until now, taking advantage of this faster GPU stack required bundling LiteRT in your app. In our testing, apps that switched to LiteRT in Google Play services are about 8 MB smaller, making them faster to download and lighter on users' devices, because the runtime, including the GPU accelerator, ships once with Play services rather than inside every app. More than 100K Android apps bundle their own copy of LiteRT or TensorFlow Lite today, so the savings add up quickly as apps switch.

If your Android app runs custom ML models on CPU or GPU, we encourage you to switch to LiteRT in Google Play services. You can see it in action in the Google AI Edge Gallery app, where the Scrapbook feature's image segmentation runs on LiteRT in Google Play services.

Developers that adopt LiteRT in Google Play Services enjoy immediate benefits:

LiteRT is the evolution of TensorFlow Lite. It keeps the same .tflite model format you rely on and adds the CompiledModel API, which makes acceleration across fragmented hardware reliable:

Whether you're starting fresh or switching from bundled LiteRT, using LiteRT in Google Play services takes a few lines of code:

If you're already using the legacy Interpreter API in Google Play services, your Interpreter code keeps working on CPU when you switch to play-services-litert. To run on the GPU, move to the CompiledModel API.

Let your coding agent do the work. In Agent Mode, the new LiteRT skill for Android Studio automates the move to LiteRT in Google Play services and the CompiledModel API. Ask it to “migrate my TFLite code to CompiledModel,” or type @litert-runtime, and it switches your app to play-services-litert, rewrites your Interpreter calls, and sets up GPU acceleration with CPU fallback. It can also scaffold a new LiteRT app. Treat it as a strong first pass: review the diff, build, and compare outputs on a real device. Get the skill on GitHub.

Upgrading from legacy APIs? The legacy GPU delegate and custom delegate APIs are deprecated in favor of CompiledModel GPU acceleration and the Compiler Plugin and Dispatch APIs, and NNAPI will be removed starting in 2027. Older SDKs now receive critical security fixes only. Apps you've already shipped keep working, but plan your move to the CompiledModel API now: code that uses deprecated APIs won't compile against the latest SDK, and staying on an older SDK can cause conflicts as your other dependencies move forward. See the release notes for details.

Looking ahead, LiteRT in Google Play services is our foundation for running your own task-specific models on Android, with CPU and GPU acceleration.

Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

Build Generative UI Apps with A2UI & CopilotKit Agents

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Agent-User Interactions are now evolving from text exchanges to rich UIs. Until recently, most conversations looked like this:

User: "Find me a holiday package to Bali in March." Agent: (six packages in prose, each with dates, hotel, flights, price) User: "The five-night one with breakfast." Agent: "Here's the link. You can book it on the operator's site."

Agents could plan the trip, but everything came back as walls of text. They had no standard way to render a selectable list of packages, a map of the destinations, or a date picker to carry you through the rest of the booking, inside the conversation itself.

Generative UI is the layer that lets agents build interfaces at runtime, shaped by what each request needs - giving users a rich, interactive experience. Implementing that takes two pieces: a way to describe the UI and an agentic stack that connects your agent to your application and renders that UI.

Generative UI covers a spectrum of approaches, from using predefined higher order components, to enabling your AI agent to have the flexibility to decide how to build the UI.

At the controlled end, you build each component ahead of time, and the agent picks which one to render and with what data. It's brand-safe but it does not allow for deep customization and personalization based on the user’s intent. At the open end, the agent returns a raw UI program written in HTML, Javascript and/or Dart that renders inside a sandboxed iframe. That covers anything you did not plan for, at the cost of a surface that drifts from your design system and executes code a model wrote.

A2UI works through the entire spectrum. It participates in controlled generation with decision models like Typesafe’s Jev and also open-ended code generation that may output A2UI JSON messages. The declarative mode, where agents assemble pre-declared components and functions, is the most popular. A2UI is the framework-agnostic standard for declaring UI intent, so local or remote agents can generate your UI from your existing component catalog using a common language.

Frontend developers can now ship the building blocks once, and the agent composes them for each request.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

The Basic Catalog ships 18 general-purpose primitives across layout, display, input, and containers. The agent can compose entirely different interfaces from the same 18 primitives, depending on what the request needs.

The Basic Catalog is deliberately sparse, so most production apps define their own custom catalog. A custom component can be anything your frontend already renders, including a live map the agent fills with coordinates while your renderer draws the route.

CopilotKit is built on AG-UI (Agent-User Interaction Protocol), which supports A2UI payload and provides Agent SDKs across many agentic backends.

Your agent emits A2UI operations. AG-UI carries them to the client as typed events. When the user takes any action, the values are sent back along the same stream.

A2UI works with any AG-UI compatible agent. Frameworks like Google ADK, LangChain, Mastra and Strands are all AG-UI compatible, so the setup is identical whichever one your agent runs on.

Setup comes down to two things: connecting your agent to CopilotKit, and defining which components the agent is allowed to use.

Using Google ADK as the example, the ag_ui_adk package wraps any ADK agent and serves it as an AG-UI endpoint on FastAPI:

On the app side, you register that endpoint with the CopilotKit runtime at /api/copilotkit. It's a few lines of standard setup, covered in the Google ADK quickstart, and it needs nothing A2UI-specific.

A2UI itself turns on from the client, where your components already are. Define what the agent is allowed to draw, map each definition to a renderer, and pass the catalog to the CopilotKit provider.

The description goes into the agent's prompt, so the model knows when to reach for FlightCard instead of a generic card. The Zod schema validates the props it sends back.

The A2UI configuration can be set up ahead of time on the server, or it can be created dynamically, shipped from the client to the server with the request. The ADK code above used dynamic configuration which is why it used generate_a2ui without defining it. The docs cover both approaches.

That’s the CopilotKit path. You can also consume the event stream directly with @ag-ui/client and hand the A2UI operations to any compliant renderer: @a2ui/react, or the Flutter, Lit, or Angular equivalent.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Most agent-generated UI is one-shot. The agent draws something, you read it, and it goes stale the moment anything changes.

A2UI surfaces are bi-directional and the components are reactive, which makes multi-step flows possible. Any action the user takes on the interface (picking dates, choosing a flight, entering passenger details) travels back along the same stream, and the agent re-emits the surface from there using those values.

Here is a high-level architecture example of an end-to-end booking flow, with an AG-UI agent on Cloud Run streaming A2UI surfaces to a CopilotKit frontend.

Two things make the round trip work: inputs bound to a data path, and a button carrying an action that names which paths to send back.

Validation runs on the client, so a bad email shows an error inline without a model call. Nothing reaches the agent until the button fires.

Then the client sends the bound values, and the agent re-emits against the same surfaceId. That is how the whole flow lives on one surface: flight search, results, passenger details, and confirmation.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Every AG-UI integration inherets these benefits, so you don't have to build them yourself:

Create a complete A2UI + CopilotKit application. This creates a starter A2UI application with Copilotkit Agent integration.

The CopilotKit Dojo has runnable A2UI demos: fixed server-side schema, dynamic schema against a custom catalog, and an error-recovery example where an invalid first render heals into a valid one.

Explore how to build Generative UI Agents with CopilotKit, or dive into A2UI documentation to learn how to integrate A2UI with any framework of your choice.

DevFest is back!

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Technology is evolving at a breakneck speed—from generative AI and autonomous agents, to scalable cloud infrastructure and modern web standards. What better way to stay connected, than to roll up your sleeves, experiment, and build alongside a thriving global community at your local DevFest.

Build, Secure and Scale in the Agentic Era

DevFest is the world’s largest, annual, community-led event series aimed to educate developers and builders about Google’s latest technologies. Hosted by Google Developer Groups (GDG), DevFest will run from October 1 through December 31, 2026. I used Claude with an MCP server to prep for an earnings report: six prompts and the tool calls behind them

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 3 • Originally published at equibles.com

Before a company reports, I want six things: the date, the consensus, management's guidance, last quarter's call, the options-implied move and the short interest. They live in six different places. Lately I've been asking for them in Claude with the Equibles MCP server connected (I work on Equibles), and I wanted to write down what the assistant actually calls behind each prompt.

The example is SoundHound (SOUN), which reports Q3 on November 5. Every answer below came from tool calls on November 3.

My first version of prompt 3 asked only for the guidance and what it left for the second half. The answer was correct and misleading. The guidance tool's newest source was the August 5 call, and SoundHound closed its acquisition of LivePerson on September 4. LivePerson booked $52.5M of revenue in Q2 on its own, and on the August call the CEO said the outlook would be updated to include it once the deal closed.

So reported Q3 revenue will include 27 days of LivePerson, while the $62.2M consensus is barely above SoundHound's own $61.9M in Q2. Comparing the result with that consensus without knowing about the deal would mislead you.

Adding "has anything happened since that changes it?" made the assistant read the investor relations news and the 8-K list, and the deal showed up. That's the general lesson for any tool-using assistant: a tool answers from what's on file as of its last source, so ask for the date of every number and for what has been filed since.

The full walkthrough, with every answer, how to check each one and the setup steps for ChatGPT and Claude, is here:

https://equibles.com/blog/how-to-research-a-stock-before-earnings-with-chatgpt-or-claude

What do you check before a company reports that isn't on this list?

Data: SEC filings, FINRA short interest, company investor relations pages and option quotes, processed by Equibles.

Hyperliquid API in Python: our $25 cap refused 892 of 892 $25 orders

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 3 • Originally published at botcookery.com

Originally published on BotCookery, a free site of build recipes for AI-built trading and prediction-market bots. Written with AI assistance. Every number below comes from real runs against Hyperliquid's public API on Oct 25, 2026. Signed calls are testnet only, and nothing here is financial advice or a trading signal.

We publish a copy-paste prompt that has an AI coding tool build a small Python starter for the Hyperliquid API: reads on mainnet or testnet, and signed orders on testnet only, capped at $25. On Oct 25 we checked whether that prompt actually holds up. We pasted the Oct 22 version into a clean folder, let it build, and ran it live: reads on both networks, a dry-run order for every listed perp, and signed testnet calls with a throwaway key that held no funds.

Most of it worked. Reads, asset lookups, price rounding, the --mainnet flag and the testnet-only guard all did what they should. Every one of the 158 live testnet perps and 178 live mainnet perps got a valid price at $10.

Then we asked for $25, and the bot refused. Every time, on both networks: 424 of 424 dry-run $25 orders on testnet and 468 of 468 on mainnet. The cap meant to keep orders small was the thing blocking them. That was one of four problems the rebuild turned up; here's each one, what we saw in the logs, and how the prompt changed.

Everything is a JSON POST. Reads go to /info with a type (meta, allMids, l2Book, candleSnapshot, clearinghouseState, openOrders) and need no signature. Orders go to /exchange and are signed, which is where the official hyperliquid-python-sdk earns its keep. Testnet lives at api.hyperliquid-testnet.xyz, mainnet at api.hyperliquid.xyz, and they are different markets with different prices. The first lines of each of our cycles were testnet mids, then mainnet mids:

A few rules the prompt spells out, because an AI tool will otherwise guess:

The starter places a post-only (Alo) limit 10% away from the mid, so the order rests instead of filling. Size is usd / limit, rounded up to szDecimals. Rounding up is deliberate: rounding down, or sizing on the mid, leaves a $10 buy under the $10 exchange minimum.

But rounding up always lands the notional a little over what you asked for. And the cap compared that notional to $25. So in the original build, plan BTC buy 25 printed:

That wasn't a BTC quirk. It refused all 424 dry-run $25 orders on testnet and all 468 on mainnet. The only orders that could pass were ones small enough that rounding didn't push them over, which made the "$10 to $25" range really "$10 to a bit under $25".

The fix is to check two different things. The requested amount must be between $10 and $25. The order's own notional must be at least $10 and no more than the request plus one size step (10^-szDecimals x limit). From the rebuilt version:

0.00035 x 72,876 = 25.5066. Over $25 by one step's worth of rounding, which is exactly what the cap now allows.

allMids still quotes delisted perps. The original build treated "has a mid" as "trades", so:

MATIC is one of 54 delisted perps on testnet. The meta response marks them with "isDelisted": true, and the asset lookup now raises on that flag:

Across three cycles of the rebuilt version, testnet listed 212 perps and mainnet 234. Every live one passed at both $10 and $25 on both sides (316/316 testnet, 356/356 mainnet), and every delisted one was refused:

The prompt has the bot call scheduleCancel before every order: a dead-man switch that cancels all open orders at a set time if the bot dies. The original rule was strict. If scheduleCancel didn't return ok, send no order.

The problem is that Hyperliquid only allows scheduleCancel above a trading-volume threshold. A published reply shows the error as Cannot set scheduled cancel time until enough volume traded. Required: $1000000. A brand-new testnet account will never clear that, so the strict rule meant a reader following the recipe could never place an order at all.

To be clear about what we saw: our throwaway key was never approved and the account was never funded, so the exchange stopped us earlier, before the volume check could come up. The orders.jsonl log from the run shows that refusal on every attempt:

So the volume error itself is sourced, not observed, and the code path for it is tested offline only.

The new behavior keeps the safety without the dead end:

Without the exchange's dead-man switch, the bot is its own. That's a weaker guarantee, so the hold is short and the cancel is confirmed.

One more check from the same runs: place and cancel-all don't accept --mainnet at all. Passing it gets cli.py: error: unrecognized arguments: --mainnet before anything is signed.

Hyperliquid lets a main account approve an "agent" (API wallet) key that signs on its behalf. The prompt's safety notes say to keep the main key away from the bot and give the bot only the agent key.

The original approve_agent.py loaded the main key from the same env file it then wrote the agent key to. So after a successful approval, the key you're supposed to keep away from the bot sat right next to it.

Now it reads the main key once with getpass.getpass(), never writes it anywhere, and saves only HL_MODE=testnet, HL_AGENT_PRIVATE_KEY and HL_ACCOUNT_ADDRESS (the main address) to ~/.config/hl-bot/.env with chmod 600. It also saves only the agent key the SDK returns from approve_agent, and only if the result is ok. On our unfunded account it saved nothing:

If you built the starter from the earlier prompt, paste this into the same project:

The full prompt is on the recipe page. If you'd rather start from code, the rebuilt starter is on GitHub at lazyclaw137-ship-it/hyperliquid-api-starter-testnet. Reads need nothing:

Signed calls need a testnet account and an approved agent key, and stay on testnet. The code checks for that twice.

Verified working on live data in read-only mode, Oct 25, 2026. Reads ran live on testnet and mainnet, and order sizing was checked against every listed perp on both. Signed testnet calls used an unfunded throwaway key, so the exchange refused them before any order existed. We did not see a resting order, a fill or the dead-man volume error ourselves; the code path for that error is tested offline only.

More recipes, including a Hyperliquid funding-rate monitor, are at botcookery.com.

Trusted SSL Certificates for Localhost

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 3 • Originally published at kevinleary.net

The best way I’ve found to add SSL certificates to a localhost environment is using mkcert, which is technically not a self-signed certificate at all. Instead, it creates a local certificate signing authority, adds it to your machine, and uses that to sign certificates. The end result is a fully valid SSL certificate that is not self-signed, so it works with modern browsers and aligns with security protocols that block self-signed SSL certs.

mkcert is an open source command line tool written by Filippo Valsorda, a cryptographer who spent years on the Go security team at Google. As described on GitHub, it’s:

a simple tool for making locally-trusted development certificates that requires no configuration.

Under the hood it does two things. First, it generates a private certificate authority (CA) that lives only on your machine and installs it into your system and browser trust stores, the same lists of trusted authorities that let your browser trust certificates from Let’s Encrypt or DigiCert. Second, it signs leaf certificates with that CA for whatever hostnames you ask for. Because the signing authority is trusted by your machine, every certificate it issues is trusted too: real HTTPS, green padlock, no warnings, no exceptions to click through. This is fundamentally different from the classic openssl self-signed certificate. A self-signed cert has no authority behind it, so browsers correctly treat it as untrustworthy. mkcert gives you the full chain of trust, just scoped privately to your computer. The private CA key never leaves your machine, which is also why this is safe: nobody else’s browser will ever trust certificates signed by your local CA.

mkcert supports Mac, Windows and Linux machines, so it should work with your setup.

The nss package is only needed if you use Firefox, which keeps its own certificate store separate from the operating system’s.

Once you’ve installed the tool, create the local certificate authority (CA) and install it into your trust stores:

Once you’ve set up a local authority for signing certificates, you can add SSL certificates for your localhost domains:

This will create two *.pem certificate files, ./example.test+4.pem and ./example.test+4-key.pem, for the following hostnames:

These SSL certificates effectively cover the following URLs that are common localhost hosts:

The mkcert -install step works by adding your local CA to every certificate root store it can find. It currently supports:

To install the local root CA into only a subset of them, set the TRUST_STORES environment variable to a comma-separated list. The options are system, java, and nss (which includes Firefox):

You’ll need to configure a host pointing example.test and www.example.test to your local web server. Once that’s set up, and you have the certificates added to your system, you might be done. Many localhost servers automatically load certificates from your machine’s certificate store. If you restart your browser and the URL loads successfully with a secure, valid SSL certificate attached (lock icon to the left of your URL) then you’re all set. If you don’t see a secure connection yet, you may need to specifically point your local web server to use the *.pem files generated by mkcert in the previous steps.

Open your httpd.conf file and add or update the following to it:

Restart Apache to load the certificates, then re-check your browser to verify they’re working. You may need to restart your browser to get your site(s) working.

Open your nginx.conf file and add or update the following to the http block:

Restart nginx to load the certificates, then re-check your browser to verify they’re working. You may need to restart your browser to get your site(s) working.

My setup is nginx configured to load the certificate files mkcert outputs, exactly as shown above. Every local project gets a .test domain, and they all share one certificate. When I spin up a new project and need another domain covered, I don’t touch the CA at all. I just re-run mkcert with the full list of hostnames:

…point nginx at the newly generated .pem files if the filenames changed, and restart nginx:

That’s the entire workflow. The new certificate is signed by the same local CA my machine already trusts, so the browser accepts it immediately with no warnings and no browser restart.

Adding domains never requires it. mkcert -install only puts the CA into your trust stores, and once it’s there, every certificate the CA signs is trusted automatically. You only need to re-run it when the set of trust stores changes or the CA itself does:

If certificates that used to work suddenly throw authority warnings, re-running mkcert -install is the first thing to try; it’s idempotent and safe to run repeatedly.

What a killed Playwright script leaves behind on Linux: processes, profile dirs and a SIGTERM that does not exit

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A Playwright script ends in more ways than await browser.close(): it throws, someone calls process.exit(), a container runtime sends SIGTERM, the OOM killer sends SIGKILL. I wanted to know what each of these leaves behind on a Linux host: running Chromium processes, temp directories, or a Node process that ignores the signal.

I ran all of it on 2026-11-03: Linux 6.8, Node v24.22.2, Playwright 1.63.0 with the bundled chrome-headless-shell (revision 1243). Everything below is that one machine and one browser build. Containers, systemd, macOS and Windows are not covered.

The child script launches a browser, opens a page, prints READY, and then either waits for a GO line on stdin and ends in a chosen way, or hangs so that the harness can send it a signal. The cdp mode does not launch anything: it attaches to a Chromium that the harness started separately, which is what many browser pools do.

The harness reads the process tree from /proc while the child is running, ends the child, waits three seconds, and then checks which of those PIDs are still alive (zombies do not count) and whether the browser's profile directory is still on disk. It kills and deletes only what it started.

In the cdp rows the first count was 14 or 15 and the second 10 or 11 across my runs, so read that as "most of the tree stays". The other rows were identical on every run. In the cdp rows the profile directory belongs to the browser the harness started itself, and it is still there because that browser is still running.

Playwright's own launch cleans up after a crash, even after SIGKILL. throw, exit and SIGKILL all end with zero Chromium processes. In a one-off check (script not included) I printed the command line of the launched browser, and it ends in --remote-debugging-pipe. I did not trace the mechanism, but it fits a browser that exits when the other end of its pipe closes. The cdp rows are the contrast: that browser listens on a port, and nothing ties it to the script. In the same one-off check, chromium.launchServer() also left no processes after SIGKILL, so it is not only launch().

Browsers you did not launch are not cleaned up at all. In the cdp rows, about ten Chromium processes stayed alive after browser.close() and after SIGKILL. This is the case where a leak actually happens. The docs for browser.close() say that for a browser you connected to, it "clears all created contexts belonging to this browser and disconnects from the browser server". It does not stop the browser. Whoever started it owns its lifetime. If you run a pool this way, you need a supervisor that restarts or kills the browser itself, and a periodic check that counts processes.

SIGKILL leaves the profile directory. The launch SIGKILL row says profile dir left: true. Each one was about 224 KB in my runs, which is nothing for a single crash and something for a worker that gets OOM-killed a few times a day on a small /tmp. A normal close(), an exception, process.exit(), SIGTERM and SIGINT all removed it. I did not look into which Playwright process does the deleting. Whatever it is, SIGKILL gives it no chance to run.

launch SIGTERM says node: still running. The child had a setInterval standing in for an HTTP server, and after SIGTERM the browser was gone but Node kept going. The reason is the handleSIGTERM launch option, which defaults to true and is documented as "Close the browser process on SIGTERM". Playwright installs a handler that closes the browser. Node's documentation says that once a SIGTERM or SIGINT listener is installed, the default behaviour (exit with code 128 plus the signal number) is removed. So the server stays up with no browser behind it. SIGINT ended with exit code 130 in my run; I did not look into why it differs from SIGTERM.

I did not test what Docker or Kubernetes do from here. The consequence I can state from this measurement is only that a service which does nothing on SIGTERM keeps running with a dead browser, and anything that waits for it to exit has to fall back to SIGKILL.

This is the service skeleton I used to test two remedies: close the browser and exit on SIGTERM and SIGINT, and sweep profile directories that nothing is using when the service starts.

The sweep treats a directory as stale only if no process on the machine has it in a --user-data-dir argument and it is older than a minute. Chromium rewrites its own argument list with spaces on Linux, which is why the pattern stops at whitespace and not only at the NUL byte. I found that when my first version of the harness reported "profile left: false" for a directory that was plainly still there.

To test it I used a second script that is not included: it starts the service, SIGKILLs it, starts it twice more, sends SIGTERM to both, and counts new profile directories under /tmp each time. The sweep deletes everything under that prefix that no process references, so the test script lowered the age limit to five seconds and waited six. Results:

After SIGKILL one profile directory stayed. The next start swept it (swept stale profiles: 1). Two live services each kept their own directory, so the sweep did not touch a running browser. Both services exited with code 0 on SIGTERM, in 106 to 281 ms across my runs, and no new directories were left.

Sources: Playwright BrowserType launch options for handleSIGTERM and handleSIGINT, the browser.close() text from the Playwright 1.63.0 type definitions, and Node.js process signal events.

Written with AI assistance. Every code sample was run on 2026-11-03 with Playwright 1.63.0 (chrome-headless-shell revision 1243) and Node v24.22.2; the output above is from that run.

Chord Buys Its Rival Zenlytic To Build An Agentic Version Of A CDP

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Two data infrastructure startups with agentic commerce ambitions are combining forces.

On Tuesday, Chord, a six-year-old commerce tech startup, acquired Zenlytic, an AI startup with an anthropomorphized data analyst agent named “Zoë,” for $5 million.

Chord’s co-founder and CEO, Bryan Mahoney, told AdExchanger that the combined business has around 30 full-time employees and some 60 customers, with an annual run rate nearing $10 million.

Chord launched as a “headless commerce” software company, Mahoney said.

At the time, the headless approach was catching on with Shopify merchants and social-native sellers. The idea was to decouple the front-end website from back-end fulfillment, making for a more agile business and faster-loading pages at a time when Google’s SEO prioritized speed. Shopify has since addressed the problem by taking steps to make its own storefronts faster.

Since the rise of LLMs, Chord and Zenlytic have both pivoted to become explicitly AI-based data infrastructure services. For instance, Mahoney said, Chord is now paid on a usage basis rather than traditional subscription pricing. And because pricing is usage-based, Chord closely tracks how often users prompt its AI agent for an answer – a rate that has accelerated to about once every 20 seconds, he said.

Although the more people use agents, the more ways they find to abuse them.

Mahoney pointed to a recent story in which someone figured out they could use McDonald’s online customer support and ordering agent to answer demanding math and computer science questions – a workaround to having to pay for one’s own AI tokens. (McDonald’s has since fixed this, by the way.)

Chord guards against this kind of misuse. It’s trained its AI agent with the necessary context so that off-base prompts return a response that this isn’t the right agent for the task. That includes requests to use its agent “like a therapist,” say, or for something like “make these results look better to a CFO,” Mahoney said.

Those guardrails matter more as Chord’s business shifts.

The business model is now more like an agentic version of a CDP, Mahoney said. For example, Sonos, a Chord client, can use it to track customers and enrich first-party data, such as understanding when customers are moving to a new home – an opportune time to send emails or SMS notifications about new products.

The data analyst agent “largely replaces” Chord’s own analyst agent, Mahoney said.

Guess human data analysts aren’t the only ones who need to be worried about being replaced by AI. Even agents can be put out of work.

But Chord also has other agents that work better alongside Zoë, Mahoney added. Chord has gone deeper into commerce infrastructure, for instance, with use cases geared toward Shopify-type businesses, and customers using the Snowflake data warehouse in particular. Zenlytic, by contrast, has tended to work with large enterprise brands of all kinds, not just in ecommerce, and supports many more cloud data warehouses.

The goal, Mahoney said, is for some of Zenlytic’s large customers, including Black & Decker, Verizon, J.Crew and Stanley, to adopt Chord’s data infrastructure and activation, including the ability to segment profiles or audiences for campaign targeting and measurement.

But winning over large brands requires confidence in the technology, and right now there’s an odd mix of growing trust and distrust of AI agent outputs.

Mahoney noted that the outright “hallucinations” of earlier LLMs have become relatively rare. “Now it might be more like a lucid dream,” he said. The outputs are believable and often can feel correct.

And so marketers need to be aware that a confident-sounding AI agent can still lead them badly astray. It’s a concern Mahoney shares.

“We constantly [need to] evaluate the answers that we’re getting back to make sure that the way we’re using AI is increasing confidence on a daily basis, not getting eroded,” he said. “These are some of the things that keep me up at night.”

If you ask the executive leadership of Affle and Perion why the two companies are merging, the answer you’ll get is “synergies.”

A combo of Tubi, sports and political ads powered Fox’s growth this last quarter, even as Fox News lost viewers and the Roku deal awaits DOJ clearance.

It’s fall, and M&A is in the air. Two acquisitions happened this week: Stagwell bought Freestar, and DoorDash bought Instacart.

Starting in Q4, Reddit will test logged-in-only mode to protect its user engagement, improve ad targeting and keep AI from scraping its content.

DoorDash on Thursday confirmed the rumors and announced its intention to acquire Instacart at a $17 billion valuation.

Last week, a district court judge in New Mexico ordered Meta to pay $25 billion for misleading Facebook users about how it handled their data. Does it matter?

Here's Why You Shouldn't Use AI to Vote

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

As Americans go to the polls today, many will have had their decision about who to vote for informed by an artificial intelligence-powered chatbot.

Around 50 percent of recently surveyed US adults have at one time used an AI chatbot, and 8 percent say they have used these tools ahead of the midterms to find information on candidates and policies, according to Pew Research.

But a lot of information from these chatbots is out of date and has been hallucinated or sourced from partisan websites.

Even supposedly non-partisan chatbots like OpenAI’s ChatGPT, Anthropic’s Claude, and Google’s Gemini have been found wanting when it comes to questions about the midterms.

Just last week, Google’s AI Mode refused to provide the results of the 2020 elections and the company’s Gemini chatbot said the deadline to request a mail-in ballot in Pennsylvania was November 3, rather than the real deadline of October 27, according to research by Forum AI.

According to the Forum AI research, almost 40 percent of election answers given by all the mainstream chatbots contained at least one factual error. In August, when Forum AI’s researchers asked why someone should vote for North Carolina congressman Don Davis, Claude gave a response that confused the lawmaker with a “former school superintendent” of the same name.

Google and Anthropic did not respond to requests for comment. Open AI directed WIRED to a blog outlining the company’s approach to elections in 2026.

Part of the problem is that chatbots are reliant on an increasingly problematic pool of online information. Research by NewsGuard found that mainstream chatbots cite so-called “pink slime” websites nearly 50 percent of the time when asked about candidates and policies related to the midterms. “Pink slime” sites present as mainstream news websites, but are in fact funded by political or ideological groups and individuals.

Ahead of the midterms, many more of these sites have sprung up, and chatbots are paying attention. The worst offender, according to NewsGuard, was OpenAI’s ChatGPT, which cited the “pink slime” sites in more than 70 percent of searches, while xAI’s Grok chatbot performed best, citing “pink slime” sites in 30 percent of cases.

“I don't think that most Americans are prepared to navigate an election in the age of consumer AI,” Nina Jankowicz, executive director of the American Sunlight Project and a former disinformation czar during the Biden administration, tells WIRED. “One of the problems is that executives and companies have really overpromised on what AI is capable of. It has to ingest this information from somewhere, and often those sources are not very good. And it's predictive, so if it doesn't know the answer, it does frequently make things up.”

What's the meaning of the "C" on your USB-C ports?

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For a cable that is considered a "universal connector," the trusty USB comes in a staggering array of different shapes and colors. You may find yourself in the aisle in the electronics store or scrolling Amazon, staring at the different options and wondering what makes each of them different from the other and which one is the correct one for your needs.

In the past few years, we have seen more and more devices finally offer just one clear choice: USB Type-C. It comes in one shape, has no color coding and promises to work with any USB Type-C device. But what does the "C" actually stand for? Does it have a secret meaning? Well, the answer is a lot more straightforward than one might think.

If you go searching online for the answer to that question, you might come across several theories that the internet loves to repeat over and over. Those are all myths and are easily explained away.

Explainer sites keep running into the same three guesses. Some people assume the C stands for "connectivity," since the new connector works with such a wide range of devices. Others figure it means "charging," because that's what most of us use it for. And a third camp thinks it refers to the rounded shape of the plug. None of them are right. The "C" only names the connector, and it does not stand for any word beginning with the letter C.

The myths don't end with the name. There are even more about what a USB-C port can do. Plenty of people assume it means fast, or that it's the same thing as Thunderbolt, or that every USB-C port will fast charge a laptop and drive a monitor. But they don't all have the same capabilities. The connector is just the shape, and what comes through it depends on the device and the cable, which is why a USB-C port isn't a guarantee of high speeds.

The real answer is a lot less exciting. USB Type-C is just the connector that comes after Type-A and Type-B.

USB 1.0 arrived in January 1996 with only those two. They weren't interchangeable on purpose. Type-A, the flat rectangle, went into the host, meaning the computer or a power source. Type-B, the chunky square one, went into the device, meaning the printer or scanner on the other end. USB was built around that one-way idea, with the host supplying power and running the show and the peripheral going along with it. Different shapes meant you couldn't plug a cable in the wrong way.

Then the different shapes and colors started piling up. USB 2.0 in 2000 brought the mini connectors, and micro followed because phones and cameras wanted something thinner. The colors were a loose convention for telling ports apart: black for USB 2.0, blue for USB 3.0 and red or yellow for ports that keep supplying power while the computer sleeps (even orange ports don't mean the same thing everywhere). Manufacturers were free to ignore all of it, and plenty did. Most of them were B-style ends on the device side, so you still had a Type-A on one end and some flavor of Type-B on the other.

USB Type-C was finalized in August 2014, and it addressed nearly every complaint people had with the older versions. It was thin, so it fit even the thinnest phones and gadgets. It was reversible, so gone were the days of guessing which way to plug it in. And it dropped the idea of two different shapes, so the host and device split moved out of the connector and into the devices themselves.

Since the shape stays the same, there is no need for color coding either. The two ends talk to each other and agree on how much data and power to send, and newer cables simply move more through the same connector. USB4 Version 2.0 now reaches up to 80Gbps over that same Type-C connector.

USB Type-C was received positively by consumers and tech companies thanks to its speed, small footprint and versatility. When it first came out, people mostly praised the reversible connection, but it's still not a perfect connector. It has a delicate, thin plastic tab (the tongue) inside the socket that holds the pins, and if it bends or snaps, the port is ruined. It's also still a little finicky to plug in.

At the moment, there are no clear plans for a Type-D connector, but if we were picking, we'd go for a simple ball-shaped plug that attaches to its socket magnetically.

Cogent Unveils VR-1.1, Its Latest Frontier Cyber Model, Which Tops LiveCVEBench for Vulnerability Repair

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On 190 recently disclosed CVEs, VR-1.1 produced working repairs for 57.9%, compared with 45.3% for both Claude Code and Codex.

SAN FRANCISCO, Nov. 3, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Cogent Security today released VR-1.1, the latest version of its frontier reasoning model for cybersecurity. VR-1.1 tops LiveCVEBench, a public benchmark that tests whether AI agents can repair recently disclosed vulnerabilities in real codebases. It repaired 57.9% of the benchmark's 190 tasks, compared with 45.3% for both Claude Code and Codex.

Security teams already know about more vulnerabilities than they can fix, and AI has sharply increased the rate at which new ones are found. In July, high- and critical-severity disclosures from major software makers hit five times the monthly record set before Anthropic announced Claude Mythos Preview in April. Each repair still depends on an engineer who has to understand the flaw well enough to change the code without breaking anything else, and that work sets the pace for most vulnerability programs.

"Finding vulnerabilities got a lot faster this year. Fixing them is still slow, and that's where security teams are stuck," said Vineet Edupuganti, co-founder and CEO of Cogent Security. "Every company is going to need AI that can fix security problems as fast as AI finds them, without breaking the systems the business runs on. We built VR-1.1 for that work, and on a public benchmark it fixed more real vulnerabilities than the leading general-purpose AI coding tools."

LiveCVEBench was created by researchers at Harbin Institute of Technology and Kuaishou Technology. It turns newly disclosed CVEs into repair tasks across 14 programming languages and a mix of injection, access-control, configuration and memory-safety flaws, and it adds new CVEs regularly. Each task places an agent in a real development environment with a vulnerability report and the affected codebase.

A patch passes only if the benchmark's security tests show the vulnerability is closed and its functionality tests show the software still works as intended. In Cogent's evaluation, outbound network access was blocked during the repair phase, and the grading tests were withheld until each agent finished. Inference budgets differed across the three systems.

Where the environment allows, VR-1.1 starts by writing its own proof of concept that triggers the reported flaw. It traces the vulnerability through the affected code, including related paths that share the same weakness, and makes a focused change, such as checking an index before use. A separate review then examines the patch in a fresh context, looking for ways around the fix and for bugs the change introduced.

VR-1.1 also writes regression tests that catch the bug if a later code change brings it back. When an attempt fails, it carries the earlier diagnosis and failure details into the next one.

"A patch might block the exact payload from a bug report, but leave the same vulnerability open via a different endpoint or break core functionality for users," said Geng Sng, co-founder and CTO of Cogent Security. "We designed VR-1.1 to check its own work for both problems before it finishes. Security teams get a patch they can inspect, with the diagnosis and test results alongside it."

VR-1.1 extends the remediation capabilities Cogent introduced with VR-1, the frontier reasoning model it released in July to find and prove the attack paths an adversary could reach inside an enterprise. Cogent Research's full write-up, including methodology, can be found here.

Cogent Security is an applied AI lab building the enterprise platform for autonomous cyber defense. Its AI agents find and fix vulnerabilities at machine speed so every enterprise can run its own Cyber Defense Factory. Starting with autonomous vulnerability management, Cogent carries vulnerabilities from disclosure to a verified fix across cloud, on-premises, and endpoint environments, identifying exposure within minutes and confirming what attackers can reach along the way. Customers set the policies the agents follow and decide which fixes run autonomously and which wait for approval. Fortune 500 security teams using Cogent have reduced the exposure window for critical vulnerabilities by 97 percent. Built by researchers and operators from Google DeepMind, Abnormal Security, and Coinbase, Cogent is backed by Greylock Partners and Bain Capital Ventures. Learn more at cogent.com.

Sailing through Cassiopeia

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Andrea Arbizzi, taken from Maremma, Italy

A vast sea of faintly lit dust in Cassiopeia envelops the Sailboat Cluster (NGC 225, upper right), an open cluster named for the way its brightest stars and the blue haze of the reflection nebula VdB 4 sketch out a sail. NGC 225 lies about 2,200 light-years away, accompanied by the dark dust lanes of LDN 1291 (just to its left in this image). The photographer collected 15 hours of unfiltered exposure with a 6-inch f/5.6 refractor and one-shot color camera.

Bosch gives chips their own division, eyeing €5bn in sales by 2030

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The car-parts giant will open its fabs to outside customers and chase robotics demand from January 2028.

The Bosch Parkhaus near Stuttgart Airport

Bosch will move its semiconductor business into a dedicated division from January 2028, as the German supplier looks to expand chip sales beyond the automotive industry.

The company is targeting annual semiconductor revenue of around €5bn by 2030, it announced today. The new unit, which will be called Semiconductors, will remain part of Bosch rather than becoming a separate company.

Its chip business currently sits within the Mobility Electronics division, which will continue to handle the production of electronic control units for vehicles.

The restructuring reflects Bosch’s ambition to find more customers for its chips outside the automotive sector.

The company plans to open its manufacturing facilities to external clients and expand its foundry business, producing semiconductors for other companies.

Its target markets include robotics, factory automation and AI data centres, alongside consumer devices such as phones and wearables. Bosch says its components already play a role in controlling humanoid robots.

“With the founding of a dedicated unit, we are opening a new chapter in our more than 60-year semiconductor history,” said Erik Rein, who heads Bosch’s chip business.

Bosch has several established positions in specialised semiconductor markets. It is a leading supplier of MEMS sensors, the miniature motion and pressure sensors used in devices such as smartphones and robots.

The company also produces silicon carbide chips and custom circuits for power systems.

Its manufacturing footprint spans around 40 chip sites worldwide. Bosch has invested approximately €3bn in its wafer plants in Dresden and Reutlingen, and is spending around $2bn to convert its Roseville, California facility to silicon carbide production.

The company also plans to expand its test centre in Penang, Malaysia, through the mid-2030s. It holds a 10% stake in ESMC, the TSMC-led semiconductor joint venture building a factory in Dresden.

The move comes as Europe’s automotive industry continues to grapple with chip supply risks.

Carmakers spent part of the autumn managing shortages during the Nexperia dispute, which exposed the industry’s dependence on a limited number of suppliers.

By offering manufacturing capacity to outside customers, Bosch could help diversify Europe’s chip supply while seeking new markets for its own technology.

Bosch deputy chairman Markus Heyn said the company’s sensors are “the key” to its products and “the technological basis” for its customers’ innovations.

Prediction: This Will Be D-Wave Quantum's Stock Price by the End of 2030

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The quantum computing pure play has its sights on a potentially profitable niche of this nascent technology.

Experts believe it could take years or even decades for commercially viable quantum computers to be useful at scale. But that isn't stopping early movers from trying their luck at the opportunity, despite the high risk of failure for those that attempt to pioneer new technologies.

D-Wave Quantum (QBTS -5.26%) is one of the ambitious pure plays in the space. And its rapidly growing business suggests it has already developed something that customers may be willing to pay for. But can its current efforts justify the sky-high valuation of its shares?

Quantum computing is a technology that many of us have heard about, but that few truly understand. At its core, it aims to bring an entirely new type of tool to bear on extremely complex computational problems. Classical computers (including whatever digital device you're reading this on) store and process data as bits, which can only have two possible values: one or zero. Quantum computers use quantum bits (qubits) that -- thanks to some peculiar but fundamental aspects of physics -- can temporarily have values that are neither one nor zero, but are instead complex probability vectors.

Think of a bit as a coin that has always either landed on heads or tails, and a qubit as a coin that (while it's in the midst of a problem) is spinning through the air. When it's spinning, it's in superposition of both possibilities, and the angles and speed of that spin influence the probability that it will land on one side versus the other.

If all of that sounds radical and head-achingly complex, that's because it is. It's also completely unnecessary for most common computing workloads.

But if quantum computing technology can ever be made to work as hoped, it could be used to rapidly solve types of problems that would take the world's most powerful classical supercomputers years or even eons to solve, and lead to revolutionary advancements in areas like drug discovery, materials science, and logistics, among others.

Scientists have been working on quantum computing for decades. And the reality is that we still don't know for sure when the technology will deliver on the lofty hopes they have for it. That said, investors have been encouraged by industry leader IBM, which has said it believes it can produce fault-tolerant devices by 2029. For its part, D-Wave Quantum has also begun early commercialization of its own quantum computing devices.

While D-Wave's second-quarter revenue was nearly flat year over year, the company did see a 1,120% jump in bookings for the first half of the year. Bookings refer to the value of client orders that were received during the period, even if they have not yet been fulfilled. That may provide a better view of how D-Wave's business is performing, considering its small scale and the challenges involved in manufacturing and developing its devices. It's also necessary to note that this growth came off an extremely small base. Its actual first-half bookings were just $35.5 million. (Compare that to IBM's Q2 revenue of $17.2 billion.)

D-Wave's flagship product is its Advantage2, a refrigerator-sized machine that is built around an unusual niche technology within the quantum computing realm: quantum annealing. That subset of the technology can be used to find some of the lowest-energy solutions to complex problems. It's important to note that it's quite different from the gate-based quantum computing being pioneered by its rivals, which are aiming for broad-use machines that are analogous to classical computers.

For the foreseeable future, clients will mostly use D-Wave's devices for research rather than full-scale commercial applications.

Over the next four years, D-Wave's stock may experience substantial gains at times driven by positive political developments or breakthroughs in the industry as a whole. But as it stands, I believe these are unlikely to lead to sustainable investor returns over the long term.

Right now, the stock trades at a price-to-sales (P/S) multiple of 307 compared to the S&P 500's average of 3.7. In other words, D-Wave Quantum is priced at a highly speculative level. That limits the returns investors can expect, even under a best-case scenario. Meanwhile, investors can anticipate that D-Wave's cash burn and losses will remain high as it strives to maintain a technical edge over much larger, more-established rivals like Alphabet and IBM.

For these reasons, I don't expect the stock to gain any value over the next four years. And shares will probably still be worth around $10.65 by the end of 2030. Investors who are optimistic about quantum technology should probably wait for a better entry point before considering a position in the stock.

Will Ebiefung has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet and International Business Machines. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Sarri, i perchè di una crisi

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Una premessa: Maurizio Sarri è un grande allenatore. Le sue idee hanno fatto scuola nel calcio italiano. C'é stato un periodo in cui il Sarrismo era diventata una moda. Da imitare. Oggi, però, ci sono nuove tendenze. Inutile nasconderlo, la proposta tattica dell'allenatore toscano convince di meno.. C'é un altro aspetto da mettere in evidenza. Nella sua carriera, soprattutto agli inizi, il tecnico è stato spesso abbandonato dai suoi dirigenti che gli hanno voltato le spalle alle prime difficoltà. All'Atalanta tutto questo non sta succedendo. Il direttore sportivo Giuntoli lo sta proteggendo a spada tratta. Dimostrando coerenza e lealtà. Merce rara nel calcio italiano. Ma fino a quando potrà continuare a farlo?

L'elenco degli infortunati dell'Atalanta è impressionante. Ed è un aspetto che l'area sportiva della Dea dovrà valutare. Tutti questi problemi fisici non possono essere figli del caso. Sono il risultato di un modo diverso di allenarsi? Di una preparazione estiva che non ha funzionato? E' chiaro che l'infermeria piena sta ritardando il lavoro del tecnico toscano. Ma i dubbi sul lavoro di Sarri vanno oltre i risultati. Dopo dieci giornate di campionato e alcuni turni di Conference non c'é traccia delle sue idee calcistiche. Come se il gruppo inconsciamente le rifiutasse. Maurizio ha lasciato il segno con giocatori molto più scarsi dui quelli che comunque vanno in campo con la maglia dell'Atalanta. Perchè questo matrimonio non funziona? Perchè c'é ancora Gasp nella testa dei giocatori. Con una proposta di calcio che è l'antitesi di quella di Sarri. Non è semplice spiegare a un gruppo che con Gasp ha vinto e si è divertito che un modo di stare in campo diverso può essere altrettanto vincente. Ed è più facile, molto più facile, passare dal calcio di Sarri a quello di Gasp che non il contrario. Anche la seconda squadra dell'Atalanta, che partecipa al campionato di Serie C, è in grande difficoltà perchè ha cambiato atteggiamento tattico e anche i giovani fanno fatica a calarsi nel nuovo abito.

Forse nel mercato estivo il direttore Giuntoli doveva essere più coraggioso nelle sue scelte. Per aiutare Sarri avrebbe dovuto operare una rivoluzione profonda nel gruppo squadra. Servivano dentro lo spogliatoio teste nuove e magari due-tre giocatori che avessero già lavorato con Sarri e che quindi fossero in grado di spiegare ai compagni concetti che all'inizio non sono semplici perchè richiedono grande applicazione mentale. Non so se il progetto Sarri-Atalanta sia ancora salvabile. Ho qualche dubbio. Ma se la famiglia Percassi e Giuntoli sono convinti che questa sia comunque la streada giusta, anche a costo di vivere un anno di transizione magari restandoi fuori dalle Coppe, allora già dal mercato ninvernale dovranno arrivare segnali giusti. Cioè giocatori che pososno esaltare il Sarrismo
