The TechCrunch Founder Summit agenda is only half the reason to be in Boston on Nov. 4

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now.

Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE.

You can learn a lot from a great founder session. But sometimes the conversation that changes what you do next happens five minutes after it ends. And with the event happening tomorrow, there is another reason to make sure you are in the room: the format is designed to help founders learn in different ways throughout the day.

On November 4, TechCrunch Founder Summit 2026 brings founders and investors together at SoWa Power Station in Boston for one day of practical learning, candid conversations, live pitching, smaller roundtables, and networking.

Register now to save up to $100 on your pass, plus get a second for free. Or save up to 30% on group bundles.

The Build Stage focuses on the decisions founders make while they are still shaping, testing, and growing the business. These sessions get into the mechanics behind building a company, from distribution and fundraising to team building and pitching.

Hunter Worland of NEA opens the conversation on why distribution can become a startup’s next competitive advantage, especially when strong technology alone is not enough to win customers.

Kristen Craft of Fidelity Private Shares brings a current view of fundraising trends, helping founders understand how the capital environment is shifting and what that means for the way they approach their next raise.

The stage also gets tactical about building high-performing teams and sharpening the story you tell investors, customers, and potential hires.

Dave Balter of True Ventures and Edisyl takes on a deceptively simple question: Does your pitch earn the next conversation?

The value of the Build Stage is straightforward: these are lessons founders can take back to their companies immediately.

If the Build Stage is about creating momentum, the Impact Stage is about what happens once that momentum becomes real. This is where founders can step back and think about bigger decisions around product-market fit, scaling, enterprise relationships, leadership, and long-term growth.

Steve Fredette, president, co-founder, and director of Toast, brings the perspective of building and scaling one of Boston’s most recognizable startup success stories.

Rudina Seseri, founder and managing partner of Glasswing Ventures, explores the tension every founder eventually faces: when to scale and when hitting the brakes may actually protect the business.

Kent Bennett of Bessemer Venture Partners tackles product-market fit before scale, helping founders think more critically about whether the signals they are seeing truly justify the next phase of growth.

Chase Garbarino, CEO and co-founder of HqO, brings the conversation home with the Boston Founder Playbook and a perspective on building ambitious companies outside the Bay Area.

The Impact Stage is valuable because it creates space for founders to get out of the weeds for a moment and ask whether they are scaling the right things, in the right way, at the right time.

Main-stage sessions can give you perspective. Roundtables give you room to get specific.

Founder Summit’s smaller roundtables are built around highly focused problems that many founders are actively solving right now, from entering difficult verticals and building enterprise trust to creating lean teams and opening doors with major customers.

Chirag Shah of Define Ventures digs into what happens when your first vertical is also one of the hardest to enter.

A technical session with Shishir Tewari of Procore Technologies moves the AI conversation from broad hype to the practical challenge of reducing hallucinations with better enterprise context.

Merve Kitapci of ADIN.AI gets highly specific about enterprise selling and what it takes to land major global customers before Series A.

Andy Tang of Draper Associates opens a different kind of founder conversation: what if you are building something the market does not even have a name for yet?

Linden Tibbets of IFTTT explores how AI may change the assumptions founders make about team size, productivity, and what a smaller company can realistically accomplish.

The value here is participation. Instead of simply hearing how someone else approached a problem, founders can bring their own questions, compare experiences with the people around the table, and leave with ideas that are much closer to their actual business.

Founder Summit also gives attendees the chance to see what happens when founders put their stories in front of investors in real time.

During So You Think You Can Pitch?, founders make their cases in front of investors, including Dave Balter, Chirag Shah, and Simran Suri of Maveron.

This is one of the most interactive parts of the day because it turns advice about pitching into something founders can actually watch: what gets an investor’s attention, where a story loses momentum, and which questions come up immediately.

For anyone preparing to raise, sell, recruit, or simply explain their company more clearly, seeing those reactions live can be incredibly useful.

The Networking Lounge powered by Braindate gives attendees another way to connect intentionally throughout the day.

Schedule a one-on-one conversation. Join a small group. Find someone who wants to talk about the exact challenge you are working through.

Hear a session on product-market fit, then find another founder working through the same issue. Watch the pitch competition, then compare notes with someone preparing for their own fundraise. Hear the Boston Founder Playbook, then meet other founders doing exactly that. Braindate helps turn the content into conversation.

The day wraps up with a closing reception presented by Fidelity Private Shares, giving attendees time to keep the conversations going without rushing to the next session.

That matters because some of the most useful connections at an event happen after you have already shared a room, a question, or a conversation earlier in the day.

Founder Summit is not built around one type of conference experience. The Build Stage gives founders practical lessons they can apply now. The Impact Stage creates room for bigger growth and leadership decisions. The roundtables let you ask questions and go deeper. The live pitch competition puts founder storytelling to the test. Braindate makes networking intentional. And the reception gives those conversations somewhere to continue. That mix is what makes the day valuable.

The Out of Touch Adults' Guide to Kid Culture: What Does 'Code Rot' Mean?

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A weekly deep dive into the current trends, slang, and viral videos of youth culture in terms that even the squarest can understand.

This week's Out-of-Touch guide takes a look at a phenomena that's probably going to define the lives of generations of young people: Code rot—both actual computer-style code rot, and the more elusive cultural kind. I'm also taking a look at the fake controversy over the "cancellation" of Freddy Krueger, and finally, bringing things home with viral videos of boyfriends dancing.

Expect to hear young people talking about code rot for awhile, maybe for the rest of their lives. It's a concept that's going to affect them more than any previous generation. Code rot, or software rot, describes what happens to a computer program when the hardware and operating system it runs on advance. If older programs aren't updated, they become less stable and glitchy until they eventually become unusable.

The idea of code rot has really caught on in the young people's popular imagination, probably because they're going to spend a lot of their lives dealing with it. They've even taken the idea of code rot into life, and use the phrase to describe anything that is worn out or malfunctioning. If the ice cream machine isn't working at McDonald's, it's code rotted. Bad grades on a report card? Code rot.

Kids are making videos where they demonstrate actual code rot:

or point out physical things that are code rotted, like a cat:

Interestingly, a lot of the online discourse around code rotting concerns the massive video game platform Roblox. Because Roblox games there are made by users, there's often not a lot of incentive for the creators to keep the games current with the platform's changes, so a lot of Gen Z's childhood memories of playing games are sinking into code rot. Here's a video about it:

Another interesting wrinkle is the tone these videos take. Code rot is seen as apocalyptic. While it's mostly hyperbole, I think part of it is the realization that the entire world is code rotted. The old programs that used to run within the "Western Culture" operating system no longer apply and everything is starting to glitch out.

The meaning of "PDF file" depends on the context. If you're at work, it refers to a Portable Document Format file, a format created by Adobe that is famously hard for older people to open. In slang, though, PDF file is algo speak for "pedophile." Because that word is flagged by moderation software online, people say "PDF File" to skirt the censorship.

The run-up to Halloween brought a lot of online discourse about Nightmare on Elm Street villain Freddy Krueger. The supernatural serial murder/PDF File was supposedly cancelled online by overly woke, Gen-Z Tiktokers outraged that he is a child predator. The controversy started with a post from Entertainment Industry Reporter that points out how strange it is that in the '80s and '90s, Freddy Krueger, depicted in 1984's A Nightmare on Elm Street as a child murderer so driven by bloodlust he transcended death, was featured on dolls, board games, and lunchboxes aimed at children. It's a good point, but not a call for "cancellation."

Although the article does say that "Freddy Krueger lunchboxes shouldn’t exist," and that parents "shouldn’t have been buying their kids action figures of a child murderer," this is not a call to action. It's not suggesting cancellation of any kind, and it's hard to even conceive of what cancellation of a fictional character in an old horror franchise would even mean. The article is actually an observation about how merchandising (and low-quality sequels) change the meaning of art. But people online missed the point entirely and invented a scenario where "woke" young people are calling for the cancellations of a fictional villain for doing bad things.

I've been scouring TikTok, looking for even one video where someone is calling for "canceling" Freddy Krueger, and I can't find any, but there are hundreds, maybe thousands of videos like this:

In these videos, people point out how enraging it is that the woke people are calling for Krueger's cancellation and vowing that they won't let it happen. Even though it is not happening. There are even levels to the myths about woke mobs canceling Freddy. Supposedly, younger users were unaware that Krueger is depicted as a "PDF file" as well as a murderer in the 2010 reboot of the franchise, so there are online arguments about whether that's the reason he's being canceled. Even though no one is canceling anything. These anti-woke folks are, ironically, defending a fictional villain from cancellation by creating another fictional villain, the shadowy woke mob that's ruining horror movies. Meanwhile, the real villain is a lack of media literacy. Saying "wow, it's crazy that previous generations made a comedy mascot of such a terrible character and marketed him to children" is not the same as cancellation.

This week's viral videos are of boyfriends dancing. The newest thing on the TikTok is young women sharing video of their boyfriends getting down accompanied by a clip from the song "gay alligations fr. my girlfriend" by TikToker OH MICKEY. Like this:

It's a simple, silly template that maybe doesn't mean a whole lot, but this generation of young men are so messed up when it comes to relationships and masculinity, I'm always happy when a meme becomes popular that indicates that young women want goofy dudes, dudes who know how to dance, and dudes who act silly as boyfriends more than they want red-pilled, manosphere, looksmaxxing types. I don't know if these kinds of messages ever really "break through" to the underground of internet incels, but I like to hope they do, because a lot of young men are not OK.

Taika Waititi’s Delusional ‘Klara and the Sun’ Instagram Post Exemplifies the Dangers of a Growing Divide Between Critics and Artists in the Digital Age

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I’ve seen enough: Taika Waititi is no longer riffing on a level that is frickin’ sublime. A quick update for those of you who don’t get your news from DiscussingFilm and/or obsessively listen to “Who? Weekly” in order to keep tabs on Rita Ora: On Saturday afternoon, writer-director Taika Waititi opened Instagram and posted — to his grid! — a less than equanimous reaction to the reviews of his new motion picture “Klara and the Sun,” which was in the process of making less money per screen during its second weekend than the average movie theater employee. It wasn’t the most embarrassing self-own of the pre-midterms weekend (that honor belongs to Michigan congresswoman Hayley Stevens, who committed political suicide on live TV with Anderson Cooper), but I’d argue that Waititi’s faux pas has more to say about how the reality distortion field of today’s media landscape has made it all too easy for highly visible people to misperceive the world around them in a crisis. Related Stories Before He Played Sylvester Stallone in ‘I Play Rocky,’ Star Anthony Ippolito Fought Hard for His Own Big Break ‘Okinawa Saudade’ Review: A Moving Documentary About the Okinawan-Brazilian Community Explores the History of Displacement

“I haven’t read a review of anything aimed at me or my peers in almost 15 years,” the director wrote under an image celebrating the 53-point mismatch between the film’s audience and critics scores on Rotten Tomatoes. “But I do listen to the people, so this Halloween, let’s spare a thought for those sad lost souls who couldn’t achieve their dreams of becoming artists, and thus became critics. The same people who also hated ‘Jojo Rabbit 😂 🤷🏽‍♂️.’ Irrelevant. Out of touch. Obsolete. In the bin.🗑️😢🫶🏽.”

When I saw that his post had been modified, I assumed that Waititi had deleted the part where he wrote “and another thing: I’m not mad. Please don’t put in the newspaper that I got mad.” In truth, he had simply tweaked the bit where it said that “sad lost souls” like me had settled as critics. Good note! Much as fallen angels plummet from the heavens before landing in the depths of Hell to anguish for all eternity, critics don’t settle for a life of press screenings so much as we pathetically free fall into it, plummeting from the sacred highs of making “Thor: Love & Thunder” to the wretched lows of watching it. It’s that natural eye for editing that explains why “Next Goal Wins” felt like it had been cut with a soccer cleat.

Of course, I don’t take any of this too personally. (Don’t you dare fucking put in the newspaper that I got mad.) For one thing, I was actually quite tolerant of “Thor: Love & Thunder,” if only because I believe that casting Christian Bale as a guy named “Gorr the God Butcher” in and of itself makes that movie better than most of what Marvel was pumping out at the time. For another thing, I haven’t even seen “Klara and the Sun” — not because I was busy when it screened here, or because I live in one of the many countries where its release has been canceled on account of the movie’s supposed awfulness and verifiable lack of public interest, but rather because I adore the Kazuo Ishiguro novel on which it’s based, and I was convinced that hiring the director of “Jojo Rabbit” to adapt such a poignant tale of loss and brokenness would be like hiring Peter Farrelly to remake “The Remains of the Day.” Except worse, because I’ve always felt like the Merchant Ivory version of that book could have used a few gratuitous jokes about Anthony Hopkins’ swollen balls.

The truth is that the perceived quality of Waititi’s film is irrelevant to what I found interesting about his Instagram post, in much the same way as its quality is seemingly irrelevant to everything else as well. And in much the same way as the perceived quality of Waititi’s other films is irrelevant to the hissy fit he decided to throw about this one. Are “the same people who also hated ‘Jojo Rabbit’” in the room with us? Because that Oscar-winning movie is sitting at 81 percent on Rotten Tomatoes (Certified Fresh™!). OK sure, I hated it, but, as we’ve previously established, I am quite literally not “the same people” who hated “Klara and the Sun.”

On the other hand, I would very much consider myself to be among the large cohort of critics who adored Waititi’s earlier features like “Hunt for the Wilderpeople,” “What We Do in the Shadows,” and “Eagle vs. Shark.” I suppose I could look up the aggregator metrics for those movies as well, but there isn’t any need — that most of the people reading this article have even heard of such modest Kiwi treasures is testament enough to the groundswell of critical support they received around the world. Perhaps, if he’d read a review in the last 15 years, Waititi might have been able to put that together for himself.

But that’s OK. As a public-facing person whose work offers considerably more fodder for mockery than praise (both by virtue of its nature, and also by that of the je ne sais quoi I bring to the job), I would never sincerely begrudge an artist for not wanting to read their own press. Especially now. Perhaps it used to be possible for celebrities to engage with such things in good faith (it was certainly easier in the days when pans were only found in newspapers, and personal attacks limited to their gossip columns), but the internet is a machine designed to make you hate yourself even more than you hate everyone else, and even our finest “anti-hate satires” have proven incapable of solving the problem.

For some folks, especially those with much greater profiles than my own, the only viable way to navigate the digital world without being fully consumed by self-loathing is to decide that all of the people who snipe at you from the sidelines aren’t really people at all. They’re more like ubiquitous wraiths — eyes looking at you from behind a painting, shadows that your subconscious might warp into an ominous shape, visions in the bog that almost lured Frodo to his death in “The Two Towers.” To let any of it in is to let all of it in, and there’s no amount of fame, money, or adulation that can fully inoculate sensitive people against the poison that seeps into their skin every time they pick up their phone (take it from me, someone who doesn’t have any of those things).

And so critics have gone from being a healthy part of the artistic ecosystem to something that artists feel like they need to be immunized against. Which isn’t to suggest that the animus between artists and critics is anything new. On the contrary, it’s as time-honored a cinematic tradition as popcorn at the movies or, say, the old trope of talented indie filmmakers who completely lose the plot once they become too successful.

If anything, that animus used to be a lot more personal, if only because it was expected that artists would know critics by name. Vincent Gallo famously called Roger Ebert a “fat pig with the physique of a slave trader” after Ebert panned “The Brown Bunny” at Cannes. Uwe Boll offered to box his biggest haters after he got sick of being a critical punching bag, and he beat the absolute shit out of all five of the people who took him up on it. Pauline Kael got so far under Clint Eastwood’s skin that he paid a psychiatrist to figure out what her problem was (the shrink naturally concluded that Kael just wanted to fuck Eastwood), while her review of “Ryan’s Daughter” caused David Lean such a crisis of confidence that he almost retired from directing altogether. And he made “Lawrence of Arabia”! (Which Kael also hated).

I’m not so bold as to presume that I — or any of my contemporaries — have a pen worthy of Kael’s poison or place in the public sphere, but the contrast is telling all the same. Instead of headline-making beefs that stand the test of time, today’s critics might be the subject of a petulant DM or a horde-summoning quote tweet. A prominent action director used to get drunk and send me crude messages about my wife via Instagram, but I think he’s sober now. Ron Perlman once called me a “cunt” on social media because I shat on some Christmas movie he made about furries, but I’d be shocked if he remembered that. Or really if he remembered “Pottersville” at all. I had a funny back-and-forth with a good-natured David Ayer after I trashed his Netflix debacle “Bright”; his promise to put my review on his fridge suggested a much less formal riff on Ridley Scott’s decision to frame Kael’s “Blade Runner” pan and hang it in his office. These days, film critics typically only get put on blast by name, at scale, when they make a major sociopolitical misstep of some kind, such as by arguing that a cartoon about red pandas somehow limits its scope by spotlighting Toronto’s immigrant community, or by fixating on the sexual possibilities that could be explored as a form of Mrs. Incredible’s superpowers. (Pixar movies are just irresistible rakes for critics to step on, I guess).

Today’s filmmakers and stars are certainly still aware of their alleged shooters (bracing for another “Dune” situation, Timothée Chalamet asked me to be kind to “Marty Supreme,” though I’m generally a fan, and he had nothing to fear in that case), but most publicly shared pushback is aimed at “the critics” writ large, if only so we can be reduced to cynical strawmen who exist in contrast to “the fans.” The marketing campaign for “Gotti” argued that critics “put out the hit” on the New York mob classic, and then asked people who they trusted more: themselves or “a troll behind a keyboard.” Ignoring the fact that this troll was behind his keyboard in a fucking tuxedo because that movie premiered as an unofficial selection at Cannes, the poptimistic notion that “real people love things” has become a go-to gameplan for an era where reality is easier to warp than it is to reckon with. In an ironic example of life imitating art, “Digger” recently adopted a similar approach.

I suspect that the systemic diminishment of arts criticism is both a cause and a symptom of this ever-worsening phenomenon. There was a time in this country when arts criticism was valued as a worthwhile pursuit unto itself, as opposed to a wonky and unreliable component of the promotional apparatus. The likes of Gore Vidal, James Baldwin, and Norman Mailer were regular fixtures on late night TV and panel shows; they were as famous as any of the people they wrote about, and when artists pushed back against their critics, it was seen as a debate between equals.

Because of that standing, the brightest minds of their generation were able to thrive as public intellectuals, and even invited to leverage their success into lucrative Hollywood careers of their own (as Vidal and Mailer both did before the resulting feud between them led to Mailer headbutting Vidal in the green room of “The Dick Cavett Show”). Today, the Vidals, Baldwins, and Mailers of the world would never belittle themselves by reviewing “Klara and the Sun” for $150, only for their work to be subsumed into a content aggregator that reduced it to a number in a larger set of numbers. In lieu of meaningful financial and intellectual compensation, any thinkers of such caliber today might understandably find arts criticism unworthy of their time and/or just share it to their Letterboxd for free.

That brings us to the reason why Waititi’s post was so excruciatingly tone-deaf, even though he’s hardly the first director to try the “Am I so out of touch? No, it’s the children who are wrong!” defense. The hierarchy of power in the discourse universe has grown as wildly lop-sided as the hierarchy of power in every other arena of public life. Even the most high-profile of critics are barely scraping by, while anyone successful enough to make news with a “the critics put out the hit” post is likely to be a millionaire several times over.

You’d have to be completely disconnected from reality not to recognize how gauche and myopic it is to call journalists of any kind “obsolete” for speaking truth to power and/or product, but I suppose that’s a side effect of spending 15 years in an ecosystem designed to protect itself at all costs. It’s an ecosystem supported by influencer screenings, celebrity interview podcasts, and so many other quality-laundering tactics that it feels like a desperate and radical act when a star like Tom Cruise dares to sit down with a real magazine writer. Only in an ecosystem like that could someone sharp and humane enough to direct “Boy” and the funny parts of “Thor: Ragnarok” become convinced that everyone who hated “Klara and the Sun” did so out of resentment for their own shortcomings. Only in an ecosystem like that could someone that sharp make a movie like “Klara and the Sun” at all. (Which is not to specifically denigrate the actual “Klara and the Sun,” which again I have not seen.)

Of course, it’s not Waititi’s fault that he’s a product of his time, and in light of Aaron Sorkin’s considerably more sensible and self-effacing response to the reviews for “The Social Reckoning,” it’s worth considering whether his relative grace has something to do with the fact that he first came to success during an earlier iteration of Hollywood, where perspective was easier to come by. Whatever the case, we’re all trying our best to protect ourselves in the face of a world where imitated humanity is of limitless value, and actual humanity is an Achilles’ heel — a world in which one of the great modern authors might be moved to write a fairy tale-like story that questions the distance that has grown between people as a result of our efforts to create external vessels for our souls.

Reading Waititi’s tactless Instagram post back for what I pray is the last time, I’m reminded of a resonant line from Ishiguro’s novel, a line that unsurprisingly failed to make it into Waititi’s adaptation: “What was becoming clear to me was the extent to which humans, in their wish to escape loneliness, made maneuvers that were very complex and hard to fathom.” Perhaps, in some abstract way, Waititi’s Instagram post captured the essence of “Klara and the Sun” better than his film ever could.

Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Switch 2 Cast Features A Returning Voice Actor From The N64 Original

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nintendo has today shared even more insights into the development of Ocarina of Time on Switch 2 in the latest chapters of its Ask the Developer series. One such story is all about the new voice cast, which apparently features some familiar pipes.

Series Producer Eiji Aonuma and game Director HokWai Ng explain that the team didn't want to "mess with established perceptions of how certain characters sound based on the original," so Nintendo conducted blind auditions to really focus on matching the voice to the role.

"We deliberately hid the voice actors’ names during the Japanese auditions to force ourselves to decide based solely on which voices most closely resembled those of the original," Aonuma-san explains.

As luck would have it, such an approach led Nintendo to select an actor who had previously appeared in the N64 original — voicing the same character, no less! "None of us saw that coming," Aonuma-san notes.

In true Nintendo hush-hush fashion, the interview doesn't mention the voice actor or character by name, but there is already a fair amount of speculation out there that Kaori Mizuhashi (the actor who voiced Navi in the original) might be returning to voice the fairy again this time around.

We won't know for sure until we get to play the game ourselves — and even then, we have to imagine that Mizuhashi would only be handling the Japanese version — but what a neat little bit of luck on Nintendo's part. Hey, you're unlikely to find anyone who sounds more like the N64 original.

The first two chapters of this Ask the Developer series revealed the remake's development team and some design sketches alongside a bunch of other interesting info. You can read more about the highlights in our coverage below.

Apple faces unprecedented memory cost increases as RAM prices continue to surge, manufacturing bill climbs by $33 billion — rising component expenses threaten margins through the holiday quarter and into 2027

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI demand is tightening DRAM and NAND supply, pushing up costs for device makers and leaving less memory available for consumer electronics

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Apple is about to feel the squeeze from rising memory prices, with the cost of RAM climbing just as the company heads into its busiest shopping season. According to Reuters, Apple expects to pay more for memory in the December quarter, with the CEO, John Ternus, describing the current price increases as “unprecedented. The company has so far managed to soften the blow by using memory bought at lower prices, cutting costs elsewhere and raising prices on some products. However, it is reported that its manufacturing bill has climbed by $33 billion in just a few months.

But that cushion is getting thinner, and Apple expects memory purchases to become even more expensive into 2027. “What we're seeing with memory pricing right now is unprecedented. And as we said earlier in the September quarter, we did see higher memory costs as compared to the June quarter, which were partly offset by the benefit of carrying inventory and lower costs on certain non-memory components. Now for the December quarter, we expect even higher memory costs, partly offset by a few factors. So as you know, we changed the price on many of our products, including Mac and iPad at the end of June and iPhone in September, and these price increases provided some offset.” Ternus said during Apple’s November 2 earnings call.

Memory is used across Apple's hardware lineup, so sustained price increases could affect manufacturing cost for several product categories—although Apple has not announced another round of price increases. Absorbing higher components could, however, indefinitely become difficult. Dave Nicholson, Chief Research Officer at the Futurum Group, also identified memory prices as a growing pressure point for Apple's margins and hardware pricing through the holiday season and into 2027. According to him, Apple is in a different position from companies pouring hundreds of billions of dollars into AI data center infrastructure. The impact of higher component costs is easier to quantify than the eventual returns on massive AI capital expenditure, he said.

The next pressure point for @Apple may not be AI CapEx, but the rising cost of memory across its hardware portfolio. @TheFuturumGroup Chief Research Officer @DaveN007 explained that higher memory prices could put more pressure on Apple’s margins and pricing through the holiday season and into 2027. Even with demand holding up, the question is how much of those higher costs consumers will absorb. Dave also sees Apple in a stronger position than companies committing hundreds of billions to data center infrastructure, because the impact of higher component costs is easier to quantify than the long-term returns on massive AI CapEx. ▶️ Watch Dave’s analysis of Apple’s margin outlook, memory costs, and why its AI infrastructure strategy could give the company more flexibility. #Apple #AI #Semiconductors #FuturumNovember 3, 2026

And the warnings from Apple's suppliers suggest that the cost pressure is unlikely to ease quickly. Last month, Samsung, a major supplier of RAM(DRAM), NAND flash storage and OLED displays for Apple, said that memory demand would far exceed supply as AI infrastructure continues to expand. Its memory division also reported a quarter-on-quarter increase in DRAM average prices during the third quarter, while NAND prices rose by high-teen percentages. Senior executive vice president of its memory business warned the supply crunch could extend to 2028 and beyond. Elsewhere, SK Hynix delivered a similar warning, saying it had secured long-term agreements covering more than Half of its production capacity for2027 and 2028. Micron, the third major supplier for Apple aside from Samsung and SK Hynix, is also benefiting from the surging demand for memory. Its latest published outlook warns that supply shortages in memory and storage could take “considerable time” to improve.

We have previously reported several signs of the pressure now confronting Apple. In January, the company was already scrambling to secure memory supplies amid strong demand, with then CEO Tim Cook warning that rising prices could have a greater impact on earnings. By June, rising component expenses had translated into higher retail prices. Apple increased prices across several Mac and iPad models, including a $400 increase to the starting price of the MacBook Pro, due to the RAM crisis. In July, Cook described the memory market as a “hundred-year-flood” and said Apple expected to pay more for memory in the September quarter. The company had also increased its inventory to $11.09 billion as it built a buffer against supply and price pressures.

On Election Day, prediction markets show Democrats as heavy favorites to sweep Congress

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

Kalshi traders gave Democrats a 94.1% chance of taking the House and 75% in the Senate in a Monday snapshot before the midterms

Kalshi traders put Democrats' chances of winning the House at 94.1% and of controlling the Senate at 75% in a snapshot taken around 2:11 p.m. ET on Monday, the company said. Voters decide the midterm elections on Tuesday.

An experimental AI-generated summary on Polymarket's site cited a 75.5% trader-implied probability of a full Democratic sweep.

Republicans control both chambers of Congress, Kalshi said. They hold 53 Senate seats, against 45 for Democrats and two independents who caucus with them. Vice President JD Vance holds the tie-breaking vote, so the Democratic-aligned caucus needs a net gain of four seats to reach 51. A party needs 218 seats for a House majority.

Kalshi said polls, forecasts and prediction markets measure different things and none guarantees an outcome.

Polls suggest Democrats are positioned to take the House while the Senate is too close to call, and prediction markets lean even more strongly toward Democrats, according to CNBC. Tobin Marcus, head of U.S. policy and politics at Wolfe Research, told CNBC that his firm believes “polling is likely overstating Democratic strength.” A Wolfe survey of more than 100 institutional investors found no strong positioning toward any specific outcome, CNBC reported.

CNBC also reported that the stock market has not priced in a Democratic sweep. Prediction markets may show "too much confidence in a good night for Democrats," according to MarketWatch.

Kalshi’s Senate odds for Democrats had reached 74% by noon ET on Monday, then the highest on the platform since trading began. That was up about 11 percentage points from about 63% on Oct. 2. Republicans held the advantage on the market from its 2024 opening, and their odds of Senate control peaked at 60% in July before reversing by mid-September.

Polling places close in waves across six time zones on Tuesday. The first closings come at 6 p.m. ET in the Eastern-time parts of Indiana and Kentucky. The last come at midnight ET, when polls close in Hawaii and Alaska.

What Recessionmobile Should Automakers Build?

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Folks, it certainly feels like there's a recession right around the corner. The AI stocks are tumbling, nothing else in the economy is doing anything, we're teetering on the edge of an auto debt crisis — things are bad. But, no matter how broke we all get, we all still have to get to work. That brings us to the topic of today's question: What recession-mobiles should automakers start building?

A good recessionmobile should be practical, reliable, and most importantly economical. Think the Cruze and Focus of the post-recession era, cars that just get you from point A to poitn B without pretending to do anything more. We've all got places to get to, and we all want to do that for as little cash as possible — especially with fuel prices where they are. What should automakers start designing now, so they're ready to hit the market with new cost-effective products when everything hits the fan?

I think, for this try at a recession, we've got to give it up on the whole idea of four-wheeled transportation. We've tried it, and cars are just too much to move around for the one or two people aboard. Plus, when we're all broke, we won't exactly be making Costco hauls — who needs all the cargo space of a Traverse? The Honda Cub is the vehicle we all need, but other companies should try their hand at it too. Give us a Suzuki, a Triumph, a Toyota version of tiny, efficient transportation.

I think more companies should make Super Cubs for the recession, but what do you think? Leave your responses in the comments below, and I'll pick my favorites out later this week. Bonus points for some real in-depth reasoning as to why you think your pick would be the ideal vehicle for getting us all through the next once-in-a-lifetime financial crisis.

U.K. Celebrity Loses Injunction Bid to Prevent Sexual Misconduct Allegations From Being Published, but Will Remain Anonymous Pending Appeal

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An anonymous U.K. celebrity has lost his injunction bid to prevent two news organizations from publishing sexual misconduct allegations against him and therefore revealing his identity.

Earlier this month, it was reported that an anonymous celebrity had applied for a permanent injunction at London’s High Court to try to stop ITN and The Guardian from publishing a story containing allegations that he abused his power to engage in “inappropriate sexual activities with a large number of girls and young women,” according to BBC News. The celebrity denies the allegations. Related Stories Matthew Weiner Exits Peacock, 101 Studios Series 'The Chairman' Starring Eddie Murphy (EXCLUSIVE) Peacock App Launches 'Your Bravoverse,' Featuring a Personalized TikTok-Style Video Feed Hosted by AI-Generated Andy Cohen

Judge Mr. Justice Nicklin rejected the injunction request on Tuesday, as reported by BBC News, but the celebrity will remain anonymous for the time being to allow him a chance to appeal the judgement.

“The claimant’s claim for an injunction was dismissed,” Nicklin said in his ruling. However, he added: “The court did not determine whether the allegations, or any of them, were true, and the dismissal of the claim does not amount to approval of any publication the defendants may decide to make.”

The lawyer for the celebrity told the court earlier that should he lose the injunction and the stories publish, it was “highly likely” he would attempt to commit suicide. According to his lawyer, he has already attempted to do so twice before.

ITN and The Guardian’s lawyer, on the other hand, argued that publishing the allegations and the man’s identity is in the public’s best interest. He said that the news organizations had spoken to over 100 people in their investigation and that many of the women “have been traumatized by the claimant’s alleged conduct and have suffered mental health crises and mental illness as a result,” according to BBC News.

The unusual case has sparked much conversation in the U.K. amid widespread speculation about the celebrity’s identity, as well as concern for potential ramifications on press freedom. on Monday, Variety spoke with defamation lawyer Paul Tweed about the likelihood that the celebrity’s request would be approved due to his mental health.

“The court don’t grant that very lightly — they’ll be very thorough in their examination of whether there is a genuine risk of suicide, or whether that is being used just an excuse, basically,” Tweed said, though he acknowledged that a “threat to life” is one of very few “main grounds that are remaining” for an injunction such as this in today’s world.

Salesforce to buy Cresta and bring its AI voice agents into Service Cloud

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cresta’s AI voice agents answer customer calls for Marriott, Comcast and Alaska Airlines. The deal follows Salesforce’s $5.7bn agreement for the customer messaging firm Braze and its plan to buy Rune.

Salesforce has signed a definitive agreement to buy Cresta, whose voice agents handle complex customer calls from start to finish, Salesforce said on Monday. The deal should close in the first quarter of Salesforce’s fiscal year 2028, subject to regulatory approvals.

Salesforce did not disclose a price. It agreed to pay $2.7bn, according to a person familiar with the deal, as Valida Pau and Kevin McLaughlin reported for The Information. TNW has not independently verified the figure.

Cresta is nine years old and started out at Stanford University’s AI Lab, The Information reported. The deal builds up Salesforce’s customer service business, the outlet wrote.

Cresta calls itself a voice-first operating system for customer experience. Its voice agents hold natural, real-time conversations and handle interruptions, Salesforce said. Marriott, Comcast and Alaska Airlines use them at enterprise scale.

The same platform guides human representatives during live calls and analyses every interaction. It also coaches staff and tests the voice agents, holding both to the same quality standard.

“Voice is where service is hardest to get right, and where it matters most to customers,” said Kishan Chetan, EVP and GM of Agentforce and Service Cloud at Salesforce.

Salesforce plans to join Cresta with Service Cloud, its customer service software. The aim is to link each conversation to the customer’s history and to the outcome, which Salesforce lists as resolution, retention and revenue.

After the deal closes, Cresta will keep supporting the contact centre and telephony platforms its customers use today, Salesforce said.

“Now, by uniting our platform with Salesforce, we will realize our full vision at scale, embedding real-time conversation intelligence and agentic experiences directly into Salesforce’s first-class customer service Platform,” said Ping Wu, chief executive of Cresta.

Cresta follows other recent Salesforce deals. It agreed a $5.7bn Braze deal on 20 October. Days before Cresta, it announced plans to buy Rune, a national security software company.

In September, Salesforce closed its Fin purchase.

Samsung's Galaxy Ring gets FDA approval for advanced sleep apnea detection

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The upgraded feature uses AI to detect the number of breathing disruptions for every hour you sleep.

Samsung's Galaxy Ring now offers more advanced sleep apnea detection following FDA approval of the new feature in July, the company announced. The feature may give users new clues about why they feel exhausted even after sleeping through the night.

Samsung first received so-called De Novo FDA authorization for sleep apnea detection on its Galaxy Watch and Galaxy Ring devices back in February 2024. (De Novo is used for health products or features that have never been on the market before and don't pose any danger to a user's health.) The original approval only covered detection for symptoms of moderate to severe sleep apnea.

The new version does that, but also uses AI to detect the frequency of breathing disruptions for every hour you sleep. Provided you sleep at least five hours for two nights over a ten-day period, Samsung's Health Monitor app can tell you if you have mild, occasional or frequent sleep apnea. Samsung points out that only a physician can do a proper sleep apnea diagnosis, but the device "can lead to productive conversations with healthcare professionals."

Neither of Samsung's primary Galaxy Ring competitors, Oura and Superhuman, offer FDA-approved sleep apnea detection. However, both can track nighttime disturbances that may indicate signs of the condition. Samsung and Oura are currently locked in a legal dispute over smart ring patents, with a preliminary decision expected to be handed down soon.

Along with the Galaxy Ring, the advanced sleep apnea detection feature is now available on the Galaxy Watch 9 with Wear OS 7.0 or later. It will also come to the Galaxy Watch 7 and Galaxy Watch 8 through software updates down the road. If you need further help with your slumber, Samsung recently revamped its Sleep Coaching feature to require less data than before.

Coventry fan, 14, arrested following ‘cruel’ Bradley Lowery taunt in Sunderland match

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A 14-year-old male has been arrested on suspicion of a public order offence and subsequently bailed after a video emerged appearing to show a Coventry fan taunting Sunderland supporters with a photograph of the late Bradley Lowery.

Bradley was a lifelong supporter of Sunderland and his battle with neuroblastoma, a rare form of childhood cancer, touched the hearts of supporters across the footballing world – and beyond – before his tragic death at the age of six in July 2017.

His legacy lives on through the Bradley Lowery Foundation which was established in August later that year and has helped support countless families in raising funds for treatment and equipment which is not readily available through the NHS.

Footage of the alleged incident came to light after Coventry’s 3-1 victory at home to Sunderland in the Premier League on Saturday afternoon.

The five-second clip in question appears to show a young spectator in the home end at the Coventry Building Society Arena grinning while pointing to a picture of Bradley on a mobile phone.

It is not the first time that Bradley’s story has seemingly been hijacked in such sickening circumstances, with Sheffield Wednesday fan Dale Houghton sacked from his job and handed a 12-month suspended prison sentence following a similar incident in 2023.

‘Coventry City FC is aware of footage circulating on social media in connection with an incident during our Premier League fixture against Sunderland,’ a statement from the Sky Blues read on Sunday.

Hands-on with the DockCase Mount Ling 2 Pro — handheld dock with a built-in capture card and space for up to 4TB of NVMe storage

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get started with free access to reviews, badges and discussions.

Unlock exclusive tools and insights for enthusiasts who want more.

Dive into our proprietary testing data and compare hardware with detailed benchmarks.

Track upcoming CPUs, GPUs and tech releases before they arrive.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Mount Ling 2 Pro offers a lot of flexibility for a gaming dock.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

DockCase is no stranger to Kickstarting unique SSD enclosures and docks, with the company’s Visual Smart M.2 enclosure making its way onto our list of the best SSD enclosures. The Mount Ling 2 Pro, however, is gunning for a spot among the best USB-C docks. It’s a dock for handhelds like the Steam Deck and Switch 2, an external enclosure for an NVMe SSD, and a game capture device, all packaged up in a dock that can show off your handheld and expand your port selection.

There are two versions of the dock: the standard Mount Ling 2 and the Pro version. The only difference between them is the internal capture card. The base version is $109 via an early bird deal while the Kickstarter gets underway, and the Pro version is $149. They’ll be priced at $199 and $249, respectively, after the early deals are gone.

It’s the world’s first “smart creator dock,” says DockCase, which is partially true. There are other docks that give you additional inputs, a stand, and capture capabilities with a handheld, and at a lower price. The AVerMedia X’TRA GO, for instance, offers all of that for $160. The differentiator here is the internal M.2 slot that can support up to 4TB of NVMe storage, with points to mount 2280, 2242, and 2230-sized drives. Of course, this external storage only works with platforms that normally support external storage (so, not the Switch or Switch 2).

For PC-adjacent handhelds running Windows or Linux, the Mount Ling 2 Pro is an expensive but useful dock that gives you capture, storage, and port expandability in one attractive package. For the Switch and Switch 2, the usefulness isn’t as clear. You can’t use the SSD with either platform, and Nintendo’s game of whack-a-mole with third-party accessories means a system update could break HDMI passthrough (and, in the process, the capture function of the dock).

DockCase says the Mount Ling 2 Pro supports the Steam Deck, ROG Ally, Switch, Switch 2, and “more,” presumably wrapping up other Windows- or Linux-based handhelds like the ROG Ally X Xbox or Lenovo Legion Go S. Switch and Switch 2 are supported, as well, though getting Nintendo’s handheld to connect properly isn’t always smooth.

The dock connects to your handheld with a USB 3.2 Gen Type-C connection capable of 10 Gbps, and DockCase includes a small, braided 40 Gbps cable in the box, along with two right-angle connectors. That single connection gives you up to 100W through the USB-C port, along with Gigabit Ethernet, an HDMI 2.0 connection (4K@60Hz), a single USB 3.2 Gen 2 Type-A port, and a USB 2.0 port. An additional USB-C connection serves as another USB 3.2 Gen 2 port on the Mount Ling 2, while it becomes the capture card output on the Pro model.

Interestingly, there’s also an optical audio output available. Although I don’t suspect most users will put it to use (HDMI transfers audio, as well), it’s a nice fallback if you plan on using the dock with a more elaborate audio setup. It’s a nice bonus, though I would’ve liked a 3.5mm audio jack as well, considering handhelds no longer have access to that connection.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The dock itself is constructed almost entirely of plastic, and holding it in your hands, you can tell it’s filled with more air than hardware. It’s a sizable package at 8.4 x 7 x 3.5 inches. Outside of the shell, there’s a thick layer of firm silicone to rest your handheld on. There are grooves to provide a natural resting place for just about any handheld, as well as a deep central cut that fits the Switch and Switch 2 specifically.

You can install your SSD at the bottom of the dock, with mounting points for 2280, 2242, and 2230 drives. There's a single 40mm fan internally, as well as venting around the back and side edges, to keep the drive cool. The fan will automatically adjust its speed out of the box, but you’re free to manually adjust the speed or turn the fan off, if you want.

Unfortunately, there’s no room for a heatsink. The face of the drive sits nearly flush against the vented cover on the bottom of the dock. Even if you left the cover off, most heatsinks would push past the rubber feet and prop the dock up.

Setting up the Mount Ling 2 Pro is mostly a matter of plugging everything in and getting on your way. The dock goes through a connecting phase when you hook up a new device, and it’s finicky. Sometimes it would drop one part of the connection, like charging, while picking up another, particularly with the Switch 2. You want to have the dock fully connected before hooking up your host.

The main page of the dock’s screen provides HDMI, SSD, and power info at a glance, which is helpful to quickly check, for example, that the HDMI passthrough is operating at the correct resolution and refresh rate without digging into the settings. A swipe to the right brings up five quick-access settings, which just take you into the settings menu, while a swipe to the right gives you access to volume and fan controls. The volume adjustment on the dock is passed through to the host.

Swiping up instead gives you the full settings menu, which has more to monitor than adjust. You can see the SSD details (including SMART monitors), the status of USB connections, and your HDMI info, andother details about the dock. For settings, there are different HDMI transfer modes, in case you run into a broken handshake, along with power delivery settings if you need to roll back to PD 2.0.

For the dock itself, you have some small control over the fan and built-in lighting. For the fan, you can adjust the speed manually, turn it off, or leave it on the default automatic mode. The lighting settings are surprisingly bare. You can choose between blue, red, or a gradient between them, and that’s it. Otherwise, you can just dim or turn off the lights. If you find the screen and lights distracting, there’s a dedicated button on the back of the dock to turn all the lights off (including the screen).

Although the screen is responsive with minimal lag between swipes and taps, the software running here isn’t perfect. Particularly with the Switch 2, if I connected things in the wrong order, the dock would force itself to reboot, only sometimes picking up the Switch 2 when it came back. DockCase offers firmware updates over USB, and hopefully it gets a patch between units shipped to fix some of these quirks.

The firmware updates are a nice sign, but they bring up an unfortunate reality of creating a device for the Switch 2 without Nintendo’s seal of approval. Both the Switch and Switch 2 are infamously fickle with third-party docks and hubs. A system update could break compatibility, forcing a firmware update.

DockCase plays up Switch 2 support on its product page, and that is a big draw. However, the dock can’t get around the limitations that Nintendo itself has placed on its handheld. You may as well fill the SSD slot with concrete if you’re docking a Switch 2, and docking the device is troublesome. It took multiple emails for me to get the Switch 2 to pass through its HDMI signal at all. You have to connect everything in a specific way, and critically, have the Switch 2 already unlocked when you plug it into the bottom USB-C port; the top port won’t work.

With the Steam Deck, everything was seamless, and although I didn’t have a Windows handheld on hand to try out, I don’t anticipate there will be major issues. DockCase may offer a lot of functionality in a single box with the Mount Ling Pro 2, but it’s all operating over standard connections that are well supported in the Windows ecosystem.

You can use the SSD with the Steam Deck, though you’ll need to head to desktop mode to partition the drive and add a folder for Steam games. The process is similar on Windows handhelds if you’re using some sort of handheld-native overlay.

For the capture portion, it serves its purpose. Critically, DockCase doesn’t have any software for capturing or recording like the mainstream capture cards you’ll find from Elgato and AVerMedia, so you’ll need to use something else like OBS. For any of these types of recording/streaming platforms, the DockCase shows up as a simple video capture device, with resolution and frame rate determined by your settings on the host device (assuming it’s within that 4K60 limit that HDMI 2.0 is capable of).

There isn’t much of a need here for HDMI 2.1, considering the types of devices connecting to the Mount Ling Pro 2, though it would’ve been a nice upside. You can still push the refresh rate higher, just not at 4K. And in fairness to DockCase, the devices it’s targeting really have no business driving a 4K display.

You shouldn’t expect wonders out of SSD performance with the Mount Ling 2 Pro, given that it uses a USB 3.2 Gen 2 connection that’s only capable of up to 10 Gbps. High performance in an external enclosure is the game of Thunderbolt 5 docks with M.2 storage, which can often reach sequential reads above 5,000 MB/s over the 80 Gbps Thunderbolt 5 interface. Here, the connection will bottleneck your speeds, all the way down to typical PCIe 3.0 NVMe SSDs.

Still, in the context of gaming on a handheld, that isn’t the end of the world. It’s nice to have that much storage on tap for a device like the Steam Deck, which is otherwise only expandable with a larger internal SSmicroSDicro SD card.

In CrystalDiskMark, the Mount Ling 2 Pro landed with sequential reads and writes around 820 MB/s, with random performance far lower. We tested with a 2 TB WD_Black SN770, which is a PCIe 4.0 drive that boasts sequential reads up to 5150 MB/s when installed directly in a motherboard. In the Mount Ling 2 Pro, it behaves more like an external SSD like the SK hynix Beetle X31 2TB, naturally hamstrung by the connection.

The Mount Ling 2 Pro does a lot, combining external storage, a capture card, and an attractive dock into a single package that works with both PC handhelds and the Nintendo Switch 2. The SSD performance isn’t great, as is to be expected out of a dock that uses a 10 Gbps USB link for connection, but that downside isn’t much of a concern considering the devices this dock targets.

Although Switch 2 support is a big upside, it’s also fickle. You don’t get access to the SSD, and the connection can fail if you don’t follow the order of operations for connection. The Mount Ling 2 Pro makes sense if you have a Switch 2 and a PC handheld of some kind, but as a pure Switch 2 dock, it doesn’t make much sense. You’d be better off buying an external capture card for use with the official dock.

Jake Roach is the Senior CPU Analyst at Tom’s Hardware, writing reviews, news, and features about the latest consumer and workstation processors.

Behind Election Night Scenes, Fox News Launches New Multimedia Era

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

On Fox News, the stars of Election Night are usually Bret Baier, Martha MacCallum and Bill Hemmer. This year, some of the most important players will rarely be seen on camera.

New control rooms are debuting Tuesday evening at the Fox Corp.-backed outlet that allow producers to move content more easily across its many platforms, whether they be TV, streaming, digital or social. The idea, says Doug Rohrbeck, Fox News’ executive vice president, Washington news and politics, is to allow for quicker distribution of the content and information being collected from reporters and correspondents, rather than having a report earmarked for one particular opportunity, like a segment on a TV program or a video for social media. Related Stories 'Crystal Lake' Hits 1.3 Billion Minutes Watched in 11 Days, Becoming Peacock's No. 4 Scripted Debut Ever Peacock App Launches 'Your Bravoverse,' Featuring a Personalized TikTok-Style Video Feed Hosted by AI-Generated Andy Cohen

“You really want to share reporting and resources across the company,” says Rohrbeck, during a recent interview, in order to “reach more people without duplicating the entire operation.”

Fox News is, like its TV-news brethren, operating at a time when audiences have spread out across a panoply of different viewing windows. Some might watch live TV. Others might stream a show on demand via a streaming outlet like Fox Nation, or jump from one clip to another of similar subjects on the subscription outlet Fox One. Some followers may keep up with the news by keeping track of Fox News correspondents like Trey Yingst as they post videos on social media outlets.

“We don’t want to try to silo the content,” says Rohrbeck. “We want to have it all there and each platform can sort of curate what they think works best for their users. But it’s really sharing all of the reporting, all of the data.”

On Election Night, Fox News will have several different operations going on at once.

Fox News Channel and Fox Business Network will simulcast the company’s signature Election Night coverage, led by Baier and MacCallum, with Trace Gallagher handling overnight hours. Meanwhile, Fox Nation and Fox News Digital will stream “Ruthless Live!” from a Washington D.C. bar with co-hosts Josh Holmes, Michael Duncan, John Ashbrook, and Shashank Tripathi offering perspective on what shapes various state races. Fox News users will for the first time be able to ask an AI-powered chatbot questions about specific races and voting trends and get answers drawn from the outlet’s reporting.

The recalibrated operations nod to the fact that different users are, as “Ruthless” co host Josh Holmes says, “experiencing news and information differently.”

Fox News has in recent years been paying more attention to people who may not watch its TV lineup in day-and-date fashion. The company has spread its resources into books, audio and digital, and launched streaming products built around weather and lifestyle programming, among other niches.

The wider aperture comes as linear TV companies grapple with splintering audiences. On Monday, Fox CEO Lachlan Murdoch acknowledged that Fox News is working its way through some “viewer fatigue” with news that caused a downturn in third quarter and October ratings. Competitors have noticed the trend in recent weeks, which surfaces ahead of midterm elections that polls have suggested are likely to be favorable for Democrats, and not the Republicans who are Fox News’ strongest audience.

Murdoch appeared sanguine about the dynamic, noting that Fox News executives have a “playbook” and know how to navigate the trend. They did it in the lead-up to the 2006 midterms, when President George W. Bush was an incumbent Republican whose approval ratings were in decline. Even so, news aficionados have never had so many competing options offering high-quality video and information.

The news audience may be spread out, but many different parts of Fox News are working to corral them. “We take all the different divisions of our company into this,” says Bill Hemmer. “You will see me on TV, but we’ve got a politics team, a graphics team. We’ve got an augmented reality team.” They’re doing what any modern news organization has to do in the streaming era — placing equal importance on many different media types.

Hedera’s Banking Push Leaves HBAR Waiting For Demand

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home » Trendsetter News » Hedera’s Banking Push Leaves HBAR Waiting For Demand

Hedera’s banking integrations are expanding, but a rapid demand rise for HBAR remains unproven.

Hedera collects only a few hundred dollars in network fees on a typical day despite HBAR’s multibillion-dollar valuation, according to an analyst examining the gap between institutional adoption and token demand.

Fire Hustle’s central question is whether banks using Hedera will need meaningful HBAR holdings – or simply benefit from its inexpensive infrastructure.

The analyst points to Taurus integrating Hedera into a platform serving more than 40 banks and regulated institutions. That gives those clients access to HBAR custody, staking and token issuance, but access is not evidence that banks have launched products or accumulated tokens.

Hedera denominates transaction fees in dollars and collects payment in HBAR. When the token’s price rises, fewer tokens are needed to cover the same dollar-denominated charge.

That makes costs predictable for businesses. It also weakens the assumption that rising transaction counts must produce proportionately stronger token demand, particularly when fees remain small.

“Who has to hold HBAR and how much,” the analyst asks. The video identifies staking and operational token inventories as possible demand sources, although it does not establish how much HBAR institutions would need to retain.

The infrastructure argument centers on CLPR, which the video describes as technology allowing networks to verify one another’s records cryptographically without an intermediary holding funds.

According to Fire Hustle, Hashgraph contributed it to the Linux Foundation and opened an early-adopter program targeting payments, foreign exchange and collateral movement.

She highlights Swift and Wells Fargo joining the foundation on the same day as the contribution. But the qualification matters: “There’s no official announcement that Swift is using Clipper.” Shared foundation membership does not establish a deployment.

The video also places Hedera among participants in a UK tokenization initiative, citing a live on-chain repo trial targeted for April 2027 and led by BlackRock and NatWest. It does not establish that Hedera or CLPR will power that trial; other named participants include Ripple, Coinbase and Circle.

How to End the Year with Purpose

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 3, 2026 | Reviewed by Abigail Fagan

As the year winds down, we can find ourselves consumed with the busyness of the holiday season, in the thick of responsibility at work and school, and perhaps wondering if we are just spinning our wheels, glancing back to see where the time has gone.

For productivity expert and author Tiago Forte, the Annual Life Review has become an anchor in his yearly rhythm, providing clarity and direction for a life lived with intention. Grab a journal and a pen, a copy of Forte’s latest book, Life in Perspective: The Art and Power of the Annual Life Review, and make deep self-reflection a gift you give to yourself each year.

Q: Share a bit about your background and what inspired you to write Life in Perspective.

Tiago Forte: I've been a content creator, coach, teacher, and author for the last 13 years, focused on helping people become more productive with technology. I decided to write this book because I realized over those years that being productive is not helpful if you don't have a clear sense of where you're going and why.

Modern technology is an incredibly powerful force for getting what you want, but you have to know what you truly want in the first place; otherwise it will just fill your time with distraction and noise. My personal answer to this need for deeper intention has been a practice called the Annual Life Review, which I've followed every year for the last 17 years, and this book is my attempt to make it available to others who feel the same need for clarity and balance in our rapidly changing world.

Q: How has practicing an annual life review impacted your own life? In today’s fast-paced world where many of us are seeking answers online, what is the value of looking within for the validation, direction, and inspiration we need?

Tiago Forte: There are endless sources of external information that we're flooded with every day, all year long: podcasts and videos we consume, the advice of gurus and media personalities, and the digital feeds that seek to dominate our attention on every platform.

The annual life review is about turning down the volume on all those external sources and tuning in to internal ones instead – our inner voice, our intuition, our desires, our dreams. No one else can tell us what is right for us, in our unique life circumstances, or what we need to feel fulfilled and joyful. The only source for those kinds of answers lies inside.

The benefit of finding those answers, which I've experienced again and again, is a sense of deep calm, peace, certainty, and self-confidence that I know where my life and career are headed.

Q: What is the method that you disseminate in this book, and how can readers apply it to their own reflective practice?

Tiago Forte: When I first started conducting annual reviews, there was no method or framework to help me structure them. So my process was haphazard and filled with mistakes and wrong turns. Later on, when I learned that many others also practiced year-end reviews of some kind, there was still no definitive book or method to follow.

Annual Review Course (ARC) is the one I developed over the years, first for myself, and later for the students in my course. It takes you on a learning journey from the past, through the present, and finally to the future, where all the insights and realizations you gleaned from the past and present can inform your future plans.

Q: What role might reading and journaling play in this process of annual life review? How have reading and writing helped you to grow, personally?

Tiago Forte: Reading and journaling play a crucial role in the annual review, alongside many of the most proven practices that many people are already familiar with, like meditation, reflection in nature, visualization, and goal-setting.

What the annual life review does is place all those practices into a framework so they build and compound on each other. This leads to an experience that is more than the sum of its parts – a crescendo of insight and meaning that no single one can produce on its own.

Q: How do you hope Life in Perspective changes readers’ lives for the better?

Tiago Forte: I hope it gives them a counterbalance to the fear, uncertainty, chaos, and mental fragmentation that modern life often creates in our lives. A special time and place that they know they can set aside once per year, or anytime they want to return to themselves, and reliably reach a state of balance and clarity. I hope it introduces them to a wider global community of reviewers, from whom they can learn and be inspired. I hope it becomes a cornerstone of their self-reflection and provides a foundation of intention for years to come.

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

I’m A Parks And Rec Fan, But I’d Never Heard Amy Poehler’s Sweet Take On What Leslie Knope Taught Her

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Amy Poehler Recalled The Moment She Knew It Was Time To Leave SNL: ‘I Was Ready To Go’

SNL Did A Paw Patrol: Special Victims Unit Sketch, And Inde Navarrette Could Not Keep It Together

I Like Netflix's Minimum Wage, But I Wish One Character Got A Bigger Spotlight

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Parks and Recreation has been off the air for over ten years, but I still find myself coming back to the workplace sitcom whenever I need to improve my mood. Pawnee feels like home; the characters always make me laugh, the heartwarming moments always make me cry, and I feel like I walk away from every rewatch having been taught a new life lesson.

Tom and Donna’s “Treat yo self” mantra, Leslie’s endless optimism, and Ron’s quiet way of loving and supporting those around him are just a few of the life lessons I’ve learned as a fan. As fans, we’re always more susceptible to latching on to characters and learning lessons because of how many times we’ll rewatch something; but stars themselves can also learn something from their time on a show. Case in point: Amy Poehler recently opened up about what playing one of the most beloved television characters of all time taught her.

Leslie Knope taught me a lot. Playing her really did improve my life. It made me a much better listener and more engaged person in the world. And it was really nice to come to work and, like, spend the whole day telling everybody how much you love them. I had to have a lot of energy to play her. And she gave me a lot of energy when I played her.

Parks and Rec was full of hilarious characters with big hearts, but it was Leslie who centered them all. Sure, sometimes she tried too hard and failed, but she always did things because she thought it would make a difference for the people of Pawnee. She’s definitely the reason why I always come back to the show and consider it one of the best sitcoms of all time.

To me, Poehler and Knope are basically the same person. Anyone who has ever listened to the Saturday Night Live alum speak over the years can attest to the way she seems to embody the Pawnee native. I’d never considered that she wasn’t always that way, but in listening to her mock job interview for WSJ Magazine, that wasn’t always the case.

That’s not to say Poehler was some terrible person pre-Parks and Recreation; anyone who worked with her on SNL would vouch for her kindheartedness — especially Andy Samberg, who credits Poehler with him pursuing Brooklyn Nine-Nine. There’s no denying she’s become an even better person because of Leslie. As she went on to say:;

So what I like about her and what I try to remember, and why I think I deserve this job, she never thought it was lame to care about things. So when I catch myself not caring about things, I do think about her. Because she wouldn't have that.

Not being afraid to care deeply about things is definitely the biggest message I’ve taken away from watching Parks and Rec, and it’s nice to hear the same is true for Poehler. She’s definitely taken that to heart in how she carries herself on her podcast and even with her charity endeavors, like back in 2020 when the Parks and Rec cast reunited to benefit Feeding America.
