Adobe brings licensed UMG and WMG music to its free Premiere mobile app

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Creators will be able to drop up to 60 seconds of licensed tracks into videos, for personal use only.

Adobe is adding fully licensed music from Universal Music Group and Warner Music Group to Premiere mobile, its free video editing app, the company said on Tuesday.

The deal brings songs by some of the world’s most-streamed artists into the editing process. UMG’s roster includes Taylor Swift and Billie Eilish, while WMG represents artists such as Ed Sheeran and Dua Lipa.

Creators will be able to add up to 60 seconds of a track directly to their videos. They can browse trending songs, preview tracks before selecting them, filter by genre or length, and explore collections curated by the labels. Favourite songs can also be bookmarked for later.

The new features are coming soon, although the licences cover personal, non-commercial use only. Creators who need music for commercial projects will have to turn to Adobe’s Stock Audio library instead.

Adobe is also introducing templates that come with music already paired to the edit. Finished videos can be exported directly to licensed social platforms. The app’s existing tools, including its Enhance Audio noise cleaner and Firefly-powered AI features, will remain available.

“We’re giving creators new ways to build their stories around the songs they love for the first time,” said Bryan Stone, SVP of business development at Universal Music Group.

He added that the agreement gives the label’s artists and songwriters another way to reach new listeners.

“Our partnership with Adobe Premiere mobile integrates WMG’s catalog directly into the creative process,” said Rachel Scarpati, VP of digital strategy and business development at Warner Music Group.

The way people engage with music now extends well beyond traditional listening platforms, she added.

The labels have had a more difficult relationship with some creator platforms. In 2024, UMG withdrew its catalogue from TikTok for several months during a dispute over royalties, before the two sides reached a new agreement.

This deal gives the major labels another route into short-form video, with licensing built into the editing process rather than handled only after a video reaches platforms such as TikTok or Instagram Reels.

It adds to the industry’s growing number of licensing agreements, which now span AI startups and creator apps.

Adobe launched Premiere mobile as a free iPhone app in September 2025. It brings a streamlined version of the company’s desktop editing tools to mobile devices, with projects transferable to Premiere Pro on desktop. The app is free to download, although some AI features require paid credits.

Adobe has not disclosed how artists and labels will be compensated for the music’s use, or when the new features will become available. The company said only that the rollout is coming soon, and that people who install the app now will be among the first to receive them.

The Criterion Collection Unveils ‘KPop Demon Hunters’ Edition: Here’s Where to Shop the 4K Blu-ray & DVD Online

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The new edition marks the first time the hit animated film has been available to watch outside of Netflix — save for a brief theatrical run.

All products and services featured are independently chosen by editors. However, Billboard may receive a commission on orders placed through its retail links, and the retailer may receive certain auditable data for accounting purposes.

More than a year after it was released on Netflix, KPop Demon Hunters is finally getting a physical release – and it’s from the Criterion Collection.

The hit animated musical is getting the Criterion Collection treatment with loads of bonus features, special artwork and other goodies. It’s set for release on Tuesday (Nov. 3), while it’s available on DVD, Blu-ray and 4K Ultra HD with prices starting at $20.99 from Barnes & Noble. This marks the first time the film has been available for viewing outside of streaming, save for a brief theatrical run last summer and a second run of a “sing-along” version last fall. The 4K Ultra HD release, which is one sale for $34.99 (was $49.99) at B&N, comes in a three-disc set with artwork from Ami Thompson. The first disc feature a new directors-approved 4K digital master of KPop Demon Hunters presented in Dolby Vision HDR with a Dolby Atmos soundtrack, while the new two discs are Blu-rays packed with loads of bonus features, including new introduction by Maggie Kang and Chris Appelhans (the directors), making-of documentary, video essays, lyric videos, art book and more. There’s even an essay included by culture writer and critic Karen Han.

Please note: The DVD and Blu-ray editions of KPop Demon Hunters also come with bonus features.

As the distribution company states on its website, Criterion is dedicated to promoting a film “as its maker would want it seen, in state-of-the-art restorations” and with “special features designed to encourage repeated watching and deepen the viewer’s appreciation of the art of film.”

Worth noting: Netflix films almost never get a physical release, let alone a Criterion Collection edition, so this is a big deal for fans and collectors alike. Already the most-viewed original title in Netflix history, KPop Demon Hunters, took home two Oscars, for Best Animated Feature, and Best Original Song for the hit single, “Golden.”

In the meantime, since the Criterion Collection title is also from Barnes & Noble, you can get your order shipped to you for free, if your cart is over $60, or if you’re a member of the retailer’s membership program, $39.99 per year (about $3.33 per month). Premium membership gets you a wide range of benefits, such as free shipping on all orders, early access to special editions and events, an additional 10% off at checkout and more. You can even get a free tote bag (a $24.99 value) with membership.

Meanwhile, if you live near a B&N location, you can order online and opt to pick up your goods in store for free. Learn more about B&N membership and rewards here.

Starting at $20.99 (was $29.99), KPop Demon Hunters is available from the Criterion Collection is available across retailers, like B&N, Critieron.com and Amazon, on DVD, Blu-ray and 4K Ultra HD. It drops on Tuesday (Nov. 3).

Journify raises $12M Series A amid 5x growth

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Investors back Journify’s push into the US and Europe

Journify co-founders Taoufik El Jamali, Amine Chouki and Omar Al Shoubaki

San Francisco, California, USA, November 3rd, 2026, TechnologyWire

Journify, the ad signal infrastructure company, today announced a $12 million Series A at four times its previous valuation. Al Mada Ventures led the round, co-led by returning investor Silicon Badia, with Shorooq and Bunat Ventures increasing their investment and Du Ventures and Oraseya Capital joining as new investors. The funding will take Journify into the US and Europe and expand the AI behind its platform.

Global ad spend will exceed $1 trillion in 2026, with algorithms expected to place three quarters by 2028, according to dentsu. These systems learn from the customer outcomes brands send back. Browser restrictions, broken tracking, and disconnected offline sales leave them with an incomplete picture of who buys.

Journify makes sure ad platforms such as Google, Meta, TikTok, Snap and Amazon Ads see the purchases, sign-ups and store sales a brand’s customers actually make. More than 100 businesses use Journify today, from fast-growing ecommerce brands to some of the region’s largest retailers, representing hundreds of millions of dollars in annual ad spend.

Customers include Jarir Bookstore, one of Saudi Arabia’s largest retailers, which increased its return on ad spend on Meta by 182%, and Saudi ecommerce brand Baytonia, which grew its return on ad spend on TikTok by 80% while cutting cost per purchase by 44%.

“AI is transforming advertising, but the intelligence behind it is only as good as the signals it receives,” said Taoufik El Jamali, Co-Founder and CEO of Journify. “Businesses know who buys, who comes back and what creates lasting customer value. Too little of that knowledge reaches the ad platforms driving their growth. Our vision is to close that gap, turning real customer outcomes into intelligence ad platforms can learn from. We’re building toward a future where advertising AI understands what makes a customer valuable to each business, not just what makes them click.”

“As AI takes on a greater role in marketing, we believe the connection between reliable customer data and the decisions built on it will become increasingly valuable,” said Omar Laalej, Managing Director at Al Mada Ventures. “What interests us about Journify is its approach to building that connection from the data layer up: a privacy-first foundation that can evolve into intelligence and action. The founders’ understanding of that opportunity gives us confidence in their ability to build a platform that marketers can increasingly rely on, both to understand their customers and to act on that understanding.”

Hossam Shafick, Partner at Silicon Badia, Seed Lead and returning investor, added, “Since our initial investment, Journify has grown its valuation by more than 4x, and its revenues more than 5x, validating both the strength of its technology and the team’s ability to execute at scale. That performance has deepened our conviction that Journify can become a global leader in ad signal infrastructure. We are proud to double down and co-lead this round as the company expands into the world’s largest advertising markets.”

Journify already uses AI to monitor every connection continuously, detect signal-quality issues and fix them. The new funding will grow those capabilities as Journify expands beyond the GCC. In the US alone, digital advertising revenue reached $294.6 billion in 2025, according to IAB and PwC.

“I’m excited to help Journify scale in the biggest ad market in the world,” said Ryan Burke, a newly appointed member of Journify’s board and three-time Chief Revenue Officer. “I’ve known Taoufik since our InVision days, and I’ve seen his product execution in action. Based on the success they’re already seeing in the Middle East, it’s not a question whether it works in the US, but how fast we can scale.”

Journify builds ad signal infrastructure for performance marketers. Its privacy-first, real-time data layer helps brands improve the quality and reliability of the first-party signals used in advertising and measurement.

Journify connects data across web, mobile, offline sales, CRM, ecommerce platforms, backend systems and data warehouses. It validates and enriches conversion events and activates consented signals server-side across Google, Meta, TikTok, Snap, Amazon Ads and other platforms.

Founded in 2022 by Taoufik El Jamali, Amine Chouki and Omar Al Shoubaki, Journify is headquartered in San Francisco with offices in Dubai and Riyadh. Learn more at journify.io.

Journify has obtained a SOC 2 Type II report and is committed to GDPR compliance.

Head of Marketing Vanessa Moreno Journify vanessa.moreno@journify.io

New iPad mini hands-on: Refreshed design, OLED display, more

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Apple’s smallest iPad has gotten its biggest update in years. The new iPad mini features a much thinner and lighter design, a larger OLED display, water resistance, and the powerful A20 Pro chip.

I’ve been testing the new iPad mini, and there’s a lot to like — but also a couple of frustrations. Here’s everything you need to know.

The new iPad mini lives up to the “mini” name even more than its predecessor. It’s significantly thinner, down from 6.3mm on the previous iPad mini to 5.4mm on the new model. In fact, that puts it very close to the ultra-thin iPad Pro and the unfolded iPhone Duo. Here’s how it compares:

The new iPad mini is also significantly lighter, dropping from 293 grams to 255 grams. That puts it in the realm of the iPhone Duo, for comparison’s sake:

The reduction in weight and thickness was immediately noticeable when I picked up the new iPad mini. It feels significantly nicer to hold, more premium, and less “dense” in my hands.

The new iPad mini features an 8.5-inch screen, a slight increase from the previous generation’s 8.3-inch display. The overall footprint is mostly the same (the new model is actually about 2.2mm narrower). The larger screen is possible because the new iPad mini’s bezels are 24 percent thinner than before.

In addition to allowing a bigger screen in the same form factor, the narrower bezels also modernize the iPad mini’s overall look and feel. The bezels are noticeably slimmer than the iPad Air and base model iPad, and slightly thinner than the iPad Pro.

With smaller bezels, a thinner chassis, and a lighter weight, the new iPad mini feels significantly more premium than before. It reminds me less of the base model iPad or iPad Air and more of the iPad Pro, just shrunken down.

The big news about the display, though, is the switch to OLED. This marks Apple’s first non-Pro iPad to offer an OLED screen. It’s not the fancy tandem OLED display of the iPad Pro, but it’s a huge improvement over the previous model’s LCD panel.

The new iPad mini offers SDR brightness up to 600 nits, up from the previous model’s 500 nits max. The iPad mini also supports HDR content, with up to 1200 nits peak brightness.

The new iPad mini’s screen is undeniably an improvement over its predecessor. All of the inherent benefits of OLED apply here, including better colors, improved contrast, higher brightness, and more.

Unfortunately, the new iPad mini’s screen has one limitation: it doesn’t support ProMotion, or a 120Hz refresh rate of any kind. Instead, it’s still locked to 60Hz. This is an incredibly frustrating decision that I can’t quite wrap my head around.

Apple touts the iPad mini’s OLED screen as offering significantly faster pixel response time than the previous generation’s LCD panel, leading to improvements like smoother scrolling and reduced motion blur.

I’ve noticed that things like swiping between Home Screen pages, scrolling social media, and system animations are noticeably smoother. They aren’t, however, as smooth as an Apple device with a ProMotion display, and they’re not a substitute for a higher refresh-rate display.

I’m sure Apple knows the iPad mini’s market, and it seems to have decided not to prioritize ProMotion based on that information. However, as someone who has longed for a so-called “iPad mini Pro” for years, I’m disappointed by the decision. The new iPad mini’s screen is so much better … I just wish Apple had gone that extra step and added ProMotion. We’ve reached a point where ProMotion should be table stakes, especially after Apple brought it to the base iPhone 17 last year.

For an extra $100, you can upgrade the new iPad mini to have a nano-texture display (on the 256GB and 512GB models).

I’m a huge fan of Apple’s nano-texture display and have it on my 14-inch MacBook Pro. On the iPad mini, I think it makes even more sense.

The iPad mini is ultra-portable, and you naturally carry it around and use it in different lighting environments. The nano-texture glass helps reduce reflections and glare in a wide variety of lighting conditions. I also really like the combination of the iPad mini’s book-like size and the nano-texture finish.

There is a slight tradeoff in color vibrancy compared to a glossy OLED screen, as on other Apple devices with nano-texture, but it’s a very minor difference in my experience. The screen still looks fantastic.

One of the more interesting changes with the new iPad mini is something Apple calls the “Acoustic Display.” Instead of traditional speakers with visible grilles, Apple has developed a new system that uses the display itself to produce sound.

Essentially, Apple has designed special woofers that vibrate the display to produce low and midrange frequencies. For higher frequencies, custom tweeters are hidden between the glass and aluminum enclosure. Apple says this new system delivers louder audio, “punchier” bass, and clearer highs, despite the iPad mini’s significantly thinner design.

In practice, I think the new iPad mini sounds great. I’d choose headphones or an external speaker for serious music or video consumption, but day-to-day the iPad mini’s built-in speakers are perfectly adequate. (I’d bet that this Acoustic Display technology shows up in future iPhones too, and the iPad mini is serving as a test bed of sorts.)

The Acoustic Display also plays an important role in another major upgrade: water and dust resistance. The new iPad mini is IP67 rated, which means it can be immersed in 1 meter of freshwater for up to 30 minutes. It’s the first iPad to have an official water-resistance rating.

Adding water resistance to the iPad mini is particularly notable given its size and portability. I naturally find myself wanting to take it everywhere, and water resistance makes that more feasible.

One use case where all these improvements really come together is reading. The thinner and lighter design, nano-texture glass, and water resistance make the new iPad mini the most compelling iPad yet to serve as an e-reader.

The new iPad mini is powered by Apple’s A20 Pro chip, which I already covered in detail in my iPhone 18 Pro review. The short version: the A20 Pro chip gives you plenty of power for anything you can throw at the iPad mini today.

One detail worth calling out is that Apple uses two different versions of the A20 Pro in the iPad mini. The 128GB and 256GB models feature a five-core CPU and 16-core Neural Engine, while the 512GB model gets a six-core CPU and Dual 16-core Neural Engine. All three configurations feature the same seven-core GPU.

I don’t think anyone should buy the 512GB version of the iPad mini just for those extra cores. You should pick your iPad mini configuration based on how much storage you need, not the version of the A20 Pro chip. You don’t need to worry about performance with the new iPad mini. And perhaps most importantly, the A20 Pro chip ensures the iPad mini has enough headroom for the future.

There’s been a lot of consternation about where the iPad mini fits in a world where the iPhone Duo also now exists. It’s a fair question. After all, when the iPhone Duo is unfolded, the two devices share a similar form factor … but that’s really where the similarities end.

The iPad mini offers the full-fledged, unapologetic iPadOS experience. It supports the full multitasking prowess of an iPad Pro, including windowing and the menu bar. The new iPad mini also supports extended display mode, so you can plug it into an external display and extend your iPad experience there.

The iPad mini also gets iPadOS app layouts, and most importantly, a desktop-class browsing experience in Safari. It’s a remarkably capable device in a remarkably compact form factor. It fits where other iPads can’t, yet it runs the same version of iPadOS as other iPads.

The new iPad mini does all of that, but better than before. The thinner, lighter design paired with smaller bezels gives the new iPad mini a meaningful visual refresh. The added water resistance means you can do even more with the compact and pocketable iPad mini design.

But all of this comes at a higher price of $699. This marks the second price increase for the iPad mini this year. It started the year at $499, rose to $599 as part of Apple’s broader price increases in June, and is now $699 as of this refresh.

The new iPad mini also still starts with 128GB of storage. Increasing the base storage to 256GB would’ve helped ease some of the pain of that price increase. It’s also getting increasingly hard to manage a device with 128GB, particularly with the storage needs of Apple Intelligence and Siri AI. The lack of ProMotion and Face ID also make the price increase sting more.

Ultimately, the iPad mini form factor is a joy to use. The design feels great in the hand, and having the full iPadOS experience in such a compact device remains impressive. It’s not quite the “iPad mini Pro” I’ve always wanted, but it’s big upgrade to a form factor that I’ve always loved.

Van Cleef &amp; Arpels Announces Support for Montecito’s Lotusland

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The jewelry house heads to Santa Barbara with a fitting gift from a brand captivated by botanic motifs.

Above: The Rose Garden Terrace at Lotusland in Montecito, CA.

Ganna Walska was a lifelong collector. First, there were the husbands: six of them, from a Russian baron to a yoga instructor. Then came the jewelry: hundreds of carats of emeralds, diamonds, and sapphires. Finally, there were plants, her love of which ultimately led her to create a 37-acre botanical garden in Montecito, California.

Walska, an opera singer born Hanna Puacz in Poland in 1887, purchased the Cuesta Linda estate in 1941. She intended to both live there and house Tibetan monks, but after her final divorce (and after the monks failed to show), Walska devoted herself to collecting rare plants. She renamed the property Lotusland, for the Indian lotus in one of the ponds, and poured her fortune into the project. In 1971 she auctioned off many of her jewels to fund her gardens. One piece was the Oiseau clip from Van Cleef & Arpels, a special order depicting a bird in flight carrying a 96.62-carat yellow diamond known as the “Walska Briolette.”

Following her death, the property was opened to the public, and now Van Cleef is making a return. The maison has announced its support of Lotusland, focusing in particular on the Rose Garden Terrace. It’s a gift that honors both Walska’s love of the brand and the inspiration that Van Cleef has pulled from nature for a century, from countless floral variations to the butterfly motif. Jewels will once again endow Lotusland—just as Ganna would have wanted.

Four States Are Voting on Abortion Access Today, and Couples Dating Across State Lines Face Tough Questions

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A partner’s politics matter, but so does the state where they live and the medical care its laws allow.

For anyone dating across state lines, checking where your match lives is more important than just calculating your future commute. This Election Day, abortion access is back on the ballot in four states, with voters in Idaho, Nevada, and Virginia considering measures to protect access while Missouri voters are deciding whether to undo a reproductive freedom amendment approved in 2024. These results will shape the health care available to many and revive tricky questions people have to consider when dating across state lines.

A partner’s views on abortion are a dealbreaker for many singles. The Kinsey Institute’s 2025 Singles in America research study found 52 percent of singles outright would not date someone who didn’t support abortion rights, considering it a deal-breaker. But in a world where state lines decide the level of healthcare available, simply sharing the same views on reproductive health with a partner is no longer enough.

Abortion is banned in 13 states, and you can date the most pro-choice person living in one of them without it changing what their local hospital is permitted to do. People in cross-state, long-distance relationships need to consider the laws when making life choices like moving or growing a family.

In a 2024 Institute for Women’s Policy Research poll, nearly 60 percent of parents and people planning to have kids in the next decade said they are worried about access to abortion, OB-GYN services, and prenatal care. Half of adults living in total-abortion ban states said they are concerned about access to fertility treatment. And a 2024 Redfin survey found about three-quarters of Democratic voters want to live somewhere abortion is legal.

The stakes go well beyond individual sentiment. Bans are changing who is even available to provide care at all. In Idaho, for example, after a near-total abortion ban took effect, the state lost more than a third of its practicing obstetricians. In Sandpoint, Idaho, all four of the local hospital's OB-GYNs left and its labor and delivery unit closed, sending pregnant patients more than 40 miles away to the nearest alternative. Nationally, more than 1.7 million women of reproductive age live in a "double desert" with neither abortion access nor maternity care nearby, per that same KFF Health News report.

The pipeline of future doctors in these states is shrinking, too. The Association of American Medical Colleges found OB-GYN residency applicants in states with complete abortion bans fell 10.5 percent in the first cycle after Dobbs (and many doctors tend to end up practicing where they train).

Fertility care is also at risk. In 2024, after Alabama’s Supreme Court ruled that frozen embryos count as children, at least three IVF providers in the state paused treatment. Lawmakers passed a fix weeks later, but the conversation has continued beyond Alabama.

Where someone lives can shape the reproductive care available to them, which makes it access an essential part of the conversation for anyone dating across state lines, not just whose city has a better job market or cheaper rent. For long-distance couples, deciding where to build a life together needs to involve asking the harder questions: If I needed care for a miscarriage, an ectopic pregnancy, or another complication, could I get it? And will there even be a doctor available to treat me?

Today’s election results won’t answer every question long-distance couples face, but they could help determine what care is available in the states where they build their lives. And for anyone dating across state lines, that makes this Election Day personal.

Novee’s External Surface Testing checks which exposed assets attackers can actually exploit

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Artificial intelligence penetration testing startup Novee Cyber Security Ltd. today added External Surface Testing to its platform so its AI-driven attacks can reach a customer’s entire internet-facing footprint.

External Surface Testing is aimed at the scramble after a critical vulnerability is disclosed, when security teams have to work out which exposed assets are affected and whether any can actually be exploited. Novee argues that attackers see a target as a single environment with many ways in, including forgotten subdomains and staging servers someone left exposed.

Conventional external attack surface management products catalog a company’s exposed assets and score them by severity. Netta Rager Dan, vice president of product at Novee, said those products “show you what’s exposed, but don’t give you the full picture of real risk.” In the company’s telling, security staff are left to validate long lists of exposures on their own.

Novee’s version needs nothing more than a domain name to get started. From there the software works outward the way an outside attacker would, and it keeps track of what it learns about each asset and service as it goes. A newly disclosed vulnerability gets run against a customer’s live systems to see whether anyone could actually exploit it.

Findings exported from other vendors’ attack surface tools go through the same check. Novee says the testing also catches flaws nobody had logged, chained attack paths among them, and the customer ends up with a short, prioritized list of validated issues backed by evidence.

Until now Novee’s testing has stopped at the application layer. External Surface Testing runs on the same platform as the company’s Application Pentesting product, so a host found during external discovery can be promoted straight into a full gray-box or white-box pentest. Whatever the discovery phase learned carries over. Novee claims no other fully autonomous AI pentesting platform covers the external attack surface as well as web, mobile and AI applications.

Limits are built in to keep the testing safe. Customers approve the scope before anything runs, and the probes come from outside the perimeter without agents or customer-supplied credentials. Exploit attempts don’t use destructive payloads. Traffic is held under configured caps so testing doesn’t cause load spikes or bury the security operations center in noise.

Former national-level offensive security operators Ido Geffen, Gon Chalamish and Omer Ninburg founded Novee in May 2025. YL Ventures LP, Canaan Partners and Zeev Ventures LP backed the company when it launched in January with $51.5 million in funding.

External Surface Testing is available now as part of the Novee platform.

I just tested the iPad mini 8 — and its OLED panel and thinner design make it one of my favorite tablets ever

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The iPad mini 8 is the most significant upgrade to Apple’s smallest tablet. The thinner, lighter design makes it more portable than ever, while the new 8.5-inch tandem OLED panel delivers stunning image quality for videos and books. Powered by the A20 Pro chip, it also delivers fast performance and more than all-day battery life. Though pricier and missing features like Face ID and a 120Hz refresh rate, it’s a first-rate iPad experience.

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

The iPad mini 8 ($699 to start) is a huge upgrade to Apple’s smallest tablet. It's not only the thinnest and lightest iPad mini ever, but it also packs a stunning OLED panel — a first for the line. The option to upgrade to a nearly glare-free nano-texture display also makes viewing more enjoyable.

The upgrades don’t stop with the design. Thanks to the powerful A20 Pro chip inside, this tiny tablet punches above its weight. This efficient processor also gives the iPad mini 8 astonishing battery life, lasting over 14 hours in our testing. Audio also gets an upgrade, thanks to Audio Display technology, which delivers punchy sound right through the panel.

Though the iPad mini 8 lacks Face ID for easier unlocking and has a 60Hz display, there’s no denying it’s one of the best iPads you can buy. It’s a superb tablet for watching movies, reading books, and even for some light work. I’ve found it a delight to use, and I’m sure you will too. Find out more in my review!

8.5-inch (2316 x 1512) Super Retina XDR display | 60Hz (glossy)

8.5-inch (2316 x 1512) Super Retina XDR display | 60Hz (nano-texture display)

A20 Pro (5-core CPU / 7-core GPU / 16-core Neural Engine)

A20 Pro (6-core CPU / 7-core GPU / 16-core Neural Engine)

Rear cameras: 12MP wide (f/1.8) | Front camera: 12MP Center Stage (ƒ/2.0)

Rear cameras: 12MP wide (f/1.8) | Front camera: 12MP Center Stage (ƒ/2.0)

The iPad mini 8 is a fantastic tablet thanks to its portable design and beautiful OLED panel. If that wasn’t enough, it delivers near iPad Air levels of performance and battery life.

The new iPad mini 8 features a thinner and lighter design than previous models, making it even more portable and easier to hold. Thanks to that, it was easy for me to read books and comics with one hand for much longer. The sturdy and smooth aluminum chassis also feels fantastic in hand. The iPad mini 8 comes in Midnight and Starlight colors, and both look great.

Another significant redesign is that the 12MP front camera is now positioned for landscape mode. This helps keep you centered during video calls or when taking selfies. The rear 12MP camera remains in the same position as before. The same is true for the power button / Touch ID sensor and volume buttons, which are located on the top when held in portrait orientation.

For the first time, the iPad mini features an IP67 rating that helps protect it against water and dust. So if you enjoy reading by the pool or want protection when working outside on a rainy day, you can do so with less stress.

Sign up to the Tom's iGuide weekly newsletter with coverage on everything from product launches to software updates, this is your go-to source for the latest updates on all the best Apple content.

The iPad mini 8’s upgraded display is a marvel to behold. Thanks to the smaller bezels, the screen is now 8.5 inches across instead of 8.3. It also gets a noticeable resolution bump to 2316 x 1512 pixels with 326 pixels-per-inch (ppi). These upgrades alone would have made this a better display, but Apple sweetened things up with an OLED panel.

Using the same tandem OLED technology as the iPad Pro, the iPad mini’s panel delivers excellent contrast between dark and light elements. As a result, colors practically pop off the screen. When watching a nature video featuring a lava flow, I was stunned by how vivid the reds and oranges were, while also marveling at the detail in the surrounding environment.

The review unit Apple sent features a nano-texture display, which does an excellent job of diminishing screen glare without meaningfully sacrificing an image’s vibrancy. A glossy screen would make everything pop a bit more, but I’d rather have less screen glare, so it’s a fair trade-off since you’re not losing much, at least to my eyes.

How did the iPad mini 8’s display fare in our lab testing? Apple said its small tablet can deliver 600 nits of SDR brightness and hit a peak of 1200 nits of HDR brightness. While the former is certainly true, HDR doesn’t quite make the claim. That said, getting a tad over 1000 nits of brightness is fantastic, and is more than double the brightness of the iPad mini. Trust me, this display gets extremely bright.

Color reproduction (sRGB and DCI-P3) is more saturated than before, which accounts for how rich colors appear. That said, hues don’t get too oversaturated and still maintain a lifelike quality. Color accuracy (Delta-E) is also quite strong.

This is the most powerful iPad mini ever, thanks to its powerful A20 Pro chip—the same 2nm processor found in the iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro Max. The Wi-Fi + Cellular model I tested has a 6-core CPU and 7-core GPU, which gives it a bit more oomph. Thanks to this chip, the iPad mini 8 comes close to matching the performance of the M4-powered iPad Air, which proves this tiny tablet punches above its weight.

Even with dozens of open Chrome tabs and a YouTube video running, the iPad mini 8 keeps going with no noticeable slowdown. If you want to get some work done on this tablet, it can handle it. The same goes for gaming: Resident Evil 4 Remake runs at a solid 30 frames per second.

3DMark Wild Life Extreme Unlimited (score/fps)

Our benchmark results confirm my anecdotal testing. As you can see above, the iPad mini 8 blows away its predecessor on the Geekbench CPU benchmarks, turning in significantly higher scores. And while the iPad Air M4 outpaces it, the gap isn’t significantly large.

We see a similar story on the 3DMark benchmark, which tests a device’s GPU performance. The iPad mini 8 not only crushes the iPad mini 7, but it also scores better than the iPad Air M4! Granted, a device’s design plays a role, so it’s not an apples-to-apples comparison (pun intended), but the fact that the iPad mini 8 is even in the same conversation as an iPad Air performance-wise is impressive.

Apple says the iPad mini 8 has up to 10 hours of battery life while surfing the web on Wi-Fi or video playback. However, our testing reveals that the diminutive tablet actually lasts well past that claim.

In the Tom’s Guide battery test, which involves continuous web-surfing over Wi-Fi with the display set to 150 nits of brightness, the iPad mini 8 lasted for an astonishing 14 hours and 24 minutes! That’s nearly four hours longer than the previous model and Apple’s claims.

We typically perform our battery test three times to get an average, but we only had time for a single run for this review. I’ll update this piece after we’ve completed all three runs, but I suspect the final results will be close to what you see above. Basically, this is one of the longest-lasting iPads we’ve ever tested!

The iPad mini 8 ditches a traditional speaker system for what Apple calls an Acoustic Display. As the company explains, specially designed woofers are paired through the display to produce punchier bass and louder volume. Custom tweeters hidden beneath the display and enclosure also help with higher-pitched sounds.

Technical jargon aside, this iPad mini produces fantastic sound that always made my ears sing. Audio from podcasts always sounds sharp and clear, and the same is true for movie trailers. I was particularly impressed when listening to Tommy Johansson’s cover of Black Sabbath’s “Headless Cross,” where the powerful vocals and roaring guitars came through brilliantly.

Though the iPad mini 8 doesn’t get as loud as an iPad Pro, nor does it deliver similarly punchy bass, it’s still one of the best-sounding tablets I’ve ever tested.

Video calls and taking photos are more natural now that the front 12MP Center Stage camera is positioned for landscape orientation. Better yet, the camera now features a wider f/2.0 aperture (up from f/2.4), which helps improve photos taken in dim areas. As you can see above, the front camera does a great job of capturing all the details on my face, clothing, and in the background.

The rear 12MP camera keeps 4K video at up to 60 frames per second, but it also adds Adaptive True Tone flash for taking low-light shots and document scanning. Like the front camera, it does an excellent job of capturing details in photos.

The iPad mini 8 supports iPadOS 27 out of the box. The latest version of the operating system is more iterative than revolutionary, but it delivers strong system refinements and better AI integration, along with a more refined user interface that I quite enjoy.

Arguably the biggest change in iPadOS 27 is the smarter Siri, which now has a dedicated Siri app that works like a standard chatbot. Not only does it understand what you have on the screen, but it can also take actions on your behalf, like creating calendar events. The Liquid Glass UI isn’t as overbearing as before, and you can better fine-tune how it looks.

iPadOS 27 is very AI-centric, so your mileage may vary on how much you’ll use the new Apple Intelligence and Siri AI features. These aren’t things I typically use, but having small things like notification summaries is admittedly useful. Overall, iPadOS 27 is another smooth and stable operating system that works great on the iPad mini 8.

The iPad mini 8 brings some fantastic upgrades, but it’s curiously missing some crucial features.

Face ID is one of my favorite features on Apple, since it makes unlocking devices that support it so much easier. Unfortunately, this is a feature lacking on the iPad mini 8.

It’s a curious omission considering all the upgrades the tablet has already received. I won’t endeavor to guess why Face ID isn’t supported, as there could be any number of reasons. Thankfully, Touch ID is still supported and works as intended.

While the display has received a marvelous upgrade thanks to the OLED panel, larger size, and higher resolution, there is no ProMotion support. Because of that, the refresh rate tops out at 60Hz.

To be fair, that 60Hz refresh rate is extremely smooth, especially for games and videos. However, if you’re used to the 120Hz smoothness of the iPad Pro, then actions like swiping the screen and opening apps will appear choppy in comparison.

That aside, touch controls are as snappy and responsive as ever on the iPad mini. This applies when using your finger and when using accessories like the Apple Pencil Pro. Speaking of the latter, the stylus works marvelously on the iPad mini’s display for taking notes and doodling.

The iPad mini 8 is a phenomenal little tablet that I can’t get enough of using. And that’s saying a lot considering how the iPad Pro is my usual daily driver. I just love how vivid and sharp videos and books look on the tandem OLED panel. Holding the iPad is also nice thanks to its thinner, lighter design.

Even if the iPad mini 8 is now pricier than before, it’s a worthwhile purchase. You won’t find a tablet this small offering the same rich features and delightful experience. It also hints at what’s to come, as I’m sure Apple will incorporate features like Audio Display into future iPads. But for now, this is the most exciting iPad mini ever.

Bambu Lab H3C 3D printer review: A nine-tool monster

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Expert insights and analysis delivered to your inbox.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Bambu Lab H3C is an excellent nine-tool 3D printer that printed everything we could throw at it. Whether you want nine-color PLA models, mix and match engineering filaments, or crazy squishable TPU shoes in a riot of color, this machine is a beast. If you don’t have low ceilings or a fear of step ladders, the H3C might be the only printer you’ll ever need.

Works with multiple materials and nozzle sizes in the same print

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

The Bambu Lab H3C is the printer we’ve been waiting for: a true Bambu tool changer. It has nine “Swaptek” tools that use a technology similar to the H2C Vortek rack, except each nozzle is built into a lightweight, hardwired tool with a dedicated filament path. The H3C is Bambu’s fastest multicolor 3D printer, and though it reduces waste, it does not eliminate it.

The H3C comes with a surprising “old school” technology: the ability to print 2.85mm filament. This is in response to China’s increasing fascination with 3D-printed shoes made with a wide variety of squishy TPU. Bambu sent along a few spools of their new super soft 75A TPU to show off, and it does make for comfy footwear. Each unit comes with one special 2.85mm tool as a standard feature, though you can buy a “normal” 1.75mm tool for $59 if you’re not into the novelty of phat filament.

You will also be able to add cutting, drawing, embossing, and foil transfer tools to the system. These were not available at the time of review, but we’ve seen these accessories on the H2D and A2L. The 3D printer will NOT come with a laser variant.

Bambu is checking off yet another item from the user wish list: the ability to print with more than one nozzle size. Because this is more of a software upgrade, you’ll also gain the ability to mix nozzles with Bambu’s other dual nozzle systems: the H2D, H2C, and X2D. The H2D will get the ability first, and the other printers will be updated by March.

The H3C has a 330 x 320 x 325mm build volume (the same as the H2C) and a massive “AMS” type module perched atop the main unit that holds nine spools. This gives it a somewhat towering mushroom shape that’s over three feet tall. It did not fit on my studio counter, which is a standard-sized IKEA kitchen countertop with a filament rack three feet off the surface. When we parked it on my industrial-grade shelving unit next to the H2D, even my 6’4” husband needed a step stool to load filament. Bambu was prepared for this problem and created a special rolling cart for the machine to make it more accessible. This is the first time the company has offered custom furniture for one of their printers and is an optional purchase that I highly recommend.

All nine tools are integrated into the bottom of the AMS 3H, which means the H3C only comes as a nine-tool 3D printer. There is no such thing as a single-color H3C. If nine colors aren’t enough for you, the printer can be hooked up to as many as four “normal” AMS units with the help of an optional buffer. Unfortunately, all outside AMS units need to run through the 5th tool slot, so you will lose the purge-free perk of the H3C.

The AMS 3H does not have motorized rollers, as retraction is unneeded on a tool changer. Seven of the slots have a TPU bypass, something we first saw on the single spool AMS HT. Two of the slots are nestled within an interior chamber for drying at up to 85°C. Though you can only dry two spools in the AMS 3H, you can run the dryer while printing, as it has its own power supply. The sealed dryer does not “leak” enough heat to dry the rest of the chamber…we checked. But that also means you don’t have to worry about the dryer melting a spool of PLA left in a nearby slot.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The FlexFeed extruder mounted on the toolhead is amazing. It can self-adjust to handle normal 1.75mm PLA or expand to push 2.85mm TPU quickly and without issue. The Bambu Lab H3C comes standard with eight .4mm nozzles for 1.75mm filament and one .4mm nozzle for 2.85mm filament. The nozzles are held in place with three screws and are pretty easy to swap out. You do need an entirely separate tool (with larger openings) for the 2.85mm filament. Because the H3C can print with multiple nozzle sizes, you could leave one or more tools dedicated to .2, .6, or .8 nozzle sizes if that fits your workflow.

For example, slots 6 and 8 are located inside the dryer and have a TPU bypass. You could dedicate a .6mm nozzle to these tools to better handle TPU, something like PA CF, which both benefit from toasty dry filament and an extra-wide nozzle. All nozzles for the H3C are hardened steel (HRC 55+) by default.

My H3C did have some pre-launch issues and is running Beta firmware that was updated several times during the review. The printer developed an annoying clicking sound coming from the left-side stepper motor, and one of the tools has a tendency to tangle and catch on the other Bowden tubes, causing it to dismount unexpectedly – and annoyingly – during long multicolor prints. I printed a few TPU spacers to try and solve this problem without much success as of yet.

The Bambu Lab H3C is launching with a retail price starting at $2,499 for all nine tools (eight standard 1.75mm and one 2.85mm). This is only $100 more than the launch price of the H2C. Additional tools and nozzle sizes will be available, though we do not have all the pricing at this time. A bundle that includes the Bambu Station (rolling cart) starts at $2,799. This makes the machine a bit on the spendy side, but for a helmet-class, honest-to-goodness tool changer with Bambu Lab’s ease of use, this 3D printer has earned its place on our Best 3D Printer of 2026 list.

330 x 320 x 325 mm (12.99 x 12.59 x 12.79 inches)

PLA/TPU/ABS/ASA/PA/PC/PET/PPS/Fiber reinforced (up to 350 degrees)

PEI textured spring steel sheet (120 degrees)

493 x 571 x 1016 mm (19.40 x 22.48 x 40 inches)

The Bambu Lab H3C comes with a toolkit and the cables needed to assemble your 3D printer, as well as a few spare parts. The printer is preloaded with files, including a scraper that you can build yourself with the included parts. There’s also a quick start guide for both the base unit and the AMS 3H.

For the review, Bambu Lab provided spare printer tools for both 1.75mm and 2.85mm filaments, as well as several spools of PLA Palette and 2.85mm TPU 75A. We also received a Bambu Station for the review.

The H3C is an incredible piece of hardware. The Swaptek system has nine tools, each with its own filament path, heater, and hardened steel nozzle. The FlexFeed extruder has cooling and everything else. Each nozzle stays loaded and is preheated for lightning-fast filament swaps. I clocked it at six seconds from printing one color to printing the next.

The tools and nozzles come in both 1.75 mm and 2.85 mm filament sizes. Each tool has a nozzle attached by three screws, which can be swapped with different sizes. The H3C ships with one 2.85mm tool, but you have the option to purchase more tools if you want to customize your configuration. Though not ready at the time of this review, Bambu Lab will be offering tools for cutting, drawing, debossing, and foil transfer at a later date.

The tools dock on a front-mounted rack that retracts, allowing the build plate to be viewed from the front of the printer. This is a not-so-subtle jab at Prusa’s INDX, which has tools mounted to a fixed rack that one must peer through to see the first layer printing. It adds a layer of unneeded complexity, but it also looks pretty cool in action.

The photo below shows all nine tools lowered, using a maintenance setting. Normally, only one tool lowers at a time while in use.

Unlike previous Bambu Lab 3D printers, the AMS is required, not optional. Instead of a box that can be parked here or there, the AMS 3H is designed to screw into the top of the H3C. The plastic sides have a weird organic shape, but are actually quite practical – hidden side panels pop out for maintenance, allowing you to reach into the machine. The massive lid can be propped open, which is good because it’s most likely going to be over your head.

The tools ship attached to the bottom of the AMS. Though the H3C has the same build volume, and the same length and width as the H2 line, it is doubtful that an H2 can be upgraded to an H3C by simply plopping an AMS 3H on top of an older machine.

The AMS 3H has a separate chamber with a magnetically attached lid covering slots 6 and 8, which can dry filament at up to 85°C. All filament slots except 2 and 4 can bypass the AMS feeders through a hole in the bottom to directly feed TPU to the tools. The spool slots are numbered in a zigzag method, with 2,4,6 and 8 on the back row and 1, 3, 5, 7, and 9 on the front. All the filament feeders are in the middle of the two rows, with the even-numbered spools feeding forward like always, and the odd spools feeding backward. It can get a bit complicated if you’re not using Bambu-branded filament with RFID tags. Being on the short side, I have to use a step stool to see the feeders.

The body of the Bambu Lab H3C looks similar to the rest of the H series except for the toolhead. The Flexfeed extruder can adjust automatically to push multiple filament widths and does well with some of the softest TPU I’ve tried.

The H3C has active chamber heating, up to 65°C, goes to 350°C at the hot end and 120°C on the bed, which makes printing PPA and PPS, nylons, ABS, and ASA easy. For the smellier filaments, it has active carbon and HEPA filtration.

The ventilation is sufficient to run lower-temperature filaments like PLA with the printer’s door closed.

The printer has an excellent camera that is used for AI detection on prints and can be monitored from the Bambu Handy app as well as the device page of Bambu Slicer. The camera can also capture time-lapses of prints when a USB stick is inserted in the slot behind the touch screen.

The Bambu Lab H3C has a footprint similar to the other H series printers but does require space on both sides to access the side panels of the AMS 3H. With the top-mounted AMS and the space required for the tool wiring and Bowden tubes, the Bambu Lab H3C requires a total of 1306 mm in height (over four feet) to operate comfortably. When resting on a standard-height counter or the 3ft tall racks we normally use for our printers, I needed a small ladder to reach the rear spools in the AMS 3H. Even my husband, who is 6’4,” had to stretch to reach the back. Shortly after I had the printer set up, I received the Bambu Station, which, at 23.5 inches tall on its wheels, is a great solution for keeping this printer in reach. If you don’t have a shelf or table the height of a coffee table, I recommend getting one.

Bambu is also introducing a companion E1 Air Purifier that syncs with the H3C and activates when you start a print job. We did not receive an E1 to test, and we’re not sure if the air cleaner will be offered as a standalone product. It is part of the premium $3,049 combo that includes the printer, rolling cart, and several spools of filament.

The Bambu Lab H3C arrives in two large assemblies: the main printer and all the things attached to the AMS 3H. After removing the packing material, zip ties holding the tool head in place, four screws holding the bed in place, and two retainers holding the rack in place, the AMS 3H can be hefted on top of the base unit. This is best done while the unit is on the floor or at least somewhere with a bunch of overhead clearance.

The touch screen attaches to the base unit without tools, and the ribbon cable is just long enough to reach the back of the screen. The nine tools have to be manually placed in the front rack, which takes some time until you learn how they attach. At least one needed to be attached once the printer was powered up.

The AMS 3H has its own power cord for the heater and a cable connecting it to the base. There are preinstalled screws to attach the Base unit to the AMS 3H, but I left these unused since removing the front panel requires tipping the AMS 3H back a bit.

The Bambu Lab H3C performs a bed level as part of its initial calibration suite and again before each print. This works flawlessly in practice.

When using non-TPU 1.75mm filament, the AMS 3H loads filament just like a regular AMS. Filament is inserted into the feeder, which automatically pulls it into the toolhead when that tool is selected. The RFID tag on Bambu Lab branded filament will automatically enter the filament type and color. Other filament brands need to be entered manually into the printer via the touchscreen.

The AMS 3H does not have powered rollers to retract the filament. You will need to select Unload from the filament screen, and the printer will cut the filament and retract it from the tool head. You will need to manually pull the filament out the rest of the way.

2.85mm filament and softer TPU will need to be manually fed through a port on the bottom of the AMS 3H. All filament slots except 2 and 4 have this port. The filament bypasses the tubing to feed directly into the tools. The standard Bowden tube must be removed from the tool and inserted into a holder on the side. For 1.75mm TPU, a small piece of Bowden tube is inserted into the top of the tool to help feed the filament correctly. 2.85mm filament can be inserted directly into the tool. Once inserted, filament using the bypass must be loaded manually.

Bambu Studio, based on PrusaSlicer and its forbear, is one of the best slicers out there. Working seamlessly with the printer for filament mapping, control, monitoring, and access to all of the files on Makerworld, it is a joy to use.

The H3C is a much faster machine than its predecessors because it does not need to reel filament back and forth from the AMS. However, it still uses a purge tower, which can waste a bit of time and filament. It only takes about six seconds to swap tools, which is quite remarkable.

The H3C did not come with filament samples, but Bambu Lab did send along several spools of PLA Palette and spools of 2.85 75A TPU to try. You’re going to need a bunch of filament for this beast, so check out our list of best filaments for 3D printing for suggestions.

To get the measure of the Bambu Lab H3C, I loaded up one of my favorite prints, a steampunk dragon by the designer Cinderwing 3D. I had previously printed it in eight PLA colors using two AMS modules on an Anycubic Kobra 3 Max, which is a bed slinger. That first print took 37 hours and 28 minutes and wasted 267g of filament in poop and purge.

Using a .2mm layer height and an average print speed of 140mm/s, the H3C cut that time down to a bit less than 24 hours, and only wasted 68g in its purge tower. The print quality is excellent, and all the colors came out clean. The main color is Prusa Pearl Mouse, Polymaker Panchroma Silver, and black, white, magenta, yellow, and cyan from Bambu’s new Palette PLA, which is actually designed for color mixing.

Eight colors were not enough. I swapped out the 2.85mm tool with the spare 1.75mm tool and printed a nine-color Princess Donut and Mongo from Dungeon Crawler Carl (my latest obsession). I have been using this print to test out other tool changers for a future comparison. The print came out fantastic, with super shiny silks and clean color transitions. I had to “hand paint” this in Bambu Slicer, which took a bit of time. The teeth on the print are a bit shaky, but that is due to the insane amount of support this model required.

This printed in 15 hours and 12 minutes, with a 152.6-gram prime tower, using a .2mm layer height and default settings. For comparison, the same print with the same settings but only 5 colors on the H2C took 24 hours and 44 minutes and used 111.4grams on its prime tower. The Prusa INDX took a similar amount of time (15 hours and 38 minutes) to run the same print in 8 colors, but only needed to waste 24grams of prime purge.

Princess Donut (Cat) Polymaker dual silk sunset gold-red, Polymaker Panchroma Silk Purple, Jessie Elixir Electric Lemonade.

Mongo (Dinosaur): Polymaker silk blue, Polymaker white HT-PLA Pro, Bambu pink PLA basic.

Saddle: Inland brown PLA+, Hobby Lobby PLA wood, Elegoo red silk PLA.

The Bambu Lab H3C is one of the best printers I’ve tested for printing TPU. The AMS 3H bypass ports require a tiny bit of set up, but they are much better than dealing with an external spool holder. With nine slots at my disposal, I can always have a spool of TPU ready to go.

My first TPU print was a strap hinge that combines normal 95A TPU with stiff PLA brackets. The designer wove the two filaments together manually, though you can make your own hinges flexible by using “beam interlocking” in the slicer.

I printed this in Polymaker red PLA and Polymaker red TPU 95A and the hinge is extremely strong. Printed with a .2mm layer height and running at an average speed of 115 mm/s the print completed in just over an hour.

I was excited to try the 2.85mm 75A TPU that Bambu Lab sent, and it did not disappoint. The 2.85mm nozzle comes preset to run filament directly from the bypass and just requires manual loading. This is the softest TPU I’ve ever run, and the FlexFeed extruder handled it perfectly.

I first ran the wrist rest from the printer’s memory using Bambu Lab’s green 75A TPU. The print took 50 minutes to run, used only 20g of filament, and is very squishy. I used the same filament and printed some tires for an RC project I’m working on. Using a .2mm layer height and an average print speed of 75mm/s the tires took 57 minutes per set of two. They look nice and are super squishy. They only have 2 walls and 7% concentric infill, so they are extremely lightweight.

For ABS, I ran a two-color Magic 8-ball-style D20. Dice Studio lets you print custom dice with numbers or text and export it as a 3mf file ready to print. This is printed in Ionized Cobalt Black Matte ABS and Natural Matte ABS from Fusion Filaments. The print was very nice, with just a little roughness on the overhangs on the very bottom of the die, and a little roughness on the text where it becomes very small.

To really test out the heated chamber, I used a filament I struggle with: PA6 GF. I printed a Twist Lock Container using grey PA6 GF from Sunlu. I ran it with 3 walls and a .2mm layer height at an average speed of 140mm/s. The print took nearly 6 hours to complete. The result is a nearly indestructible canister that looks good too.

The H3C is quite the multimaterial powerhouse and handles every material equally well. I don’t own any filament that it can’t print well, from soft TPU to PPS. Multiple nozzle sizes and multiple filament sizes can all be used together, and you’ve got nine places to put it all. Unless you need a print farm, I can’t really see the need for a different printer. This is a one-stop shop.

With a retail price of $2,499 for the H3C Tool Changer, this is a printer to definitely put on your wish list. In fact, I’m so impressed with the tool changer aspect, I keep forgetting that it’s also a helmet-class printer, which makes this machine capable of handling nearly any print you need to create.

If the Bambu Lab H3C seems out of reach, you can also check out last year’s Bambu Lab H2C, which is no slouch at multimaterial and has the same build volume. It’s currently on sale for $1,899 with a four-color AMS combo. The Snapmaker U1 is the most affordable tool changer we like on the market right now, and though it is smaller and only four-color, the $699 Black Friday price tag is very tempting. And of course, we have the Prusa CORE One INDX eight-color machine for those who want to keep their printers off the cloud, currently retailing at $1,949 as a kit.

Denise Bertacchi is a Contributing Writer for Tom’s Hardware US, covering 3D printing. Denise has been crafting with PCs since she discovered Print Shop had clip art on her Apple IIe. She loves reviewing 3D printers because she can mix all her passions: printing, photography, and writing.

Bambu&#8217;s nine-nozzle H3C foretells the future of 3D printing

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Bambu H3C is a tool-changing, color-mixing, nine-nozzle printer that keeps your filament dry too.

The Bambu H3C is a tool-changing, color-mixing, nine-nozzle printer that keeps your filament dry too.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Three years ago, I told you how home 3D printers had evolved for speed, producing parts in minutes that used to take hours or days. Now, the next evolution is here: printers that can swap between materials on the fly, mix different colors to produce nearly the full rainbow, mix soft and hard materials, and even mix different sizes of nozzle and different diameters of filament so you can switch between high speed and fine detail in a single print.

Today, Bambu Lab is announcing its most advanced 3D printer yet, the $2,499 H3C, which can do all of that mixing goodness — by loading nine different rolls of filament into nine different print heads.

Even if you’d never buy a printer this pricy, even if you’ve sworn off Bambu printers for good, the H3C is the perfect vehicle for me to explain where the state of the art in home printing tech is headed.

While the H3C still only prints in one material at any given moment, Bambu’s new “Swaptek” system lets the printer swap in any of the eight other nozzles in six seconds flat. All nine tools are stored in a rack at the front of the printer, where they can independently preheat to the correct temperature right before they’re needed.

With Bambu’s new “PLA Palette” cyan, magenta, yellow, and white filament, I printed a beautiful rainbow bird feather that emulated most of the other colors between those inks. With a mix of flexible TPU and rigid PLA filaments, I was able to print a hard wristwatch housing with an integrated flexible watchband. With an ultra-soft “75A” TPU filament plus a harder 90A variety, I printed a breathable, wearable shoe that… didn’t quite fit, unfortunately. (I’ll measure and try again.)

To be clear, Bambu didn’t invent toolchanger printers or color mixing. You could even argue Bambu’s slightly behind the curve, particularly after the $700-$900 Snapmaker U1 and its four-color, five-second swaps pleasantly surprised the community earlier this year. (Prusa also sells its hotly anticipated 8-tool INDX system for $2,249, though I’ve heard it has some issues.)

But I would have never bought previous toolchangers for myself, because they don’t have a place to keep your filament dry when you’re not printing. (Wet filament is one of the biggest remaining causes of print failures.) The H3C theoretically changes that by storing its nine rolls in an ostensibly airtight auto-loading container called the AMS 3H. Two of those nine slots even have built-in dryers that can hit 85 degrees Celsius and keep on drying your filament while you print, a big step up from Bambu’s previous AMS systems.

I haven’t tried the H3C for long enough to tell you if it’s ready to buy, and I’ve definitely run into some quirks in my first couple weeks of testing. I’ve had to start every single print twice because the printer complains that its “toolhead camera” isn’t initializing properly, I once lost half a day of printing to a false-positive print failure detection, and the beta build of Bambu Studio provided to reviewers initially got confused when I tried to mix colors. (I’m told that last one will be fixed by launch, and Bambu eventually provided some troubleshooting steps for the toolhead camera close to embargo time.)

You also have to load flexible TPU filaments into the H3C manually, and I learned the hard way that not all of the printer’s nine slots can actually accept a roll of TPU without causing trouble. Slot #6 can load standard filaments, but if you load the flexible stuff, another printhead can snag it and cause a nasty jam:

This also isn’t a printer where you can quickly repair a nozzle without tools, or use previous-gen toolless H, P, and A series swappable nozzles. It’s an all-new system that involves some minor disassembly, and I’m not sure about Bambu’s decision to leave filament in the toolheads between prints where it might attract moisture.

But so far, this seems like an easier, more reliable printer than the two-nozzle Bambu H2D was at launch. Other than the Slot #6 TPU jam, which Bambu actually warns reviewers about in a section of the reviewer’s guide that I missed reading, everything I’ve tried to print has come out great so far — and without creating a mountain of little “printer poop” waste when I switch between colors! Dramatically reduced waste is one of the benefits of having extra nozzles.

With any luck, the future of 3D printing won’t just be for hobbyists willing to figure out which colors of filament they need to order before each and every project, or obsessives who store dozens of colors in vacuum-sealed bags. Instead, it should be more like today’s inkjet printers — an entire rainbow of color, ready to go at a moment’s notice. And hopefully without all the inkjet printer shenanigans, of course.

If you like Bambu’s toolchanger system, you probably won’t have to pay $2,499 for long. Certification filing leaks show that the company’s already prepping a smaller “P3C” with seven tools and seven rolls of filament storage instead of nine.

Additionally, Bambu will let all three of its existing dual-nozzle printers print with two different sizes of nozzle by next spring, including the H2D, the H2C, and the X2D.

The 2026 iPad Mini is a terrific tiny tablet — for a big price

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

With better performance and a better screen, this is a Mini to be reckoned with. But it’s not perfect, and it sure isn’t cheap.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

With better performance and a better screen, this is a Mini to be reckoned with. But it’s not perfect, and it sure isn’t cheap.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The iPad Mini sometimes feels like a trap. It is, in some ways and for some people, the perfect iPad: meaningfully larger than my phone’s screen but still small and light enough to use for reading in bed or gaming on the subway. As most of the rest of the iPad line has tilted ever closer to laptop territory, the Mini might be the most iPad-y iPad left. I’ve taken a Mini everywhere with me since the very first one. But Apple only updates the Mini sporadically, and those updates sometimes feel like someone inside Apple Park picked its specs by closing their eyes and clicking randomly in an Excel sheet. The Mini is always a little underpowered, a little overpriced, and a little afield in Apple’s tablet lineup. We, the iPad Mini People, sometimes feel a little forgotten.

This year’s model, though, is an upgrade through and through. Apple made the screen larger and better, while making the iPad itself smaller and lighter. It upgraded the processor in a big way. It made the whole package more durable, so you can take it to more places and use it for more things. It actually feels like a tablet made for a reason, not just another occasional bone thrown to the small-tablet fans.

For the past week, I’ve been testing the midnight iPad Mini with cell service, 512GB of storage, and the nano-texture matte display coating. I really like it. It’s the best iPad Mini yet, and a huge leap over the previous model. The one in my hands also costs $1,249, and I cannot in good conscience tell you to buy it. Even the very cheapest version — Wi-Fi only, 128GB of storage, regular OLED display — starts at $699, by a wide margin the highest starting price ever for a Mini.

If you don’t already know the iPad Mini is your one true love, there’s probably a better iPad out there for you. But if you’re an iPad Mini Person and you can stomach the price? You’re getting the best Mini in years.

OLED display looks greatWaterproof and dustproofA20 Pro chip is a big bump up

The Mini’s new display is its most important and most controversial upgrade. It now measures 8.5 inches instead of 8.3, and it’s now a 2316 x 1512 OLED panel instead of an LCD. The OLED gives the screen a little more dynamic range than the previous model, with brighter bright spots and darker dark spots. Its peak brightness is noticeably brighter than before, and its minimum brightness is low enough to be totally pleasant for reading in the dark (as long as you’re in dark mode). In general, it’s just a little more crisp and contrast-y, in a nice way; the old Mini’s display was the least of my worries, but the OLED is a solid improvement. The tablet I tested also has Apple’s nano-texture matte finish, which seems to dull and darken the screen slightly; on a particularly white background, the whole thing seems to shimmer. I suspect I’d like the OLED panel even more without it.

The nano-texture does, however, do an excellent job of reducing glare. The effect is actually really cool: The textured coating doesn’t extend to the bezels, so you can see a source of light or glare come in from the edge of the screen and then simply disappear as it hits the screen. So what you lose in pure screen fidelity, you gain in the ability to read on the iPad in a coffee shop without it reflecting the overhead lights like a mirror. The nano-texture option costs an extra $100, though, and requires you to upgrade to 256GB of storage, so you’re now looking at a $900 tablet. Spend the extra money on the cellular version or, better yet, don’t spend it at all.

For some reason, Apple has never deigned to put its ProMotion display tech on the iPad Mini, which has a refresh rate capped at 60Hz rather than the 120Hz you’ll get on most other recent Apple devices. This is such an odd spec choice that it has launched a bunch of conspiracy theories. Maybe it’s to make more people buy an iPhone Duo instead? Maybe it’s a 120Hz panel downgraded to 60Hz by software just to make people upgrade again next time? Apple’s answer, to Chris Lawley and others, has been something to the effect of “120Hz is irrelevant, OLED’s faster pixel response solves the problems.” I don’t agree. I don’t think the lack of ProMotion is an immediate and total dealbreaker, but refresh rates matter! To games and high-touch creative apps, of course, but you notice the difference even with basic swiping and zooming. The Apple Pencil feels more juddery moving across my screen. On a 120Hz screen, I can scroll a browser page and still clearly read the text as it’s moving. With 60Hz, it gets blurry. In all these cases, even if you don’t explicitly see the difference, you really do feel it.

The most meaningful upgrade in the new Mini, at least for me and how I use my iPad, is that it is designed to be more or less waterproof and dustproof. Its IP67 rating means that the Mini got the highest possible score for protection from “solid foreign objects,” which for our purposes mostly just means dust won’t get inside, and is also “protected against the effects of temporary immersion in water.” It’s not quite as rugged as, say, the iPhone 18 Pro, and I don’t recommend using it as an underwater camera or anything, but your tablet should be fine as long as you don’t drop it in the tub and leave it for a while.

This increased ruggedness immediately changed my iPad usage in a couple of ways. I became less worried about handing the Mini to my toddler, whose general stickiness and penchant for spilling things no longer feel like threats. I also started using the Mini more in the kitchen — I love to cook with an iPad nearby for recipes and Community episodes, and it was nice not to be constantly guarding against flour explosions and water spills. At one point, the iPad Mini even came with me in the shower during a particularly close football game. It’s still kicking.

This is, for lack of a better word, the Field iPad.

The Mini’s IP rating is also the feature that finally made me see who Apple sees as the ideal iPad Mini user. This is, for lack of a better word, the Field iPad. We hear all the time about pilots who use Minis instead of sheaves of paperwork, but Apple also sees them in use on construction sites, oil rigs, doctor’s offices, and all kinds of other places. (It’s a fun rabbit hole, actually — can I interest you in an “explosion proof” last-gen Mini for $4,500 or so?) Apple’s goal with the Mini is not really to maximize performance, satisfy the pixel-peepers, or enhance creator workflows. It’s durability and usability. Body and screen.

Apple made a couple of changes to the Mini to receive its IP rating, like ditching the speaker holes and opting for what it calls an Acoustic Display. It means the sound fires out of the front panel rather than from speaker holes. (Some TVs currently work the same way.) It doesn’t sound any better than before, maybe a tiny bit bassier, but it also doesn’t sound worse, and if anything it’s slightly better to have the sound actually pointed at you instead of out the sides of the tablet. Mostly it’s a wash in service of the ruggedization, which is good enough for me.

The rest of the hardware is just wins all the way around. The A20 Pro chip, the chipset also on the iPhone 18 Pro (and the Duo), benchmarks about 50 percent faster than the 2024 Mini for standard computing tasks, and about three times as fast for graphics-intensive ones. (The chip in my review unit has one more CPU core than the A20 Pro in the cheaper Minis; those will still be drastic improvements, but maybe a hair less so.) I notice the difference, even side by side with the old model, in how fast apps load and games play. Games like Assassin’s Creed Mirage, which were all but unplayable on the 2024 Mini, work nicely on this one. The body is about a tenth of an inch thinner and about an ounce and a half lighter. Perhaps as a result, the battery is a tiny bit smaller, but battery life remains steadfastly somewhere around nine hours of screen time.

One last hardware thing I should mention: Apple moved the front-facing camera from the portrait side of the Mini to the landscape side. On every other iPad, all of which seem to want to be used horizontally, that’s clearly the right decision. On this one, the only iPad I actually hold vertically for long periods, I’m torn. And in either case, that camera still doesn’t enable FaceID — it’s still TouchID or nothing on the Mini.

I thought the last iPad Mini was a disappointment, a product made from spare parts without any particular reason for existence. This year’s Mini feels mostly like a return to form. It’s powerful enough to be useful for a long time. It’s lighter and thinner than ever. The 60Hz refresh rate is a bummer, but I don’t think it’s a dealbreaker for most people. And it’s really the only downside. Though at this price, should you have to deal with any downsides?

This year’s Mini feels mostly like a return to form

After a run of new models and price increases from Apple, it’s worth level-setting a bit on iPad prices. The cheapest model you can buy is now the base iPad at $449, with an A16 chip (the one from the iPhone 14 Pro) and 128GB of storage. The cheapest iPad Air, with an M4 processor and 128GB of storage, is $749. The Pro has an M5 and 256GB for $1,199. iPads are expensive!

In this context, the new Mini’s $699 starting price feels at least $100 too high — this model has always been more expensive than the base iPad, but unless you really want or need the small screen, $50 more gets you a much better and more versatile iPad Air. As ever, the iPad Mini is really only for iPad Mini People (and pilots and oil riggers, apparently). Someday, Apple will give us the tiny flagship we’ve been waiting for, and I’ll gladly pay Apple’s top dollar for it. This is a very good iPad Mini, but it’s not quite the Mini in its final form.
