iPad mini (A20 Pro) review: Apple's tiny tablet gets a big update

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Apple made significant improvements this year, but they don’t come cheap.

The iPad mini has often gone for years without updates, making it feel like a forgotten part of Apple's tablet lineup. But it's often been sneaky powerful despite its size, usually toting specs and features that make it a more compelling option than the basic iPad, even with its smaller screen. And after two versions of the iPad mini without a home button, Apple has given the tiny tablet a compelling update in almost every facet.

The new iPad mini's screen is slightly larger, but the device is thinner and lighter than the one it replaces. It's the first iPad mini with an OLED display, a major step forward from the standard LCD this model has had for years. And for the first time, you can get a nano-texture glass front for reduced glare in bright light. Naturally, its chipset has been upgraded, as well — the A20 Pro inside it is brand new, just released alongside the iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo. The previous iPad mini made do with the A17 Pro, which was first used in 2023's iPhone 15 Pro series. It has a new speaker system and is the first iPad to have an IP67 rating for dust and water resistance. Finally, it's the second iPad to get fast-charging capabilities, following last year's iPad Pro.

All told, it's a major update to a device that doesn't always get a lot of love. But, of course, these improvements come at a cost. The iPad mini now starts at $699 with 128GB of storage, $200 more than the last iPad mini cost when it was released two years ago (but only $100 more than Apple was selling it for after its round of major price increases this past summer). Between the cost and new set of features, it almost feels like an iPad Pro mini. And while technology-wise it's an impressive device, I'm not sure if the price will be worth it for most.

If I handed you the new iPad mini, you might not immediately notice how different it is compared to the 2024 model. At first glance, it has a similar enough physical impression and display, and its aluminum unibody and glass front are just as solid as ever. This year, you can only get the iPad mini in the all-business "midnight" and "starlight" shades, which is a bit of a bummer. But there are nips and tucks throughout that add up to a refreshed experience. While the iPad mini still has relatively large display bezels, they're noticeably thinner than before thanks to the 8.5-inch screen Apple included (compared to the 8.3 inches on the prior mini). Apple also reduced its thickness from 6.3mm to 5.4mm, and its weight from 0.65 pounds to 0.56 pounds (or 0.66 to 0.58 for the cellular-enabled models). More screen in a thinner and lighter package is always a win, and Apple has come the closest it ever has to making an iPad that you can truly carry with you everywhere without thinking about it.

While those physical tweaks are small but welcome, the new display tech is more significant. The iPad mini's OLED screen offers twice the peak brightness when viewing HDR content at 1,200 nits, while SDR brightness has been slightly increased from 500 to 600 nits. It can also go all the way down to 1 nit minimum brightness. And thanks to OLED, blacks look exceptionally black and overall contrast is excellent. It has a 2,000,000:1 contrast ratio, which far surpasses what you'll get on an LCD like the older iPad mini used (Apple doesn't specifically list the contrast ratio for older iPad mini models, but its recent LCD screens are in the 1000:1 range).

What this means in practice is that the iPad mini is an even better portable cinema than ever. If you're going to use this tablet to watch movies on the plane, it's a notable upgrade. It's not the most technologically advanced OLED screen you've ever seen, but its dark blacks, HDR performance and excellent overall contrast makes even dimly-lit scenes much sharper than what you'll get on an LCD-equipped iPad mini. You'll notice this the most when watching video, but it makes everything you do with the iPad mini, whether that's playing games or just browsing the internet, look much more visually appealing.

One place where I will ding this screen is its refresh rate: Apple stuck with 60Hz here, compared to the 120Hz "ProMotion" screen on the iPad Pro. It's pretty evident at this point that a high refresh rate screen is going to be limited to the iPad Pro, but it's still a bit of a let-down — especially when you consider the fact that the iPhone 17 got a ProMotion screen in 2025. That said, the smartphone market is far more competitive than the tablet market, so while Apple may have felt the pressure to finally put a high refresh rate screen in the standard iPhone, that still isn't the case on the iPad.

The iPad mini I tested also has a nano-texture glass front. The timing for trying this was impeccable, as I just reviewed Amazon's new Alexa Tablet 12 Pro, which also has a "nanomatte" display that was lovely to use. The etching on the glass front scatters reflections in a way that makes the iPad mini much easier to use when there's a bright light source nearby. And while the Alexa Tablet sticks with an LCD, the combo of OLED and the nano-texture glass made for an exceptional viewing experience here.

As you can see below, the new iPad mini with its nano-texture glass disperses strong light, while the one on the right with standard glass is a mess of reflections as the sun was setting.

As is typical, but still unfortunate, the nano-texture option costs a pretty penny. It's not an option on the base iPad mini with 128GB of storage, so you'll need to spend at least $899 to get the 256GB model with the nano-texture glass (or $1,049 for 256GB, nano-texture and 5G connectivity). That's a lot of cash for an iPad mini, no matter how nice it is.

Other subtle tweaks include the new "Acoustic Display," which Apple says places tweeter and woofers beneath the display enclosure for improved audio performance. This also allowed Apple to remove the speaker holes on either side of the iPad mini so that it could achieve that IP67 dust- and water-resistance rating.

I remember being surprised at the iPad mini's audio performance when I reviewed it in 2024 — it wasn't something I wanted to listen to for long periods of time, but it was definitely better than I expected given the small space Apple has to work with. Well, the new Acoustic Display speakers are another step forward, with a much stronger bass presence and overall more pleasing audio profile. The older iPad mini now sounds positively tinny by comparison. It's not the kind of thing that's going to radically change how you use the device, but it's a nice quality of life improvement.

The same can be said for the front-facing camera, which is now located on the landscape edge of the iPad mini. It's the same 12-megapixel Center Stage camera that most iPads use, but at least if you use a case like Apple's Smart Folio, the iPad mini's camera will be properly lined up for a video call. Like all other iPads except the Pro, though, that camera doesn't support Face ID. You'll need to use the Touch ID power button (or a numerical PIN) to unlock the iPad mini.

Given that the iPad mini has a brand-new A20 Pro chip inside, it's unsurprisingly speedy and handled every task I threw at it. Games like the recently-released ports of Control and No Man's Sky ran with no hitches and looked great while doing so. It's not the same fidelity you'll get from playing on a high-end PC rig or even a PS5, but having games of this caliber on an iPad is pretty great. The iPad mini naturally had no problems with lower-stress fare including games from Apple Arcade like Mini Motorways What the Car? and my former iPad mini fav Balatro.

But performance on an iPad hasn't really been an issue unless you're really finding ways to stress the system, and nothing that I wanted to do with the iPad mini made it break a sweat. For me, it's a fairly casual couch computer for browsing and messaging, triaging emails, playing games or watching videos — all lightweight tasks. It definitely has the headroom to do more, though, and if you're paying at least $700 for one now, it's good that it has a chip that'll run well for years to come.

The one bit of higher-load work I did was in the Lightroom app. I loaded up my library, which has a ton of RAW photos, and the iPad mini applied any changes I wanted near-instantly. It took a few seconds for Lightroom's AI-powered Quick Actions tool to analyze a photo, but once that initial scan was done, any adjustments were rendered basically in real time.

It is worth noting that Apple offers a few variants of the A20 Pro chip, depending on how much storage you opt for. The 128GB and 256GB models have a 5-core CPU, 7-core GPU and 16-core Neural Engine for AI processing. However, the 512GB model (which I tested) steps up to a 6-core CPU and, more crucially, a dual 16-core Neural Engine. Apple specifically says this will help speed up on-device AI performance. I wasn't able to compare the two different models, but I'm confident in saying that most iPad mini shoppers will be fine with the standard configuration.

The new iPad mini is the first with Apple's own networking chips on board, as well. The N1 handles Wi-Fi 7, Bluetooth 6 and Thread. It was first introduced last year with the iPhone 17 series and has since arrived in a variety of new iPads and Macs. The model I tested also has the C2, Apple's new cellular modem that first showed up on the iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo in September. It came with Verizon's service activated, and was extremely fast when connected to the carrier's 5G Ultra Wideband network — I routinely got download speeds exceeding 500 Mbps. When you combine that with other refinements like the nano-texture glass and its smaller profile, a cellular-connected iPad mini can really go anywhere without adding any noticeable weight to your bag.

I haven't had time yet to do a full battery test on the iPad mini, but in-use over the last five days it feels very similar to basically every other iPad I've used in recent years. I only got around five or six hours of battery life when I was using it heavily for things like games, but lower-intensity tasks like watching video or internet browsing extended things closer to the 10 hours Apple promises. I played Control for about 30 minutes and the battery dropped by 11 percent, which is pretty good for one of the heavier games you can play on an iPad. I plan to loop video playback on the iPad mini until it dies and will update this review with those full results when it is complete.

In another feature it borrows from the iPad Pro, the new iPad mini has fast charging. Provided you've got the right adapter (like Apple's 40W dynamic power adapter that can hit up to 60W), you can charge to about 50 percent in 30 minutes. That almost matches up with my testing: When I plugged the iPad mini in at 29 percent, it was up to 74 percent in a half-hour.

Unlike the 2024 iPad mini update, this model has several significant upgrades beyond a more powerful chip. It now has the second-best display of any iPad available, assuming you're okay with its compact size. The size and weight reductions, the slightly larger screen and the improved speakers weren't necessary, but they are welcome. And the nano-texture glass is a wonderful option that makes it much easier to use the iPad mini in a wide variety of lighting conditions.

But the problem, as is so often the case in 2026, is the price. The 2024 iPad mini cost $599 after the recent increase, and this new one starts at $699. I think all the updates on board here — the screen, the A20 Pro and a tighter design — make a $100 price bump reasonable. But it's fair to wonder if Apple is misreading what people want out of an iPad mini. Would it have been more appealing if they stuck with the older design and screen, put in a new chip and kept the price the same? It's hard to say, as the iPad mini has never been positioned as Apple's most affordable device — instead, it's more of a small iPad Air. From that perspective, the pricing makes sense.

The iPad mini really is a fun and adorable device to use. If I had any talent for visual arts, it would be a perfect digital sketchbook and jack-of-all-trades device for consuming content or doing basic tasks. But in 2026, with consumer electronics prices continuing to surge, a more expensive iPad mini might be an even more niche device than it was before.

U.S.-Based Japanese Music Company Nebula17 Expands Team & Roster: ‘J-Pop Feels Ready for Its Moment’

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The L.A.-based company was founded by former Avex USA CEO Naoki Osada, who says he's seeing major "momentum" for J-pop in the U.S.

Nebula17, the L.A.-based music and creative company founded by former Avex USA CEO Naoki Osada, has expanded its executive team, publishing roster and label operations and is beefing up its initiatives to connect Japanese and U.S. artists, the company tells Billboard.

The new hires include Daniel Hong, who has joined Nebula17 as COO following stints as CEO at YG USA and COO at JYP USA; Martha Blair Reynolds, who joined as senior manager of marketing following her work at Ricky Reed‘s Nice Life Recording Company; René Mark, joining as director of digital following previous roles at Interscope Geffen A&M and Life Life; Natalie Walsh, who will serve as director of A&R after working with artists including Sabrina Claudio and Nasri; Harukaze Wakabayashi, formerly of Warner Music Japan, who joined as senior manager of international; Zac Cruz, previously of Avex and Revels Group, as A&R/marketing manager; and Oscar Cornejo, who has worked with Tyla and Ye (formerly Kanye West), as head engineer.

Nebula17 has also signed three to its U.S. publishing roster. They include producer, musician and sound designer Oh Gosh Leotus (Doechii, SZA, Mariah the Scientist); artist, songwriter and producer Hydra (Sango, GoldLink, ELHAE); and producer Xansei (XG, NLE Choppa, Sabrina Claudio). On the label side, the company inked New York-based artist and producer Hojean, who blends indie pop and alt-R&B; XAMIYA, a duo consisting of Sansei and Kamiya that was selected for this year’s Spotify’s Fresh Finds program; and artist, producer and creative director Brent de la Cruz.

For distribution, Nebula17 has struck a global partnership with Sony Music’s The Orchard, giving its artists and partners in Japan greater access to U.S. and international streaming platforms. To foster connections between Japanese and U.S. creators, the company will also be using its West Hollywood-based studio house as a hub for collaborations and writing camps.

Nebula17 is currently spearheading A&R initiatives for the U.S. launch of Japanese boy band BE:FIRST, whose first global EP, WATCH ME, debuted at No. 130 on the Billboard 200. Other recent Nebula17 projects include Japanese hip-hop artist Awich, girl group HANA and Tuber and artist Hoshimachi Suisei’s single “GUM & DROP,” which was co-written by Sabrina Claudio and co-produced by Xansei.

The expansion announced Monday is part of Nebula17’s goal to make J-pop more of a going concern in the U.S. market, similar to how K-pop gained a foothold before exploding in popularity with bands like BTS and BLACKPINK. Speaking with Billboard, Osada says Hong, the company’s new COO, brings “tangible experience” helping break K-pop acts in the U.S. — a valuable skill set as Nebula17 tries to achieve the same kind of breakthroughs for Japanese artists. “Daniel knows what worked and didn’t work [in his previous roles], so he can advise and lead,” Osada adds.

By setting up collaborations between Japanese and U.S. music creators, Osada says the goal is to “localize” Japanese music for U.S. fans while maintaining its authenticity, with its West Hollywood studio house serving as a meeting point where cross-border collaboration can happen. As for J-pop’s potential, he feels the time is ripe. “I’ve been in L.A. for 10 years, but I didn’t think of this momentum five years ago,” he says. “It was very quiet, but now I realize the momentum of [Japanese music] is just going up [in the U.S.].”

Osada attributes this in part to the recent U.S. breakthroughs of K-pop and Afrobeats music, which he believes is making it possible for other global sounds to gain traction here. He also cites the Japanese government’s recent “significant” investment in exporting Japanese IP, including music. Last year, the Japan Culture and Entertainment Industry Promotion Association, along with the country’s Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry, launched Music Awards Japan — effectively the country’s version of the Grammys — in an effort to lift the profile of Japanese music globally.

In a statement, Hong echoed Osada’s sentiments about Japanese music’s potential in the U.S., stating, “J-pop really feels ready for its moment out here — the production is incredible, the home fanbase is ride-or-die, and the sound is super fresh. Having seen K-pop grow from day one gives us a solid head start, but J-pop definitely moves at its own pace. What’s most exciting is working with artists who are massive stars back home, yet still have that genuine hunger to try new things and build something from scratch.”

Part of that push includes Nebula17’s involvement in the A&R, marketing and strategy for BEYOND BORDERS, a project featuring Japanese virtual singer Hatsune Miku’s collaborations with Vocaloid producers that was launched in partnership with Japanese entertainment company Dwango with support from the Japanese government. In September, the initiative produced the EP EMERGE M♡DE MIKU, which paired U.S. and Japanese creators including Grimes, slayr, MOLIY, Frost Children, Odetari and George Clanton. It’s all part of a long-term effort to build on the momentum Osada has seen over the past decade while bridging the cultural differences between the Japanese and U.S. music industries.

“Japan is very process-oriented, in my opinion, and [the] U.S. [is] result-oriented,” he says, noting there are pluses and minuses to both approaches. “So what if we take [the] good parts of both cultures? That’s our strategy too.”

The Google Pixel Watch 5 Is $100 Off Right Now

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Amazon has knocked $100 off the latest Google Pixel Watch 5 (45mm, LTE), bringing it down to $429.99 from $529.99, its lowest price yet, according to price trackers. If you can do without cellular connectivity, the 45mm wifi model is also down by $100, now selling for a record-low $329.99 (down from $429.99). Plus, either one comes with 90 days of free Amazon Music. PCMag calls the Pixel Watch 5 the best smartwatch for Android users, largely because Gemini is built in so deeply—the assistant works proactively, so it can surface your flight details on the way to the airport or suggest a contact's number while you text about dinner plans, and a new AI-powered Health Coach reviews your workouts with tailored feedback, though some of its fitness advice sits behind a paid subscription.

Helping all of those AI features along is a faster Qualcomm processor that Google says improves the overall performance of this smartwatch by 20%, while RAM increases from 2GB to 3GB and storage doubles to 64GB compared with the Pixel Watch 4. As for its health hardware, Google left it largely untouched, so you still get heart rate and blood oxygen monitoring, ECG readings, skin temperature, GPS, and sleep tracking, with reportedly accurate findings. The catch is that this remains an Android-only watch, requiring Android 12 or newer, and some of the better AI features work best with a newer Pixel phone.

Physically, the Watch 5 doesn’t stray far from its predecessor, keeping the familiar domed display under 3D Corning Gorilla Glass while adding new watch faces that curve around its edges—with the screen reaching up to 3,000 nits and adjusting to ambient light, so it should remain easy to read during a sunny afternoon run or in a dim room. As for its durability, its IP68 and 5ATM ratings mean you can wear it through a workout, at the beach, or in the pool without worrying about dust or water. And charging shouldn’t necessarily be a nightly routine, either, with the watch lasting up to 56.5 hours in testing. Heavier use will cut into that figure, but you should still have a good chance of making it through two full days with enough power left to track your sleep overnight.

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker.

Community: "I Think Of Her Every Time I Play It" - Your Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Memories

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

With the Switch 2 remake of the N64's The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time fast approaching, we recently asked Nintendo Life readers to share their memories of the 1998 original.

Whether you were a Zelda veteran taking your first steps into a 3D Hyrule or this was your very first encounter with the series, this particular entry holds an extra special place in many a player's heart, having a profound effect and eliciting emotions that have stuck with us ever since.

So, for those looking to reminisce as we await the new version, as well as Ocarina newbies and younger gamers who, perhaps, are wondering what all the fuss is about, here's a selection of the messages you sent...

Thank you to everyone who took the time to share these moments. Some entries have been edited for brevity and clarity.

Ocarina of Time is what introduced me to one of my best and longest-time friends. I picked up a used copy in the 6th grade, and got stuck navigating the Lost Woods. I was talking to someone about it on the bus ride home from school, and my (now) friend overheard me, and immediately jumped over and gave me advice. After I thanked him the following day, he let me borrow his strategy guide in case I got stuck again. That experience and the long-term friendship it resulted in cements this as not just my favorite Zelda game, but one of my favorite games of all time. Broosh

I was still at college when Ocarina of Time came out in the UK, and I clearly remember playing it religiously on my N64 each afternoon or evening after lessons, over about a three week period. Ocarina totally dominated my life for that brief period, and I genuinely felt like I'd lost a friend when I finally finished the main quest - all these years later I can still recall the hollow feeling of not knowing what would fill the void it left! Still one of the most memorable adventures I've ever had. Tom Charnock

Fighting Dark Link for the first time triggered something in me, as a kid it opened my mind to question who Dark Link was. Was Link capable of being evil? Another core memory I have is the first time I ever beat Ganon I quickly rushed to the phone and dialed my best friend and excitedly told him what I just accomplished. We used to switch turns when it came to playing Single Player games. And who can forget the TITLE SCREEN. Arkay

Not sure why this one sticks out, but in the Shadow Temple, fighting two Stalfos on the ghost ship made me feel like a badass. I was 10 at the time, admittedly. Mattock1987

I do remember one funny occasion where, I turned the N64 to mess around a bit later at night, when I was not supposed to, and went to the title screen, I pressed some buttons and somehow deleted my father's save. I didn't even realize I did so, I just thought it was cool there was keyboard where I could insert my name. Once he went to play the next time, he grounded me for 1 month. It was the first time I was grounded as well. Brandon Silva

My older neighbor had OoT when it came out, and I would go to his house to watch him play. We'd sometimes take turns, until we got to the well dungeon! I remember thinking it was such a scary, grown-up game. I got my own copy when our local Blockbuster rental store closed, and was barely brave enough for the Shadow Temple then. I love the variety of ambiences in Hyrule, and I hope the remake can convey how scary the original could be! Jake

Countdown Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Dungeon Crawl #7 - Grave Expectations

In a collective save played by every kid at my after school babysitter, my only contributions were earning the cow in the horse race with Ingo and the golden scale in the fishing minigame, which taught me the value of learning to take on the weird tasks in any group project. Zach

Playing it when it first came out in 1998, the beginning area felt impressive in the early days of games in three dimensions. But when I left the Kokiri Forest and stepped out on to Hyrule Field, I set the controller down and just sat there in awe. My kid-brain couldn't comprehend how vast a video game could be, and it was beautiful to stare at as the sun slowly set. To this day, each time I replay OoT, I always take a moment to relive that sight. I'm looking forward to being stunned again. Adam Hollerud

My brother and I used to play Ocarina of Time together in the early 2000s. We browsed the early fansites, especially those that documented known glitches. Replicating these glitches ourselves was endlessly entertaining. This was the era when rumors ran rampant and the idea of finding the Triforce didn't seem so far-fetched. Does anyone remember "turning all the gossip stones blue?" The doctored photo of the "Temple of Light?" This game reminds me of my brother and of the unique online culture that surrounded it. Mike DeCou

When both my grandparents were still alive, I used to spend a lot of time at their house, an apartment in a 17th-century building. Going there was an adventure in itself: a big entrance door, three flights of old stone stairs, the smell of soot, and finally, the front door to their big apartment. It was December, and from the very first day, everything felt like a prelude to the long-awaited Christmas Eve. My sister, my cousins and I couldn’t wait to spend some time together. My cousin had a Nintendo 64. I still loved my SNES, but I also had a PlayStation and was really into PC gaming. And 1998 was the year of Ocarina of Time. He owned the game, and I distinctly remember how jealous I was, how I felt almost like a traitor for not owning a Nintendo 64, after being a Nintendo kid for so long. He pushed the cartridge into the console, switched it on, and my world stopped. All of a sudden, I forgot about Doom, Quake, Duke Nukem 3D and whatever else I was playing at the time. I was home. It was Zelda. Matteo Guarino

I was in first grade when we got Ocarina. My mom played it mostly, but I loved watching. One time in school, I was trying to draw the item subscreen (yeah...) and couldn't remember what was in a certain spot. My classmate who was watching and had no idea what it even was suggested "I think you should put a giraffe there". My other classmate slapped me in the face when I told him about the different temples, because "Only God could have those" (church and temple being the same word in Hungarian). Krisi

I played Ocarina of Time so many times that I gave myself a challenge: beat the game with only the 3 hearts you start with, and I did it! (I’ve pictures to prove it) Joseph R Casper

As a 53 year-old, I didn’t get into playing non-sports games until the mid 2000s. Up until then, I was only using my PS2 to play FIFA and Gran Turismo. But my brother who’s four years my junior? He had every Nintendo console. Including the N64. In January and February of 1999, we lost our mum and dad very suddenly. They were only 47 and 51 respectively and it hit us very hard. At the time, I was already married and had our first daughter, but I went to my mum’s place to see my brother every day and I vividly remember one day just sitting there watching him playing Ocarina of Time. It looked utterly incredible and that memory, no matter the brevity, was fundamental in me migrating to Nintendo and playing all the big, proper games. The Zelda’s, the Mario’s, the Metroid’s. That probably isn’t what you guys at NL are looking for here, but for me, just watching Ocarina was huge. It absolutely helped the both of us escape the pain of bereavement, even if only for an hour or two. And, to us, that was priceless. The_Pixel_King

In 7th grade, The Legend of Zelda OOT was my entire personality! We couldn’t afford it when it first released, but in 2001, my parents surprised us with it. I was obsessed! I carried that beautiful N64 instruction manual around like it was my Bible, to school and everywhere I went, studying every page. Some of my favorite memories were sitting with my mom, trying to solve puzzles together, getting stuck, and celebrating when we figured them out. Looking back, it wasn’t just a video game. It was pure magic, a piece of my childhood I’ll treasure forever. Giodannie Isabel de leon

I cried when Link left the Kokiri Forest with Saria standing alone on the bridge in the Lost Woods and again with Mido expressing remorse over how he treated Link before allowing me passage into the Forest Temple. I sobbed when Navi left Link in the Temple of Time. I also [remember] feeling tears of joy after finishing the game and seeing all the lives Link had interacted with throughout my entire journey. I also sobbed when I beat Majora’s Mask and Wind Waker and seeing all the people I had helped and bettered their lives in the process! VirtualVisage87

Sitting before the big TV in the playroom, my friend Maysen and I gazing up at a wide open field, enchanting music beckoning us away to the time of castles and kingdoms and brave knights. Navigating a tricky gated area in the field, feeling so smart. Exploring a quaint village (with a spooky-at-night graveyard). Sneaking about a castle courtyard. Finding a fun yellow mask, at last being allowed to pass the gatekeeper. Then it gets hard! Now, I sometimes revisit this land, experiencing again the faraway, magical places. Imagining my dear friend sitting beside me. Brian

This game is one of my favorite early memories with my mom. I started playing before I could read very well at all. My mom would sit with me and read the dialogue, stories, and signs to me. She would help me solve the puzzles when I was frustrated or didn’t understand some of the lore specific words that were being used. It is one of my absolute favorite memories of growing up and I think of her every time I play it or see the Triforce tattoo on my leg. Thank you Nintendo for all of these awesome memories. Joseph McKinnon

I was already a Zelda super-fan years previously, and when news started coming out for "Zelda 64" my friends and I followed that religiously. So when the OoT release was finally coming, we decided to have a launch party at my house. A couple of us saved and pre-ordered the game at the same EB Games to make sure we all got the gold carts. Immediately after school, Mom picked us up, brought us to get our copies and picked up pizza on the way home. When we all got to my place, starting up that game was awe inspiring. The music, the graphics, everything sucked into Hyrule and enthralled from the start. We all took turns playing while others chowed down on pizza and eventually my mom came out with a cake for us that she had decorated with the Triforce! We played till all hours of the night. It's a vivid and cherished gaming memory for me even 30 years later. David Hannah

I got the 3DS remake in 2019 (I was late) after hearing that it considered the greatest game of all time. I dabbled in the franchise before. I played BotW, and while I love that game, my nine year old brain was used to Mario platformers. OoT was a better introduction with its smaller world. Despite that, I sucked at the game. I loved what I played, it was so cinematic, but when I got stuck on a puzzle, I'd give up and wouldn't come back in months. Thus, I finally beat the game earlier this year. (Yay). The_Mario_Bro

My brother got a cool Christmas gift from the U.S.—an N64 and Ocarina of Time. Even though he wasn’t really into gaming, I totally got hooked! I kept getting stuck and even used the library’s internet to find tips. I skipped school a few times and picked up magazines to help out. I’m not sure exactly how long it took, but I’d guess about a month. Alejandro A

I got Ocarina when it was first released on N64. I had hit a roadblock at the Water Temple. After watching me fail repeatedly, my sister brought me and my game to her friend who showed me exactly what to do. The solution was so simple I was so mad at myself. Then I panicked as he kept playing, so I made up something “we forgot to do” and had to leave immediately. Needing help, the victory was bittersweet. Joshua Younis

I remember before the game came out, there was a hype website with a flash game that taught you the correct path through the lost woods. My friends were all amazed when I just ran through without any trouble. Justin V

I was always a gamer, and so I had made friends with the Best Buy associate. When Ocarina of Time was about to release I went to Best Buy every day in my car until he told me it was in stock, and standing by for release. The night before release I went, and he sold me a copy. Gold cartridge. The night before it came out. I got home at 9pm, my sophomore year of high school and I locked in. I stayed up all night. One of the first people to ever play the game. One of the best gaming memories of my life. Ed Tilden

We're around halfway through these golden recollections - turn the page for tales of young love, final battles, tears, realisations, Sharpies, and uncondonable launch-day activities that are surely past the statute of limitations...

Ethereum Layer 2 Aztec gets Tierra V6 upgrade to support faster private apps

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Tierra V6 fixes a critical V5 security issue and speeds up private transaction proofs, with new apps launching or preparing to launch on Aztec.

Aztec Foundation, the team behind the privacy-focused Ethereum Layer 2 network Aztec, has launched Tierra, its Alpha V6 network upgrade, to fix security issues and make private transaction proofs faster.

The upgrade resolves vulnerabilities found in earlier versions, including a critical issue in V5, the foundation said. It also adds a new AI-based system for security testing.

When asked why the critical issue wasn't fixed sooner, an Aztec spokesperson said the fix required the whole network to move to a new version.

"Because Aztec is a decentralized network, if a critical vulnerability is discovered, the entire network must upgrade to the new version to implement the fix," the spokesperson told The Block.

Aztec has no central operator that can make the change on its own, the spokesperson said, adding that there are plans to make future upgrades faster if vulnerabilities are discovered.

Aztec said its network has more than 3,000 validators, no centralized sequencer, and no privileged viewing keys.

Besides the security fixes, Tierra makes private transaction proofs twice as fast, taking one second to generate on a native device, according to the foundation.

Messages from Ethereum can now reach Aztec in as little as 12 seconds, compared with an average of two minutes before, the foundation said, noting that the change speeds up deposits and other transactions between the networks.

"Aztec Tierra delivers a stronger, more secure foundation for applications that no single company or institution can control," Zac Williamson, co-founder and CEO of the Aztec Foundation, said in a statement. "Privacy shouldn't be something you have to request or trust someone else to protect. It should be built into the network."

Developers on Aztec can choose which functions of their apps are private and which are public, the foundation said, adding that private functions run and generate proofs on the user's device, while public functions run on the network.

The upgrade comes shortly after Aztec Labs relaunched zk.money, a self-custodial wallet for private crypto payments, three years after discontinuing the original product.

"With Aztec Tierra and zk.money, users can deposit directly from any Ethereum wallet and make lightning-fast private transactions without complicated bridging workflows," Joe Andrews, co-founder and CEO of Aztec Labs, said in a statement.

Other apps launching or preparing to launch include Galactica for private AI inference, Nemi for private perpetuals trading, and the private browser wallet Nulo, according to the announcement.

Xochi supports private intents and trading, while Nyx lets users make private payments to any Ethereum wallet. Olla offers liquid staking for the Aztec token.

Rolling Chat, a private message board based on Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin's Aztec experiments, is also on the list.

Aztec also introduced a new brand and website alongside Tierra, describing its aim as becoming the platform for "unstoppable private apps."

Exclusive: Cogent Security’s VR-1.1 model outscores Claude Code and Codex on vulnerability repair

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Vulnerability management startup Cogent Security Inc. today released VR-1.1, a new version of its frontier reasoning model that writes fixes for newly disclosed software vulnerabilities.

On a public repair benchmark, the model outscored Anthropic PBC’s Claude Code and OpenAI Group PBC’s Codex.

The release goes after the part of vulnerability management that has not sped up. In July, research group Epoch Artificial Intelligence Inc. counted about 2,500 high- and critical-severity disclosures from major organizations, roughly five times the monthly record set before Anthropic announced Claude Mythos Preview in April.

Each repair still needs an engineer who understands the flaw well enough to change the code without breaking something else. Co-founder and Chief Executive Vineet Edupuganti called that stage “where security teams are stuck.”

To test the model on that repair work, Cogent turned to LiveCVEBench. Researchers at Harbin Institute of Technology and Kuaishou Technology built the benchmark from vulnerabilities disclosed in recent months and add new ones regularly. VR-1.1 produced a working repair for 57.9% of the benchmark’s 190 tasks. Claude Code and Codex tied at 45.3%.

Each task drops an agent into a real development environment with a vulnerability report and the affected codebase, in one of 14 programming languages. To pass, a patch has to close the vulnerability without breaking the software, which the benchmark checks with separate security and functionality tests.

Cogent blocked outbound network access during repairs and hid the grading tests until each agent was done. Inference budgets differed across the three systems, according to the full methodology Cogent Research has published.

Where the environment allows, VR-1.1 starts by writing its own proof of concept that triggers the reported flaw. From there the model follows the vulnerability through the affected code, including related paths that share the same weakness. The fix itself stays small, for example checking an index before use.

Every patch goes to a separate review that starts from a fresh context and hunts for a way around the fix. New bugs introduced by the change are part of that check. The model also leaves behind regression tests, which fail if a later code change reopens the flaw. When an attempt fails, the diagnosis and failure details carry into the next try.

“A patch might block the exact payload from a bug report, but leave the same vulnerability open via a different endpoint or break core functionality for users,” said Geng Sng, co-founder and chief technology officer of Cogent. “We designed VR-1.1 to check its own work for both problems before it finishes.”

Cogent introduced VR-1 in July to find and prove the attack paths an adversary could reach inside an enterprise. The new release extends the remediation capabilities that model introduced.

Founded in 2025, the company sells artificial intelligence agents that detect and fix vulnerabilities, with customers choosing whether a human signs off on each change. According to Cogent, Fortune 500 users have cut the exposure window for critical vulnerabilities by 97%.

Greylock Partners backed its $11 million launch round in July 2025, and Bain Capital Ventures led the $42 million Series A in February that took total funding to $53 million.

Hyundai's XRT Off-Road Division Is On The Right Path But A Step Behind

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There's nothing more in vogue right now than automakers soft-roader-ing their crossovers for weekend warrior types who enjoy a bit of camping or a dirt road every once in a while. Honda has TrailSport, Subaru has Wilderness, Toyota has TRD, GMC has AT4, and so on and so forth. Of course one of the largest automakers in the U.S. would want to get in on the action, so Hyundai spun up its own off-road sub-brand a few years back: XRT, which stands for Extreme Rugged Terrain.

What started out as more of a trim and appearance package on vehicles like the first-gen Palisade and fourth-gen Tucson and Santa Fe has evolved into offering styling changes with a bit of added capability when the going gets bumpy. With the introduction of Hyundai's XRT Pro trim on the Palisade and upcoming fifth-gen Tucson, that idea gets taken up another notch. However, after spending a day with both the 2026 Santa Fe XRT and 2026 Palisade XRT Pro off-roading in rural Missouri, I'm not totally sure Hyundai is all the way there yet. That's not to say the Korean automaker isn't on the right path and won't keep improving expeditiously, but as it stands right now, it may be just a step or two behind.

Full Disclosure: Hyundai flew me out to Bentonville, Arkansas, put me up in a very nice hotel and fed me, all so I could sample the 2026 Santa Fe XRT and 2026 Palisade XRT Pro.

It can be sort of tricky to evaluate the capability of a soft-roader like Hyundai's XRT fleet. Technically, both the Palisade XRT Pro and Santa Fe XRT were able to do everything that was thrown at them on this program, but they both lacked a bit of the sure-footedness I just experienced in Honda's Passport TrailSport, and that was on a far more technical and challenging course. That being said, what I did with the XRT siblings is probably 10 times as grueling as what the average Hyundai owner will do with their SUVs. There's also the issue of tires. While the Passport TrailSport is fitted with beefy General Grabber A/T Sport all-terrain rubber, the Santa Fe XRT makes do with less-aggressive Continental TerrainContact A/Ts, which leads to a good bit of wheel slippage.

Still, both XRTs do an admirable job of tackling trails, though rock crawlers they are not. The Palisade XRT Pro I drove was certainly the more capable of the two, thanks to its electronic limited-slip differential in the rear and a 360-degree camera that helped overcome its sheer size, but it never felt particularly at home, even if it did tackle every obstacle without much protest.

I say much protest because, while the car didn't get stuck at any point, the 4,575-pound crossover didn't exactly seem happy, and that especially goes for its Hyundai-built 8-speed transmission. While the naturally aspirated 3.5-liter V6 provided enough power (287 horsepower and 260 pound-feet of torque) to get up just about anything despite a lack of low-end oomph, I could tell that the 8-speed was none too pleased. I couldn't hear it on the road, but when switching to the Sand setting in its Trail drive mode, there was a noticeable whine coming from it on the throttle. A temperature readout in the gauge cluster also served as a constant reminder it was, uh, hot, and when I got out of the car, there was an unmistakable smell of hot transmission wafting through the air. From a functional standpoint, the XRT Pro didn't skip a beat, which is certainly a testament to the rest of the Palisade's drivetrain and its 8.4 inches of ground clearance; it bottomed out far less than I expected it to.

It's a similar story with the Santa Fe XRT. It may lack the e-LSD and all-out grunt of the Palisade, but it does make up for that somewhat with more low-end grunt courtesy of a torquey turbocharged 2.5-liter inline-4 that pumps out 277 hp and 311 lb-ft of torque. This drivetrain lent itself far better to the old-school way of getting yourself unstuck: give 'er some gas. Sure, there are 10 fewer horses than the Palisade's V6, but an extra 51 torques is massive.

Still, you can tell the Santa Fe is a far less luxury-oriented product than the Palisade, and I got bumped around a hell of a lot more than I did in the Palisade. Obviously, there's a price delta to consider, as the Santa Fe XRT starts at $43,940 including destination, and the Palisade XRT Pro has a far more costly $51,820 entrance price.

Currently, there are four XRT models on sale: the two I've been talking about, and then the Santa Cruz XRT and the Ioniq 5 XRT. I've driven the Ioniq and had a pretty good time with it, but came away feeling it was more of a styling exercise than anything else. Sure, it can handle some dirt, but that's just about it. While all these models do come with all-terrain tires, a bit more ground clearance, upped towing capacity, better approach and departure angles, and functional front recovery hooks, they're without a doubt behind industry frontrunners like Honda TrailSport and Subaru Wilderness when it comes to real capability.

I believe that's because XRT, as a brand, is so new. Hyundai's still trying to find its footing in an ever-more crowded world. It promises that its next product, based on the fifth-generation Tucson, will be its best yet, and far more than an appearance package. Even though the Tucson won't have the e-LSD of the Palisade XRT Pro, despite sharing the "Pro" moniker, it will have skid plates, all-terrain tires, 8.3 inches of ground clearance, some sick body cladding, and Hyundai's new off-road software called Smart Terrain Assist.

That program has the ability to analyze the terrain ahead of you, determining traction and slip conditions. From there, it provides visual guidance via green, orange or red "stuck zones," and shows the driver the best route to take. It works in concert with Automatic Terrain Detection (which has auto, snow, mud, sand and rock modes) with intelligent mode switching to remove any guesswork from off-roading. Clearly, Hyundai wants to make it as friendly to first-time off-roaders as possible.

Lest we forget, this same team is working on the body-on-frame Hyundai Boulder SUV, which will likely be powered by a hybrid or EREV drivetrain. It shares underpinnings with a future body-on-frame truck Hyundai is also working on, and it's targeted specifically at the American market.

While I'm not sure Hyundai's XRT division is quite the cream of the crop just yet when it comes to soft-roading — it might be a bit too focused on aesthetics, rather than actual performance — there's little doubt in my mind that it'll get to the front of the pack sooner rather than later. Just look at what this massive company has done with its N performance division, or what it has done with Genesis when it comes to luxury. When Hyundai puts its mind to something, it gets the job done, and I don't expect XRT to be any different.

How Travel Credit Cards Are Becoming Full-Trip Companions: Watch Video

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Travel credit cards are moving well beyond points and booking. At Skift Global Forum, Capital One Travel’s Sarah Moore said the opportunity lies in remaining useful throughout the trip by combining context, experiences, technology, and trust to build deeper relationships with travelers.

This sponsored content was created in collaboration with a Skift partner.

Travel credit cards have long competed on points, perks, and booking benefits. For Capital One Travel, the next phase is about extending the relationship further into the trip and finding more ways to stay useful after a customer books.

The company sees an opportunity to connect more parts of the travel journey, from planning and airport support to destination experiences. That approach draws on Capital One’s technology, booking data, travel products, and existing relationship with cardholders as their financial partner.

At Skift Global Forum in New York, Sarah Moore, head of Capital One Travel, discussed how the company is trying to become what she called a “travel companion” for its cardholders, with a role that extends before, during, and after the trip.

The Travel Card’s Next Act: From Earning Points to Erasing Friction

Capital One entered the travel business relatively recently, launching the Capital One Travel booking platform in 2021. Moore said arriving later gave the company an opportunity to look at what already existed and build around the expectations of a modern traveler.

That meant taking a technology-first approach, but also starting with the customer rather than simply recreating the traditional credit card travel model.

“We were able to really see what was out there in the marketplace and understand what the modern traveler was looking for,” Moore said.

That distinction matters as the boundaries between financial services, travel booking, loyalty, and trip planning continue to blur. A travel card may still sit in a traveler’s wallet, but the relationship increasingly extends into the rest of the trip.

For years, booking has been treated as the finish line for many travel companies. Moore sees it more as a starting point.

Capital One Travel wants to remain with customers after the booking transaction, whether that means helping a traveler navigate the airport by surfacing lounge information, providing flight updates, or helping them decide what to do once they reach their destination.

Being useful when something goes wrong can be particularly important, giving a company an opportunity to demonstrate the value of the relationship.

“Knowing when to step up is what builds trust,” Moore said.

For Capital One Travel, those moments can help turn a single booking into a longer-term customer relationship.

Moore also emphasized the importance of collaboration across the travel industry to better support travelers throughout their journeys. No single company can manage every aspect of the travel experience today, making partnerships across the broader ecosystem essential to delivering consistent support to customers throughout their journeys.

Delivering that kind of support also requires a different approach to personalization.

Travel brands have spent years collecting customer data, but Moore said the most useful form of personalization is contextual. The question is not simply who someone is, but what kind of trip they are taking right now.

A traveler taking a weekend trip with a spouse, for example, may want something entirely different from the same traveler on vacation with children.

Moore used her own family as an example. A trip with her husband might call for a relaxing stay at one of Capital One Travel’s Premier Collection hotels. Traveling with her children could mean booking a four-bedroom vacation rental, also available on Capital One Travel, with many homes offering the same luxury benefits found in the Premier and Lifestyle hotel collections. Even within the same family, different travelers may want completely different activities.

That same thinking extends to what travelers actually do once they arrive.

Moore said in a survey conducted by Capital One Travel, more than 70% of travelers are looking at some type of event or live experience, from sports and dining to other forms of cultural immersion. For some travelers, those experiences are increasingly shaping the trip itself rather than simply being added after the flight and hotel are booked.

“Travel is not just a flight and a hotel,” Moore said. “It’s what you’re actually doing when you land and what you’re engaging in when you’re there.”

Capital One has responded by building experiences into its broader cardholder proposition. Moore pointed to partnerships with Major League Baseball and touring artists, as well as more intimate experiences tied to food and culture that can be booked through activities on Capital One Travel.

One example is a culinary–driven trip led by chef José Andrés in his hometown of Asturias, Spain, exclusively for cardholders, giving them a more distinctive and immersive way to experience the destination.

Taken together, those efforts point to a broader shift in what a travel credit card can become. Moore said Capital One is focused on becoming a travel companion that supports cardholders from booking through servicing and the trip itself.

The company launched a dedicated Capital One Travel app within the past year, an example Moore cited of meeting travelers in the channels where they increasingly expect to manage their trips.

AI could push that evolution further. Travelers are increasingly finding inspiration, researching destinations, and making decisions through new interfaces. Moore said Capital One Travel will need to meet them in those places while maintaining the value and trust that come with its role as their financial partner.

That trust also creates an opportunity to use the information Capital One already has more effectively. Transaction data can provide signals about how some customers travel and what they may be looking for next, provided it is used to make the experience easier and more relevant.

For Moore, that is ultimately what “frictionless” travel means: understanding what a traveler needs and making it simple for them to get it.

This shift has implications beyond credit cards. Travel companies across the industry are trying to extend their relationship with customers beyond a single transaction. Doing that effectively requires more than adding another loyalty benefit or digital touchpoint.

It means understanding the context of each trip, being useful when travelers actually need help, and creating access that enhances the experience. It also requires accepting that the traveler, not the brand, is ultimately in control of the relationship.

For Capital One Travel, the opportunity is to make the card and the platform behind it more relevant throughout the journey. If that works, the booking becomes less of an endpoint and more of one moment in a much longer relationship.

Nov. 3, 1957: The flight of Laika

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Less than a month after the launch of Sputnik 1, the Soviets launched Sputnik 2. The mission was readied quickly in order to keep the propaganda momentum from Sputnik 1 and to coincide with the 40th anniversary of the Bolshevik Revolution.

Carrying mixed-breed dog Laika, Sputnik 2 was the first flight to carry a living animal to Earth orbit. A small stray from the Moscow streets, Laika had undergone training to help her become accustomed her to the close quarters and gravity changes. Electrodes attached to the dog sent data back to Earth, for future research into the biological effects of space travel. Unfortunately, Laika died a few hours after takeoff – always the expected outcome of the mission, as the life-support systems on Sputnik 2 were imperfect and there was no plan for recovering the satellite or its passenger after launch. Sputnik 2 burned up on reentry in 1958, after its batteries died the previous year. In 2008, a statue commemorating Laika was erected in Moscow.

Elisa Neckar is senior production editor of Astronomy magazine, and the editor of the Today in the History of Astronomy feature on Astronomy.com.

Apple iPad Mini (2026) Review: A Dream Device, but Who Is It For?

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Some thought it might be dead. Some thought it had no place in an iPhone Duo world. But the iPad Mini isn’t being put out to pasture—it’s getting a massive glow-up.

Apple’s smallest tablet has been refreshed—now in its eighth generation—and with it, nearly every aspect of the device has been upgraded. It now has an OLED screen, a faster chip, a thinner chassis, more memory, waterproofing, and much more. That all comes with a higher price, though, and raises the question of just who the iPad Mini is really for among the other choices in the iPad lineup.

I’ll come straight out with it: I love this little tablet. I’ve been using it for about a week, and I happened to take it on a short trip. The perfect time for it to really shine—and shine, it did. It was the perfect companion for long Lyft rides to the airport and wait times at my gate.

The star attraction of the new iPad Mini is its 8.5-inch display. That’s a slightly larger screen than the previous generation, yet the overall dimensions are barely bigger. But the change from IPS to OLED is what matters. It’s a Tandem OLED Super Retina XDR display, based on the same technology as the iPad Pro—though it doesn’t have the 120-Hz refresh rate. Nor is it quite as bright: It can reach 600 nits in SDR and 1,200 nits in HDR, which is 400 nits less than the iPad Pro. It’s more or less similar to the M6 MacBook Pro, though. Not the OLED MacBook Pro, but the Mini-LED model.

The iPad Mini OLED’s brightness is sufficient for most uses, even in bright lighting conditions. But you can take it to the next level by opting for a nano-texture display, which is totally worth the extra $100 it costs. (It’s the model I tested.) It’s not an antireflective coating or a matte layer; it’s actually etched into the glass. The result is a surface that almost entirely diffuses outside light without reducing the screen’s perceived brightness. Reading outside with the iPad Mini is particularly enjoyable, as the nano-texture display not only resists reflections but even gives it a paperlike crispness. This option is also available on the MacBook Pro and iPad Pro, and there’s really nothing else like it.

The iPad Mini one-ups its siblings in sharpness. Its smaller size gives it 19 percent higher pixel density than every other iPad. And yes, it really does look incredibly clear and sharp.

There’s more to it than the display. Part of the magic is how lightweight it is—barely over half a pound, and it doesn’t feel much heavier than my phone in my hand. One-handed use feels completely natural. That’s always been true of the iPad Mini, but this latest version emphasizes that even more.

Lastly, the color options have been pared down from four to just two. The lighter goldish Starlight is still available, but now there’s the introduction of the blackish, navy-blue Midnight. This is a finish carried over from the MacBook Air. The more playful Blue and Purple color options are both gone, as is Space Gray. More options are always better, but for a device that costs $700, I can see why these more professional colors make sense.

Apple has also completely redesigned the iPad Mini’s four-speaker sound system, and a couple of interesting changes are happening. As part of its process of making the device IP67 water-resistant, Apple removed the speaker grille from the sides to create a completely sealed enclosure. Instead, the tweeters sit behind two small slits on the edges of the display glass. And then bass drivers are built directly into the display. Apple calls it an Acoustic Display.

The stereo image sounds fullest when you hold the device in portrait, as long as you keep your fingers away from the display. The vibration caused by the location of the speakers isn’t terribly noticeable unless you have the volume on full blast. Overall, the audio sounds decent for a device this size, but it won’t compare well to larger devices like an iPad Air or MacBook Air. But for watching videos or the occasional movie, it suffices.

The water resistance is a first for any iPad, which is significant. You might still worry about bringing your $700 tablet poolside, but the IP67 rating means it can handle submersion for 30 minutes in about three feet of water. In comparison, iPhones have had an IP68 rating for the past eight years, meaning they can handle twice that depth for 30 minutes.

As always, it has to be said that iPads are not really designed for taking photos. That’s a bit less true with the iPad Mini, if only because it’s so easy to use one-handed. Still, I doubt you’ll use the 12-megapixel rear camera all that much, outside of the occasional document or QR code scan. It’s decent but hasn’t been changed from the previous generation. The front-facing camera, however, got a big upgrade. It’s still a 12-megapixel camera, but it has moved to the side bezel instead of the top. That location makes video calls feel more natural.

One thing the iPad Mini is still missing is Face ID. While it’s a standard iPhone feature, Apple appears to really be reserving it for only the iPad Pro. That’s a shame. This does have Touch ID built into the power button.

A number of other iPad features still apply here, though, that make the iPad Mini feel like something you could work on. It supports extended display mode, letting you connect it to an external monitor without forcing it to mirror the screen. iPadOS also now has windowing and proper cursor support. Without a keyboard, I can’t imagine you’ll use these features as much. On the other hand, the iPad Mini’s size makes it possible to type with your thumbs (like you would on a phone).

Clearly, the 2026 iPad Mini seems to have its sights set on more than just reading books and playing games. As if the OLED display and price tag weren’t already enough to give that away. Most of the demos Apple has been using to show off with the iPad Mini were more creator-focused than I expected. We’re talking running on-device AI models or creative applications.

There are three storage configurations available: 128 GB, 256 GB, and 512 GB. All come with 12 GB of unified memory, four more than the previous iPad Mini and matching the current iPad Air. You’d never know that from Apple’s own spec sheets, though, since it’s never mentioned. Regardless, it’s part of what makes the iPad Mini a more capable little tablet, whether in heavier multitasking or for on-device AI. It’s kind of absurd that this little tablet has 12 GB of memory while the MacBook Neo is stuck at 8.

The iPad Mini’s A20 Pro chip is also more powerful than the MacBook Neo’s. This is the same silicon as in the iPhone Duo, iPhone 18 Pro, and iPhone 18 Pro Max—with one caveat. For the first time, the storage options dictate which version of the A20 Pro chip you get. Only the 512-GB model gets the full six-core CPU and dual 16-core Neural Engines. I tested this six-core model, but the other configurations have nerfed performance, with one fewer CPU efficiency core and only a single Neural Engine.

Performance is really solid. The A20 Pro handles games quite well, and I tested several, including Oceanhorn 3, Fortnite, No Man’s Sky, and Assassin’s Creed Mirage. None of these games looked particularly nice here, with textures and effects turned down, but the iPad Mini played them smoothly at 60 frames per second. Visually, the most impressive was No Man’s Sky, which came to iOS and iPadOS just last week. It still feels amazing to play a game this dense on a mere iPad Mini. Of course, the iPad Pro with the M5 will be a substantial upgrade for gaming performance, but if you connect a mobile controller (we like the Backbone One), you’ll have a good time.

I also tried out some local AI in LM Studio. With 12 GB of onboard memory, you’re limited in what models you can use. LM Studio recommended the 4-billion parameter version of Qwen 3.5. This isn’t the kind of model that’s going to blow anyone away, but for simple tasks, it’s nice to run a text model completely locally. This is all GPU-dependent, so performance should be identical without the second Neural Engine that comes exclusively on the 512-GB model. Of course, Siri AI is now a significant part of this platform, and the iPad Mini can run all the advanced features, including the more expressive Siri voices and on-the-fly dictation.

The previous iPad Mini cost $499 when it came out (before the price increases), and that at least felt reasonable. At this new price of $699, it’s a little harder to justify. The iPad Mini has always been a luxe but niche device. It’s sized for reading books, watching movies, and playing games. It doesn’t have the same high-stakes use case as a phone or laptop, which makes that higher premium price harder to swallow.

If you want the most powerful iPad Mini as a professional tool, you’ll probably spend at least $999. Or $1,099 with the nano-texture display. That’s the same price as the MacBook Air! I get it: The iPad Mini is an entirely different device. But I’m wondering whether real professionals would at least prefer a larger display—not only for the extra screen real estate but also for keyboard support.

I will say: The iPad Mini does work remarkably well with the Apple Pencil. If I had to choose an iPad specifically for note-taking, it’d be the iPad Mini. On the other hand, illustrators will likely prefer a larger screen. Like previous versions of the iPad Mini, it’s only compatible with the Apple Pencil Pro and USB-C Apple Pencil. Even for those who use tablets in the workplace as a point-of-sale system or for specialized use cases, the higher price seems hard to justify.

Is it all just a plainly disguised way of justifying the higher prices Apple is paying for memory on the backend? The cynic in me says yes. It’s not unlike the direction Apple is heading with its OLED MacBook Pro. But putting the cynicism aside, there’s real quality-of-life value being added here, especially in the form of the OLED screen, waterproofing, and extra memory. For the right person, the iPad Mini will be the perfect product. It’s a dream device for casual use, whether it’s gaming, reading, or browsing. But I’m guessing that demographic has shrunk quite a bit with this increased price.

I’m a little perplexed why Apple isn’t at least offering an 8-GB RAM model that would bring the price down more. I know it goes against the focus on Siri AI, which needs at least 12 GB, but it would at least keep the core audience that would buy an iPad Mini in the first place. It would also help create some more separation between this and the iPad Air. The iPad Mini is $150 cheaper than the iPad Air despite having an OLED screen. The iPad Air, meanwhile, has a larger 11-inch screen and is big enough to use with the Magic Keyboard. You’ll have to weigh a number of trade-offs when choosing between these two.

And with the iPhone Duo’s arrival, the iPad Mini’s size feels even less unique. Sure, the Duo costs $2,000, but it solves the iPad Mini’s main problem: convenience. As your phone, the Duo fits in your pocket, goes with you everywhere, and is far more versatile as a pocket computer. The iPad Mini is less flexible and easier to forget and leave at home. And at $699, you may wonder whether you really need that fancy OLED screen and 12 GB of RAM.

Why Good Product Management Starts On The Factory Floor

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Harmjan Derksen, Chief Product Officer, Ultimo. AI-augmented enterprise asset management (EAM) solutions for industrial businesses.

​At 6:30 a.m. on a weekday, a team lead at a manufacturing plant is already at work. She is cross-referencing overnight breakdown reports, shift logs and open notifications, building a mental picture of the equipment before her technicians arrive. It takes her 45 minutes every single day.

No analyst report ever told me about that morning ritual. I learned it by standing next to her and observing it.

As a chief product officer, I used to believe that strategy comes from market research, competitive analysis and analyst briefings. Although those inputs matter, years of building software for manufacturing, logistics, healthcare, energy and utilities companies have taught me there’s a different layer to strategy. Enterprise software teams should spend more time observing end users than reading analyst reports. And industrial executives should demand the same from every vendor who wants their business.

For industrial CXOs, introducing software that lacks operational reality creates a financial risk. Research from Splunk and Oxford Economics puts the cost of unplanned downtime at $400 billion a year across the Global 2000, roughly $200 million per company. And ABB’s 2025 research puts the average cost of a single hour of downtime anywhere from $10,000 to $500,000 across industrial sectors.

The workforce math is just as unforgiving. Deloitte and The Manufacturing Institute project that as many as 1.9 million U.S. manufacturing jobs could go unfilled by 2033 “if workforce challenges aren’t addressed.” Our own maintenance trend research found that 63% of industrial organizations are struggling with an aging workforce. Every experienced technician who retires takes decades of institutional knowledge with them—knowledge that exists in no system unless someone deliberately captures it.

Software built from a distance won’t solve problems that only reveal themselves up close.

Analyst reports tell you what the market is buying. The factory floor will tell you why products demand attention after they’re bought. Three lessons stand out from the time my teams and I have spent shadowing maintenance crews.

1. Workarounds are the truth. When a technician keeps a paper notebook next to a tablet, that’s unfiltered product feedback. The workaround shows you exactly where the software stopped fitting the work. You’re not going to find this often in a user satisfaction survey because people rarely report the friction they’ve already routed around.

2. Conditions change everything. A tool that demos beautifully in a conference room behaves differently at the top of a ladder, in gloves, next to a machine running at 90 decibels, in a corner of the plant with weak connectivity. If your product team has never watched software being used in those conditions, they’re designing for a facility that doesn’t exist.

3. Reality doesn’t care about your roadmap. I once presented AI capabilities at an industry conference, and a maintenance leader gave me candid feedback over dinner: What he needed was for his engineers to use their phones for work orders. Where I was talking about AI, he was still trying to solve mobile adoption. That gap between what vendors present and what customers are up against is where roadmaps go wrong, and it shaped ours more than any market forecast that year.

That dinner conversation is what listening looks like, but it’s not the whole roadmap. Innovation doesn’t always emerge from explicit user requests. The balance is staying grounded in what customers face, while still leading them toward what they haven’t asked for yet.

Observation also changes what you build. When our team watched that morning cross-referencing ritual up close at a European door manufacturer, the insight was humbling. The highest-value application of AI wasn’t an exotic prediction engine—it was automating the tedious assembly of information the team lead already trusted and giving her back nearly an hour a day. That priority came from the floor, not from a quadrant.

If you lead an industrial or healthcare business, you can turn this principle into procurement and operating discipline. Here’s my advice:

1. Ask every software vendor, “When did your product team last spend a shift with people who do this work?” Get specifics: which site, what they observed and what changed in the product as a result. Getting a vague answer can be just as telling as a fully detailed account.

2. Make a site visit part of your evaluation. Before signing, put the vendor’s product people, not just sales engineers, on your floor for a day. How they behave there tells you whether you’ve found a partner or sat through a pitch. A vendor confident in their product will welcome it.

3. Put frontline workers on the selection team. The technicians and operators who will use the software daily should have a real vote, not a courtesy demo. Systems chosen without them are systems worked around.

4. Measure adoption, not deployment. Go live isn’t success. Track whether people use the tool 90 days in and treat workarounds as defect reports. Usage data will tell you more than any steering committee update.

5. Apply the same discipline internally. Your IT leaders and digital teams should walk the floor regularly, observing and asking questions. The gap between IT strategy and operational reality closes one observed shift at a time.

“Go and see” is a well-worn idea in lean manufacturing, and industrial leaders have applied it to their operations for decades. What’s missing is applying the same discipline to the software those operations depend on, and to the people who build it.

The next time a vendor shows you a roadmap, ask where it came from. If the honest answer is a stack of reports, be cautious. If the answer involves worn safety shoes, you may have found a partner who understands that in industrial business, the truth lives on the floor.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?
