Meeting AI Demand Starts With The Right Infrastructure

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

​Juan Font is President and CEO of CoreSite.

In the words of Benjamin Franklin: “Great haste makes great waste.” It’s an apt perspective for viewing the rush to AI deployment in the enterprise today, where organizational leaders race into AI deployment decisions without always having the infrastructure in place to support them.

For many enterprises, the default setting is to contract directly with major frontier labs such as Anthropic or OpenAI and big cloud providers such as Google or Microsoft. Effectively, they’re racing to allow employees to use tokens for almost anything—often without much oversight.

This rush is strikingly reminiscent of when the cloud was introduced in the 2010s, and employees opened public cloud accounts, running up very large tabs—again with very little oversight over the projects being undertaken.

Today, companies are rushing to consume tokens with relatively little thought given to how that consumption is supported. In many cases, the problem isn’t the AI deployment itself. It’s the infrastructure supporting it. And solving that problem is critical to developing and realizing a sound AI strategy.

At the innermost layer, AI requires compute. But compute in the form of GPU chips is extremely scarce. To deal with that scarcity, a whole new segment of companies called neoclouds that offer GPU as a service has emerged.

Enterprises that want to develop or train their own medium-sized models are renting or leasing compute infrastructure from these neoclouds. As that market matures, some companies are beginning to buy the hardware themselves, particularly in financial services, where firms require massive amounts of compute as well as real-time access to financial data and exchanges.

Companies developing their own models and doing their own inferencing require space, power and cooling for GPU clusters, along with connectivity to the models they use for other workflows. Less sophisticated companies are simply using the cloud, which can become increasingly expensive as usage grows. Running GPUs is extremely costly, and performance can degrade when long-haul fiber is used to connect to compute resources. You end up with chips waiting for data—resources you’re paying for but not fully utilizing.

That’s where colocation data centers come into the picture, particularly those close to population centers and network hubs. Using colocation data centers as inferencing hubs to manage the handoff between private and public infrastructure can improve performance by enabling direct connections.

Adjacency to where compute is performed is of vital importance, but so is being in an environment that allows you to future-proof your infrastructure as the mix of workloads changes. Getting there takes a partner who can help you step back and build the right infrastructure foundation before you scale your AI.

We’re also seeing a preponderance of multi-cloud and multi-model environments. Cloud providers are trying to become the entry point into the intelligence realm, and they want to connect you to everybody. But to operate in a multi-platform environment, you need the interconnection infrastructure to support it. The ability for huge models to connect is what ultimately drives their value.

From a cost perspective, it’s impossible to overstate how expensive it would be to transfer terabits per second of data across regions. And now, companies are releasing agents. Meta recently released Muse, an agentic AI personal assistant, and we see OpenAI and Anthropic moving in the same direction. Each of these applications requires dedicated compute, cybersecurity controls and tremendous amounts of bandwidth. That rise in agentic inferencing workloads will require inexpensive, high-speed connectivity to these models. Having compute in metro centers, adjacent to the clouds, will be vital from both a cost and speed perspective.

The enterprises that have the greatest need to place their digital infrastructure in a colocation data center—and gain closer proximity to inferencing—are not necessarily traditional brick-and-mortar hospitals and banks. They’re the AI models themselves: Anthropic, Meta, OpenAI, CoreWeave, Microsoft and Google. These companies are deploying edge networking infrastructure to support data transfer because the focus until now has been on the compute layer primarily devoted to training those models. Only now is the interconnection layer requiring edge compute and network being built. Demand for this interconnection capacity is already outpacing supply. Existing data center inventory is extremely limited, and we’re racing to build incremental capacity to meet growing enterprise demand.

All that horizontal stratification between the different participants of the edge AI compute ecosystem is happening right now, and it requires multiple megawatts of capacity. That edge infrastructure must be built to support the exponential growth in demand that is coming. In essence, this is the “plumbing” that is going to enable the massive growth in agentic AI workloads by enterprises and individuals alike.

Ultimately, because the network was not designed for the projected explosion in bandwidth, leveraging colocation data centers becomes a matter of survival. You must colocate—ideally adjacent to the edge compute infrastructure hosted by hyperscalers, neoclouds and frontier labs—and cross connect directly to the infrastructure. That’s the edge compute and interconnection on-ramps we’re building right now, and we’re just scratching the surface. The companies that take the time to build that foundation, before they scale, will be better positioned to turn today’s rush into an AI strategy that is sustainable tomorrow.

UK school estate hit by 3AM ransomware in suspected AI-assisted attack

A 3AM ransomware affiliate targeted a school estate in the UK and successfully encrypted around 37 of nearly 50 machines reached from an unmanaged host. [...]

3AM Affiliate Embraces AI-Like Loops in European School Attack

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Evidence suggests a model may have generated and revised commands during the ransom gang's breach, though researchers stopped short of claiming full automation.

A 3AM ransomware attack targeting a UK school estate may provide an early look at how cybercriminals could use AI as a force multiplier rather than a replacement for human operators.

Bitdefender today published research concerning a "hybrid AI ransomware attack" observed in the wild, attributing certain actions taken to human or agentic elements. On the surface, the attack is fairly straightforward. As a blog post explained, a 3AM ransomware affiliate attacked a school in the UK, "then used ordinary Windows tools to build domain trusts and reach other schools."

Attackers stole backup credentials, created rogue domain-admin accounts, established trusts between previously separate Active Directory domains, moved between schools, stole data, and deployed 3AM ransomware. In other words, the attack largely followed a familiar ransomware playbook, despite Bitdefender's claims that AI helped execute parts of the operation.

Related:'AdFection' Turns Ad Impressions Into Zero-Click Spyware Delivery

Where the attack got more interesting was how, as the security vendor argued, it didn't fit neatly into pre-existing buckets for attacks involving AI. It wasn't exactly an AI-generated attack where the malware was fully or largely created by a large language model (LLM); it wasn't exactly an AI-assisted attack where human engineers built the tools while using AI to speed up work; and it wasn't exactly an AI-operated attack where the LLM orchestrates actions and runs the show, as it were.

In this case, Bitdefender argued an AI system appeared to handle repetitive command generation and troubleshooting while a human operator made high level decisions.

Many elements of the attack were ordinary for ransomware, such as significant use of living-off-the-land (LOTL) techniques, where attackers abuse tools already present on the victim's machine rather than relying on malware — in this case PowerShell, Windows Management Instrumentation (WMI), reg, netsh, rundll32, and other domain and scheduling utilities preinstalled on Windows. The attacker also used "an Impacket-style remote-execution tool," as well as Mimikatz and a previously published Veeam script.

But despite the attack's ordinary nature, Bitdefender assessed that several things suggest the use of an agentic loop, the control structure that makes an AI agent work. Certain actions appeared to be taken by a program rather than a human operator.

"The commands carry the usual tells of a model: a call to the backup software's web interface (Veeam, a widely used enterprise backup product) on a port and with a login method that do not match the vendor’s documentation, a mistake typical of writing from memory rather than from the manual (Veeam REST API authorization), along with confirmation files, self-labeled output and numbered revisions of the same script," the blog post read. "The retries behave like a loop too."

Related:Biomemory Launches DNA Shield, First Commercial Product Built on Its Molecular IT Platform

In another example, researchers assessed that the time taken between PowerShell command attempts was shorter than a human would take if issuing them by hand, but longer than if an LLM was orchestrating by itself. The longer time taken between individual tasks but the shorter time between retries "is what it looks like if a person is choosing the next step and a model is handling the retries."

Bitdefender caveated its findings by noting that it was unable to access the machine the attacker worked from, so it couldn't document anything tangible involving the AI model itself. The research acts as an argument that this attack behaves like a human operating alongside an agent, which is a working model defenders may wish to plan around.

With that in mind, Martin Zugec, technical solutions director at Bitdefender, tells Dark Reading that "a hybrid model is more reasonable for financially motivated cybercriminals, technically and economically, than a fully autonomous attack."

Related:Foul Language: WordlistLoader Disguises Malware as Ordinary Text

But even as a one off, the situation is notable because the primary victim was an organization in a critical sector, and Bitdefender's anecdote suggests the affiliate may have used an AI agent to scale the attack by handling repetitive troubleshooting and command generation, although the report stops short of showing AI-enabled capabilities the attacker otherwise lacked.

Moreover, other similar institutions could fall in the crosshairs. "From a domain controller it enumerated trust relationships between domains and searched SYSVOL (the shared folder where Active Directory stores logon scripts, readable by any domain user) for the names and subnets of other schools," according to the research blog post.

The good news is that because much of this attack followed the typical ransomware playbook, the defender recommendations that stop regular ransomware would also stop the attack described in this research.

Zugec adds that agentic loops are noisy from an indicator standpoint, as many of their actions are repetitive and brute force in nature. "An agentic loop doesn’t make an attack quiet, it makes it repetitive: the same command re-run, small tweaks, numbered scripts and probe files left on disk. Even the quickest retries took 40 seconds or more. 'Machine speed' is mostly a marketing phrase about very expensive incidents; in real attacks, especially with local models, we expect this slower and noisier pattern. Speed isn’t the giveaway, repetition is."

The vendor recommends focusing on prevention: Keep admin credentials of different domains separate, treat backups as a possible target, restrict remote execution and administrative shares to known management hosts, block interpreters and shells on machines that never use them, and put endpoint security tooling on every machine.

"Prefer controls that work regardless of volume and speed," the blog post read, "because they stop an action the first time and the hundredth."

Alex is an award-winning writer, journalist, and podcast host based in Boston. After cutting his teeth writing for independent gaming publications as a teenager, he graduated from Emerson College in 2016 with a Bachelor of Science in journalism. He has previously been published on VentureFizz, Search Security, Nintendo World Report, and elsewhere.

At Dark Reading, he covers a variety of cybersecurity topics, including the cybercrime ecosystem, open source security, and the intersection between AI and threat actors. In his spare time, Alex hosts the weekly Nintendo podcast, "Talk Nintendo Podcast," and works on personal writing projects, including two previously self-published science fiction novels.

He has received numerous awards, including TechTarget's Writer of the Year in 2022 as well as more than 10 Azbee awards for his reporting between 2022 and today.

Europe on alert after supermarket freezers hit by cyber-attacks in 10 countries

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Investigations have begun in several European countries in response to the cyber-attacks

A spate of cyber-attacks that hit supermarket chains across at least 10 European countries on Monday has prompted European Union officials to warn of a deliberate attempt to disrupt the food supply chain.

Supermarkets have reported problems in Belgium, Estonia, Finland, Ireland, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, Portugal, Germany and Sweden, and the significant increase in freezer temperatures has forced retailers to discard food.

Temperatures in chains across Europe reportedly rose as high as 50C (122F).

No-one has yet admitted carrying out the cyber-attacks.

Supermarket chains said the temperatures of refrigerators and freezers in some of their shops soared as high as 50C

The European Union has raised concerns about the attacks, which come three weeks after a cyber-attack disrupted as many as 700 French grocery stores, causing food losses estimated at tens of millions of euros.

The security of refrigeration systems as well as other devices that can be monitored remotely has heightened concerns about government-backed hybrid attacks.

The EU's food and agriculture commissioner, Christophe Hansen, said the bloc was taking the incidents very seriously and warned of further disruption in the future.

Commission spokesman Thomas Regnier was careful not to attribute blame, but said: "These incidents of course show once again how cyber-attacks can disrupt essential services and affect citizens' daily lives."

"These are not isolated incidents and of course we have to be collectively resilient and prepared for such cyber incidents."

In Italy, Giorgia Meloni's government called a meeting with intelligence chiefs, but said the impact was "currently very limited" and most of the food distribution chain had up-to-date security systems able to fend off such attacks.

At least four Co-op supermarkets in Sicily and one business in Tuscany were affected, according to the national cybersecurity agency.

Meloni's office said those businesses affected were "mostly still equipped with obsolete security systems, with interfaces directly exposed to the internet and inadequate authentication mechanisms".

Belgian supermarkets were among those hardest-hit, with dozens of stores reporting refrigeration failures. Federal prosecutors have opened an investigation.

UK meat industry hit by cyber-attack which targeted European supermarket freezers

Mathieu Tilman, a manager at a Delhaize supermarket in Belgium, said he was shocked by this kind of attack: "I don't even understand how anyone could do this... How can we end up throwing away so much food? I find it truly distressing. In moments like these, you just feel like quitting your job."

In Ireland, 13 SuperValu stores were impacted by what the chain's owner, Musgrave Group, called a cyber-incident. "Replenishment of chilled and frozen ranges in the affected stores is well under way," a spokesman said on Monday.

A small number of corner shops in the Netherlands also suffered disruption. Last month, supermarket chain Albert Heijn had to take several services offline because of a separate cyber-attack.

There was disruption to refrigeration systems at the Rewe BÃ¼sken supermarket in western Germany after a cyber-attack on system provider Danfoss, the company told the BBC. Its systems have been back in operation since Monday afternoon.

In Portugal, the Mosqueteiros group said that incidents at seven IntermarchÃ© stores had been linked to a cyberattack, which "appears to have targeted an external service provider responsible for managing the refrigeration systems".

Swedish chain ICA also said "a limited number" of its stores had experienced refrigeration problems, without confirming a cyber-attack, and Sweden's national cyber-security agencies said they were monitoring the incident.

In Finland, irregular temperatures were detected in a few smaller grocery stores belonging to the Kesko group, which said it was investigating the issue.

In Estonia, at least one supermarket was attacked by hackers who raised the temperature in the refrigeration units to 50C, public broadcaster ERR reported.

Food losses are estimated in the tens of millions of euros

In the UK, the National Cyber Security Centre (NCSC) has warned British retailers to update the software on fridge temperature controls.

The agency said the country was likely to suffer an AI-powered cyber-attack on critical national infrastructure within two years.

Richard Horne, the head of the National Cyber Security Centre, told BBC Radio 4's Today programme there were a couple of instances in the UK where "vulnerable technology has been exploited" after the cyber-attacks.

"We don't know the full impact, but we do know there are a couple of instances where vulnerable technology has been exploited and we're working with those organisations."

Asked if there was any evidence that supermarkets in Britain or freezers of the same make or model had been targeted, Horne said: "It's not just supermarkets, it's through the whole food supply chain, and we've been working through the night alerting organisations where we can see they've got exposed technology that's vulnerable in this way."

Shop freezers in seven European countries hit by cyber-attack

If hackers can take control of a supermarket freezer, how vulnerable is other tech?

Modders bring Steam Frame build of SteamOS and all of its translation layer goodness to Mac

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Unofficial support, but still in the game.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Gaming on Mac has come a long way. Gone are the days of limited releases, though not absolutely everything is playable on the platform. Thankfully, community developers are only too keen to plug the gaps.

FX Steam Launcher on GitHub brings SteamOS to Apple Silicon Macs. Specifically, this independent, open-source project is based on the ARM64 Steam Frame version of the operating system. Therefore, you're getting Windows game compatibility on Mac thanks to Proton 11 and FEX, alongside the very swish SteamOS interface (via Videocardz).

Obviously, there are a few things to bear in mind if you want to go all in as a Mac gamer. For one thing, this is not a native installation, and is instead running SteamOS by way of a virtual machine on your Mac. For another, this is a GitHub download to begin with, but once you've got it installed, the launcher directly downloads the official SteamOS image from Valve and then even handles OS updates too. Neat!

As this is an unofficial effort, there are a few more caveats still. The FX Steam Launcher itself currently only supports Apple Silicon Macs running either macOS 15 (from September, 2024) or newer. However, to actually get the latest and greatest running, you may need something much more recent than that.

Bear with me here, but Proton and FEX are essentially only the first hurdle. DXVK is required to run DirectX 9, 10, and 11 games, while DirectX 12 releases rely on VKD3D-Proton. Then either MoltenVK or KosmicKrisp is the next hurdle graphics instructions will need to pass before finally handing off the baton to Apple’s Metal API (though there's some experimental support for DXMT, which allows DirectX 10 and 11 games to run without VKD3D-Proton).

Anyway, all of that is to say KosmicKrisp, a pretty pivotal translation layer in this whole process, requires at least macOS 26 or later to run. That's a fair amount of hoops to jump through, though; if you can get it running, the launcher does allow you to run SteamOS either windowed or fullscreen, while letting you still access macOS either way.

On account of not being a Mac power user, I've not had a chance to test it out myself, but YouTube creator ETA Prime tested the launcher with The Witcher 3, Stellar Blade, and Left 4 Dead 2 on their Apple M4 Max. At high settings, the creator saw performance to the tune of over 70 FPS for each of these games, with Left 4 Dead 2 breezing past 200.

Keep up to date with the most important stories and the best deals, as picked by the PC Gamer team.

That said, Stellar Blade was running at a mere 800p resolution, while the rest of their tests ran at a maximum of 1080p. Furthermore, not all the games they tested even ran as well as these cherry-picked examples (to say nothing of The Witcher 3's funky-looking trees).

Still, what's possible with this launcher remains impressive, though gaming on Mac even without it is far more viable than it once was. For Apple's big 50th birthday earlier this year, our Jacob explored the things Mac does better than PC. Our Ian was also mighty impressed with the Apple Mac Studio M4 Max—though he does also note, "Christ, what are you doing? Buy something else for gaming".

On that front, any would-be Mac gamer could do much worse than the MacBook Neo, which is now going for $699/£699. Not even Apple is safe from the memory supply crisis, but as the situation continues to drive up the cost of traditional gaming PC parts, who could blame a gamer on a budget still picking up the MacBook Neo instead? Especially with unofficial projects like FX Steam Launcher at their fingertips.

3. Best handheld gaming PC: Asus ROG Xbox Ally X

4. Best mini PC: Minisforum AtomMan G7 PT

Jess has been writing about games for over ten years, spending a significant chunk of that time working on print publications PLAY and Official PlayStation Magazine. When she’s not investigating all things hardware here, she's either constructing a passionate defence of a 7/10 game, daydreaming about her debut novel, or feeling wistful about the last time she chased some nerds around a field with an oversized foam sword.

What’s at stake for crypto as the US votes in the 2026 midterms?

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Crypto groups spent about $176 million ahead of the US midterms, with control of Congress and the future of crypto legislation at stake.

US voters head to the polls in midterm elections on Tuesday that could shift control of Congress and shape the future of cryptocurrency regulation.

Crypto industry groups have spent roughly $176 million across more than 100 races this election cycle, according to an Oct. 27 Reuters report.

Coinbase, Ripple and Andreessen Horowitz are among the main financiers of the Fairshake political network.

The results could reshape the prospects for the stalled CLARITY Act and determine which lawmakers lead committees overseeing the industry.

Republicans enter Tuesday’s midterms controlling both chambers of Congress, with 219 seats in the House of Representatives and 53 in the Senate, according to the Blockchain Association’s election dashboard.

Democrats are seeking to overturn those majorities, with prediction markets favoring the party to win the House and Senate, according to Kalshi data at the time of publishing.

Voters will decide all 435 House seats and 35 Senate races, determining which party controls Congress for the final two years of President Donald Trump’s second term.

Democratic control could bring more congressional scrutiny of Trump-linked crypto businesses and regulators’ decisions. Control of the Senate would also affect confirmation of future presidential nominees to regulatory posts.

Ohio’s Senate race between Republican Jon Husted and Democrat Sherrod Brown is a major test of crypto’s political influence. Industry groups have reportedly spent more than $50 million opposing Brown, a longtime crypto critic who could return to a senior Senate committee role.

In Nebraska, Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts faces independent Dan Osborn. Ricketts, a Senate Banking Committee member who has supported crypto-friendly legislation, has benefited from nearly $11 million in spending by Fairshake, according to the Washington Examiner.

Texas is another race to watch, with Republican Ken Paxton facing Democrat James Talarico in a closely contested Senate election. As Cointelegraph previously reported, the Winklevoss-backed Digital Freedom Fund has spent money supporting Paxton.

A shift in congressional control could delay the CLARITY Act, although the crypto market structure bill has already struggled under Republican leadership.

The legislation would divide oversight of crypto markets between the Securities and Exchange Commission and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, as well as establish rules for intermediaries. The Senate failed to advance it in a 49–50 procedural vote on Sept. 15, short of the 60 votes required.

Democratic Sen. Mark Warner cited insufficient safeguards against Trump profiting from his administration’s crypto policies. Republican sponsors said their final draft incorporated substantial ethics concessions sought by Democrats.

“A change in control of Congress would most likely reset the timetable for the CLARITY Act,” Christian Davies, group head of innovation and digital capital at Relm Insurance, told Cointelegraph.

Related: Crypto PAC spending before midterms possibly obscured after reported FEC hack

Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, agreed that the challenges extend beyond party control.

“The CLARITY Act was already struggling to get through the Senate with Republicans in control, so clearly the problem goes beyond which party holds the majority,” Kirkley said.

The industry has already secured a federal stablecoin framework through the GENIUS Act, which became law in July 2025.

Bitcoin’s (BTC) price history around US midterms has attracted attention on social media, but analysts caution that past declines offer little guidance on what could happen after Tuesday’s vote.

Bitcoin fell below earlier yearly lows after the 2014, 2018 and 2022 midterms. Source: ManGana

“I’d be careful reading much into Bitcoin’s record after past midterms. There haven’t been enough cycles to call it a pattern, and the market is built differently now: ETF flows, institutional portfolios and global liquidity move the price far more than they used to,” Michael Heinrich, CEO and co-founder of 0G Labs, told Cointelegraph.

Related: From Uptober to Hacktober: Are Bitcoin’s seasonal patterns becoming obsolete?

GSN’s Kirkley said institutional flows, interest rate expectations and market liquidity were more immediate factors affecting Bitcoin.

“Bitcoin doesn’t need a particular election result to succeed. It needs capital, confidence and a macro environment that gives investors a reason to take risk again,” Kirkley said.

Magazine: Who needs CLARITY anyway? ARB could see 70X increase: Hodler’s Digest

Remedy’s Big Bet On Control Resonant Appears To Be Paying Off

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Following the September release of Control Resonant, business is boomin' for developer Remedy Entertainment. The studio reported that its third-quarter revenue surged 226% to €39.6 million / $44.3 million after the game's release, following the financial review period on October 15, a feat CEO Jean-Charles Gaudechon called "a defining milestone" for Remedy.

In terms of operating profit, Remedy said it made €0.6 million / $670,794, and while that may not sound like much, it's still better than the €16.4 million / $18.3 million loss the studio posted in the same quarter last year. Control Resonant has sold over 1 million units, making it Remedy's fastest-selling title to date, with most sales coming from the US. The game has also been popular in the US and the UK, and Remedy says that half of the players were new to the franchise.

"Reaching new players was a key goal for this game, and these early results indicate a growing audience for the franchise and for Remedy," Gaudechon said, adding that the developer is looking to get the game into the hands of more people. Control Resonant's predecessor, Control, also saw increased sales, and that financial runway will help Remedy as it continues working on other projects, like its Max Payne 1 & 2 remake. Gaudechon also noted that Control Resonant is currently on 2.4 million wishlists, which could provide a "substantial potential audience" for the future.

"Excellent player reviews, penetration in all our target markets and a substantial outstanding wishlist base give a solid foundation for the game’s longer-term sales potential, supported by both organic visibility as well as our targeted marketing activities," said Gaudechon. As for the other games in the Remedy catalog, Gaudechon said that most people are playing FBC: Firebreak through subscription services like Game Pass or PlayStation Plus, and in its second year, it is making less money. The Alan Wake franchise had a quieter quarter, but the Alan Wake 2 capitalized development costs were fully depreciated during Q3.

For more on Control Resonant, read up on how different it is from its predecessor, why you have to watch out for scary buses, and the cool little details lurking in the background.

Just a little AI resistance, as a treat

📅 Nov 03, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI EMFCamp NaBloPoMo · 3 comments · 750 words · Viewed ~388 times

At the recent EMFCamp, I bumped in to Professor Kate Devlin who was talking a little about the AI Resist List. I think one of the useful things we can do is loudly complain about shitty uses of AI. Here's an example.

As mentioned in a previous post, I'm contacting companies which send malformed calendar invites. One of the organisation I contacted was the British online supermarket Ocado. They don't have an easy way to contact their technical team, and no obvious paths to raise a bug report, so I filled in their usual customer service feedback form. I included a clear statement of the problem and a detailed technical analysis of why I thought it was a problem with their service.

After sending in my message, I received this back from "Lulu" at Ocado:

Thanks for getting in touch and sharing this feedback about our calendar files.

You're entirely right—having a technical glitch pop up when you're trying to organise your delivery is a real pain, so I've made sure to pass your feedback on to our technical and webshop team.

We really appreciate you taking the time to help us improve our service. I hope you have a wonderful rest of your day

I mean, that's AI right? It starts with the classic tell of a sycophantic agreement.

Next up is the em-dash. Beloved by chatbots but almost unheard of in British English style guides.

Finally (and this is hard to quantify) it just doesn't feel like a customer service response. They're usually poorly spelled, have dodgy grammar, and ask a bunch of irrelevant questions. The message gave me no confidence that anyone had looked at the problem and I doubted it had been passed on to a technically competent human.

I immediately got a customer satisfaction survey. Usually I give top marks to humans because, even though Net Promoter Score is bullshit, I don't want customer service agents to suffer the indignity of a bad review. But in this survey I vented my spleen - giving crappy 1 ⭐ scores on every metric and complaining vociferously about how rude it is to use an AI to deal with a genuine complaint.

I was both surprised and delighted to receive this back a few days after my invective was sent:

I saw your survey comment about getting a robotic, AI-generated response previously, and I wanted to reach out personally to apologise. There is nothing more frustrating than pointing out a legitimate bug only to feel like you're talking to a brick wall. I promise you there is a real person typing this out!

Second, I wanted to send a massive thank you for the incredibly detailed diagnostic. It is rare to get feedback that tells us exactly what is broken and how to fix it.

I can assure you that this has been passed to our team to look into. Your feedback is going to make things much smoother for a lot of people, and we really appreciate you taking the extra time to help us get it right.

Thank you again, I do hope that this issue is resolved quickly. If there is anything else I can help with in the meantime, please don't hesitate to get in touch.

Perhaps I'm being touchingly naïve, but that feels a lot more human, right? The grammar isn't perfect, the sentences are a bit more rambly, and it appears to have actually engaged with the contents of my message.

I get that having a million irate customer service messages is a hassle. Using AI to triage them probably makes business sense, summarising the verbose ones could be useful for customer service agents, and totting up general themes is almost certainly a sensible way to gather metrics without overloading the workforce.

But using an AI to reply to a human is just disrespectful. It says the organisation values their customer's time at less than their own.

It's OK to call out shitty AI responses and insist that a human engages with you.

Enjoyed this post? Show your support and pay the author.

Sadly they seemed to do things the other way around, the initial responses were brief and to the point, but when I pressed them I started getting rambly generated messages devoid of actual information.

Versant earnings beat expectations as digital platforms drive growth

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Versant Media on Tuesday reported quarterly earnings and revenue that beat expectations and reaffirmed its full-year 2026 guidance, citing strength across its portfolio and growth in its platforms revenue.

The media company reported $245 million in revenue from digital platforms including Fandango, marking a nearly 10% year-over-year increase. Versant said Fandango saw "significant growth" in ticketing revenue in the third quarter, notching its highest level since the pandemic.

Advertising revenue, however, slipped to $373 million during the period, down 5.3% from last year, primarily due to the impact of last year's Ryder Cup, according to the company.

The Versant portfolio includes CNBC, MS NOW, Golf Channel and other cable and digital properties.

Here's how the company performed in its third quarter, compared with what Wall Street was expecting, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Versant completed four major distribution renewals this year following its spinoff from Comcast's NBCUniversal.

During the third quarter, Versant said, its networks reached more than 120 million viewers monthly and its aggregate viewership grew by double digits year over year. The company also launched MS NOW's direct-to-consumer product and Fandango's on-demand product as new growth drivers. CNBC is expected to launch a direct-to-consumer product next year, Versant said.

For the period ended Sept. 30, Versant recorded net income of $47 million, or 34 cents per share, compared with $80 million, or 56 cents per share, the year prior.

Adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization was $353 million, beating Wall Street estimates of $305 million, according to StreetAccount.

Revenue fell slightly to $1.59 billion from $1.66 billion in the year-ago period. The company said its revenue figure included the acquisition of golf simulation company Full Swing and the sale of sports management software company SportsEngine.

The company reaffirmed its full-year revenue outlook of between $6.2 billion and $6.45 billion and its adjusted EBITDA outlook of $1.9 billion to $2.05 billion. It adjusted part of its full-year guidance, saying it now expects free cash flow to exceed the prior range of between $1 billion and $1.2 billion.

"We're building on strong relationships we already have with consumers, advertisers and distributors," Lazarus said on the call. "We're finding new ways to reach and serve those audiences. We have the brands, the financial strength and the people to execute this transition successfully."

Versant began trading as a standalone company on the Nasdaq in January after separating from Comcast. Though it began largely as a portfolio of cable networks, Versant executives have said they intend to diversify the company's revenue mix to eventually draw 50% from digital, platform, subscription, ad-supported and transactional businesses.

Live sports continues to be a major driver for the company. Versant said it saw "robust audience engagement" in the third quarter and that WNBA viewership on Versant's USA Sports networks was 25% higher than the prior year's cable average.

Versant also announced an additional $100 million accelerated stock repurchase program beginning Wednesday. It expects that transaction to be completed during the fourth quarter.

Disclosure: Versant Media is the parent company of CNBC.
